Beyond Endoscopy via Poisson summation for GL ⁡ ( 2 , K ) \GL(2,K)
Melissa Emory, Malors Espinosa Lara, Debanjana Kundu, Tian An Wong

Abstract
. In the early 2000’s, R. Langlands proposed a strategy called Beyond Endoscopy to attack the principle of functoriality, which is one of the central questions of present day mathematics. A first step was achieved by A. Altuğ who worked with the group GL ⁡ ( 2 , ℚ ) \GL(2,{\mathbb{Q}}) in a series of three papers. We generalize the first part of this result to a class of totally real number fields. In particular, we cancel the contribution of the trivial and special representations to the trace formula using an additive Poisson summation on the regular elliptic terms.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 11F72

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Philosophy

Langlands’ principle of functoriality is one of the central open problems in modern number theory.
Its study is a driving force for the development of number theory, harmonic analysis, representation theory, and the deep connections between them.
More precisely, functoriality conjecturally maps the world of automorphic
representations across different groups: given a morphism of
L
-groups associated to reductive groups
H
and
G
, it predicts a corresponding transfer of automorphic representations from
H
to
G
.
Underpinning this mechanism is a deep analytic compatibility; the transfer must respect the spectral decomposition of
L^{2}
-spaces on adelic quotients, carrying automorphic representations over while preserving the core analytic properties of their
L
-functions and
\varepsilon
-factors.
The trace formula has been developed as a major tool to study the structure of automorphic representations.
Some pioneering work in this direction include
[
24
,
30
,
28
]
.
In recent years, progress has been made to prove particular cases of functoriality such as
[
4
,
1
,
2
,
15
,
20
,
21
,
27
,
39
]
,
yet a general proof remains out of reach.
For a comprehensive account of the history of the problem and the current state of progress, we refer the reader to
[
6
,
5
]
.

1.2.
A strategy to prove the principle of functoriality

The trace formula is an equality between two distributions, usually called the
geometric side
and the
spectral side
.
For
G=\GL(n)
, over a number field
K
, the main part on the geometric side is

\sum_{[\gamma]}\vol(\gamma){\mathcal{O}}(\gamma,f),

which is called the
regular elliptic part
of the trace formula; here
[\gamma]
runs over regular elliptic conjugacy classes in
G
,
\vol(\gamma)
refers to a certain adèlic volume and
{\mathcal{O}}(\gamma,f)
is the orbital integral associated to the test function
f
.
On the other hand, the main term on the
spectral side
is

\sum_{\pi}m(\pi)\Tr(\pi(f))

which is the contribution of the discrete spectrum to the trace formula.
Here,
f
is a test function,
\pi
runs over the automorphic representations occurring in the discrete spectrum, the trace
\Tr(\pi(f))
is the character of the representation
\pi
of
G({\mathbb{A}}_{K})
, and
m(\pi)
is the multiplicity of
\pi
in the
L^{2}
-spectrum of
G
.

In
[
31
]
, R. Langlands proposed a strategy to prove the principle of functoriality via the construction of a
refined
trace formula.
Roughly, given a representation
r
of the
L
-group (denoted by
{}^{L}G
), the idea is to weight the
spectral side
of the trace formula with a coefficient
\mu_{r}(\pi)
, whence

\sum_{\pi}\mu_{r}(\pi)m(\pi)\Tr(\pi(f)).

The factor
\mu_{r}(\pi)
should be non-zero if and only if the associated automorphic
L
-function
L(z,\pi,r)
has a pole at
z=1
. For example, Langlands considered, as we shall do also,
\mu_{r}(\pi)
to simply be the order of
L(s,\pi,r)
at
z=1
.

When
K={\mathbb{Q}}
and
r=\text{Sym}^{k}
, Langlands showed there is a family of adèlic test functions
f_{p}
indexed by the primes
p
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi}\mu_{r}(\pi)m(\pi)\Tr(\pi(f))

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\#\{p\mid p\leq n\}}\sum_{p\leq n}\log(p)\sum_{\pi}m(\pi)\Tr(r(a_{p}(\pi)))\Tr(\pi(f))

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\#\{p\mid p\leq n\}}\sum_{p\leq n}\log(p)\sum_{\pi}m(\pi)\Tr(\pi(f_{p})),

where
a_{p}(\pi)
is the Satake parameter of
\pi
at
p
.
Naïvely, assuming the equality of the main terms on both sides of the trace formula, we see that

\sum_{\pi}\mu_{r}(\pi)m(\pi)\Tr(\pi(f))=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\#\{p\mid p\leq n\}}\sum_{p\leq n}\log(p)\sum_{[\gamma]}\vol(\gamma){\mathcal{O}}(\gamma,f).

For the limit to exist, the contribution of the non-tempered spectrum of each of these trace formulas needs to be removed.
In our case of interest, i.e. when
G=\GL(2)
note that the only non-tempered representation in the discrete spectrum is the trivial one and its twists by grossencharacters.
For the moment, let us assume only the trivial representation appears in the residual spectrum.
Therefore,

(1)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\#\{p\mid p\leq n\}}\sum_{p\leq n}\log(p)\sum_{\pi\neq\mathbf{1}}m(\pi)\Tr(\pi(f_{p}))\\
=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\#\{p\mid p\leq n\}}\sum_{p\leq n}\log(p)\left(\sum_{[\gamma]}\vol(\gamma){\mathcal{O}}(\gamma,f)-\Tr(\mathbf{1}(f_{p}))\right).

What remains on the spectral side should now converge.
Thus, the geometric side must converge as well.
In
[
21
]
, it was suggested that an additive Poisson summation-type formula could be applied to the regular elliptic contribution on the geometric side to isolate the trace of the trivial representation and cancel it.

In a series of papers by A. Altuğ
[
4
,
1
,
2
]
this cancellation is performed for
G=\GL(2,{\mathbb{Q}})
with
f
such that for all primes
q\neq p
, its
q
-adic local factor is the indicator of the maximal compact open subgroup of
\GL(2,\mathbb{Q}_{q})
and when the representation
r
is the
k
-th symmetric power of the standard representation.
For the case
k=1
, and under certain assumptions on
f
, the above limit equality is verified.
In particular, in
[
4
]
the trace of the trivial representation is isolated from the discrete spectrum—which is the only representation that has to be removed in this case.
Altuğ shows that

\left(\sum_{[\gamma]}\vol(\gamma){\mathcal{O}}(\gamma,f)-\Tr(\mathbf{1}(f_{p}))\right)

can be manipulated so that the limit on the right hand side of (
1
) converges.
This has been regarded as a proof of concept of the strategy proposed in
[
21
]
, whose hypothetical Poisson summation formula, while highly suggestive, ‘cannot literally be true;’ see
[
8
, Footnote 6]
.
Until now, no other example of the removal of the non-tempered spectrum via Poisson summation has been established since then.
We refer the reader to Section
1.4
for work that was done since this paper was first made public.

1.3.
Goal of this article

We extend the work of
[
4
]
to totally real number fields
K
(under additional technical hypothesis stated at the beginning of Section
2
which are clarified as we progress through the paper), a problem posed by Arthur in
[
5
, §11]
.
That is to say, we show that

\left(\sum_{[\gamma]}\vol(\gamma){\mathcal{O}}(\gamma,f)-\displaystyle\sum_{\chi}\Tr(\mathbf{1}\otimes\chi)(f_{p}))\right)

can be manipulated so that the limit on the right hand side of (
1
) converges when
G=\GL(2,K)
.
The sum over
\chi
runs over all grossencharacters of
K
unramified outside a finite set
S
.
We emphasize that for this case these are all the representations whose traces must be canceled from the discrete spectrum.
Note that in the case of Altuğ this sum consists only of the trivial character, whence only the trivial representation has to be canceled.
The strategy of proving our main result can be divided into five parts:

Rewriting the regular elliptic part:

The regular elliptic part of the trace formula is given by a concrete sum that runs over the regular elliptic conjugacy classes.
For
\GL(2)
, these classes can be parameterized by the trace and determinant of their characteristic polynomial.
In this step, we explicitly rewrite every term involved in terms of these two parameters.

Application of the approximate functional equation:

The regular elliptic part of the trace formula involves a volume term that can be expressed as the value of certain
L
-functions evaluated at
z=1
.
However, these
L
-functions are not absolutely convergent on the
\Re(z)=1
line.
To overcome this difficulty, we use the approximate functional equation—a technique to expand the values within the critical strip in a way that absolute convergence is recovered at the expense of certain weight factors.
The goal is to modify the
L
-functions appropriately and obtain absolute convergence on the line of interest.

Problem of completion and Poisson summation:

We can now perform Poisson summation provided the following two conditions are satisfied:

•:

the original sum arising from the regular elliptic part of the trace formula can be extended to a sum over a
complete lattice
.

•:

the function referred to above is a smooth function of compact support.

The second issue is resolved relatively easily by introducing weight factors, i.e., smooth cutoffs, when applying the approximate functional equation.
The first issue is significantly more deep—the condition of being ‘regular elliptic’ rules out many pairs of integers which cannot be ignored when performing the Poisson summation.
We circumvent this serious problem by introducing a modified Hilbert symbol.
We can now perform Poisson summation in the standard way.

Concrete evaluation of Kloosterman-type sums:

Performing Poisson summation yields character sums, which we call Kloosterman-type sums; here the ‘character’ is the symbol introduced above.
Evaluating these sums is a critical but lengthy
local
process and the final answer is an explicit quotient of zeta functions.

Isolation of the trivial representation and twists from the discrete spectrum:

There were two steps above which introduced integrals; namely, introducing the weight factors during the approximate functional equation and the Fourier transform in the Poisson summation.
In this stage, we invoke complex analysis—we shift the contours of integration and go over the poles of the weight factors, as well as of those of the quotient of zeta functions coming from the Kloosterman-type sums.
The original sum gets divided into the sum of many other terms via the residue theorem.
Some of the terms cancel each other and among those that remain, one is exactly the trace of the sum of trivial representation and its twists by character whereas the others are multiples of the sum of the traces of the special representation and its twists by characters.
(We follow the terminology of Altuğ, referring to the discrete contribution of the continuous spectrum to the trace formula for GL(2) as the special representation.)

1.4.
Subsequent works

Since this paper was submitted in 2024, several works related to the strategy of Beyond Endoscopy, as Langlands and Arthur developed it, have appeared.
Following the path of Altuğ, there are the papers
[
10
,
11
,
12
,
13
]
.
In these, Y. Cheng extends the results of
[
4
,
1
,
2
]
for the case of
\GL(2,{\mathbb{Q}})
by allowing for ramification.
In another direction, the recent paper
[
16
]
proves results analogous to
[
4
]
for
\GL(3,{\mathbb{Q}})
.

2.
Results and detailed outline of the strategy

The purpose of this section is to discuss the above steps in a little more detail and highlight our main contributions.
In addition to explaining the results we prove in this paper, this section includes a discussion of obstacles that appear when working over a general number field but do not arise over
{\mathbb{Q}}
.
In §
2.6
we pose some questions and present some speculations of what we think should happen in the more general setting.

For our discussion, once and for all we fix a prime ideal
{\mathfrak{p}}
in
K
.
Next, we choose the representation
r
to be the
k
-th symmetric power of the standard representation for a fixed positive integer
k
.
The choice of test function
f
is the same as in
[
4
]
and depends on
k
; see Definition
3.4
for the precise form.
Throughout this paper we assume that
K
is a totally real field, although several of the facts we discuss are true in general.
In §
4.2
we explain the main reason for invoking this hypothesis.
In fact, for certain steps we need to make a stronger assumption that the system of fundamental units is ‘totally positive’; see
(Hyp-pos)
.
Finally, we have to assume that
2
is completely split in
K
to carry out the evaluation of the Kloosterman-type sums; see
(Hyp-split)
.
These hypotheses are technical and were invoked to simplify our calculations.
Our strategy should work without these hypotheses - we will only need to be more careful and precise with some definitions and some calculations will become more cumbersome.
Mathematically, we do not expect there to be any major obstructions in removing these hypotheses.

2.1.
Rewriting of the regular elliptic part

An immediate and striking challenge of considering a general base field is that if a regular elliptic class contributes a non-zero term to the regular elliptic part, its determinant must generate the ideal
{\mathfrak{p}}^{k}
where
k
is a positive integer fixed at the time of defining the test function.
This is automatic when the base field is
\mathbb{Q}
because it has class number
1
, but not always true for a general number field.
Therefore,

•

if we make the ‘wrong’ choice of the prime
{\mathfrak{p}}
or integer
k
, both the regular elliptic part on the geometric side and the sum of the traces of the residual spectrum, in the spectral side, equal
0
.
This case requires no cancellation and one could proceed directly to the evaluation of the limit (analogous to
[
1
,
2
]
).

•

The ‘right’ choice of
{\mathfrak{p}}
and
k
is then used to extract the unit—we fix
{\mathfrak{p}}
and a generator (say
\varepsilon
) of
{\mathfrak{p}}^{k}
.
We write
\det(\gamma)=u\varepsilon
, for some unit
u
in the ring of integers of
K
(denoted by
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
).
Altuğ implicitly does this when writing
\det(\gamma)=\pm p^{k}
; choosing
\varepsilon=p^{k}
and
u=\pm 1
.
Throughout this paper, we work under the assumption that this
divisibility hypothesis
is satisfied; see
(Hyp-div)
.

The regular elliptic part of the trace formula is written as

\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}\\
\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon\neq\square\end{subarray}}\mbox{vol}(\gamma){\mathcal{O}}(\gamma,f),

where
{\mathcal{O}}(\gamma,f)
is the orbital integral and we have used the irreducible characteristic polynomial of
\gamma
, namely

P_{\gamma}(X)=X^{2}-\tau X+u\varepsilon

to classify the regular elliptic conjugacy classes.
The goal of this step is to rewrite the volume and the orbital integral explicitly in terms of the trace
\tau
and the unit
u
.
Since
\varepsilon
is fixed, the determinant is in fact determined by the unit
u
.
This calculation is carried out in §
3
and §
4
.
Our main result is the following which is Theorem
4.3
.

Theorem A
.

Let
{\mathsf{p}}=\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{p}})
, the absolute norm of
{\mathfrak{p}}
from
K
to
{\mathbb{Q}}
.
The regular elliptic part of
\GL(2,K)
can be written as

R(f)=\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\in L(u)}\theta^{\pm}(\tau,u)L(1,\chi_{\gamma})\left(\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})}\prod_{{\mathfrak{q}}\mid(S_{\gamma}/{\mathfrak{d}})}\left(1-\frac{\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{q}})}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{q}})}\right)\right)

where

L(u)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{\tau\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon\neq\square\}.

The theorem is proved in the following steps.

2.1.1.
Volume term

The characteristic polynomial
P_{\gamma}(X)
generates a quadratic field extension
K_{\gamma}/K
and associated to it, we have the quadratic sign character
\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{q}})
for a prime
{\mathfrak{q}}
defined by

\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{q}})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccl}1&\text{if }{\mathfrak{q}}&\textrm{is split}\\
-1&\text{if }{\mathfrak{q}}&\textrm{is inert}\\
0&\text{if }{\mathfrak{q}}&\textrm{is ramified.}\\
\end{array}\right.

Set
D_{\gamma}
to denote the absolute discriminant of
K_{\gamma}
.
Proposition
3.14
shows that the volume term is proportional to

\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{\gamma}\right\rvert}}\cdot L(1,\chi_{\gamma}).

In other words, up to a constant
\kappa
which is the residue of the Dedekind zeta function
\zeta_{K}
at
z=1
, the volume term is a product of
\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{\gamma}\right\rvert}}
and a special value of an
L
-function.

2.1.2.
Orbital integrals

First, we prove the existence of an integral ideal
S_{\gamma}
satisfying

(\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon)=S_{\gamma}^{2}\Delta_{\gamma},

where
\Delta_{\gamma}
is the relative discriminant of
K_{\gamma}/K
.
When the base field is
\mathbb{Q}
, this coincides with the fundamental discriminant and the above equation can be taken as an equality of numbers as opposed to ideals.
In Proposition
3.18
we show that the product of orbital integrals at finite primes satisfy

{\mathcal{O}}(f_{\fin},\gamma)=\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{p}})^{-k/2}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\prod_{{\mathfrak{q}}\mid{\mathfrak{d}}}\left(1-\frac{\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{q}})}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{q}})}\right).

On the other hand at the archimedean places the test function is

f_{\infty}=f_{\nu_{1}}\times\cdots\times f_{\nu_{n}},

where
n=[K\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\mathbb{Q}}]
.
For each real place (which we generically denote by)
\nu
, we follow
[
4
]
and expand the local orbital integral using germ expansions.
This is carefully explained in §
3.5
.

Remark 2.1
.

The fact that we have such a concrete formula is essential for the success of the strategy we adopt.

2.1.3.
Units

If we have to isolate the step whose generalization to the number field setting was the most non-trivial, it would be in computations involving the units.
To deal with the units, we include them as a part of a proper subset of a lattice, over which we later perform Poisson summation.
This part of the lattice is an artificial one but, its presence is crucial for matching the two sides of the trace formula.
We will return to this point in the last step; see §
2.5
.

A crucial observation for dealing with the units is that via the Dirichlet Unit Theorem, the pairs
(\tau,u)
indexing the regular elliptic part of the trace formula can be identified with a full lattice inside of
{\pm}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n-1}
.
This technical construction is carried out in §
4.2
.
More concretely, we define the
interpolation function

\theta^{\pm}(x,y)
from
{\mathbb{R}}^{2n-1}
to
{\mathbb{C}}
in Definition
4.2
where
x
is viewed as a vector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
y
as a vector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}
.
This is a generalization of
\theta^{\pm}(x)
used in
[
4
]
, except that the function appearing in
op. cit.
does not have two coordinates.
In our case, the main reason for the difficulty arises from the presence of fundamental units.
It is in handling these fundamental units that we require that the number field is totally real and satisfies the condition of ‘positivity’.
Settling the issue with the units is particularly challenging because there many choices of how to handle the units without any apparent reason as to why one is better than the other.
Our choice of definition of
\theta^{\pm}
is essentially dictated by our understanding of how to generalize Langlands’ calculations for the special representation when working with a general number field rather than over
{\mathbb{Q}}
.
In other words, once we developed the strategy for the calculations in §
11
, we could make the appropriate choice for the definition of the interpolation function.

The interpolation function resembles the germ expansion of the archimedean orbital integral.
Indeed, the discriminant term arising from studying the volume decomposes into local factors (Proposition
4.1
).
Distributing each local factor to its corresponding orbital integral yields the form of the interpolation function we have defined (in coordinates) at the archimedean places, while at the finite places it amounts to changing the variable
{\mathfrak{d}}
to
S_{\gamma}/{\mathfrak{d}}
.
Substituting the above analysis into the expansion of the regular elliptic part yields Theorem
A
.

2.2.
Application of the approximate functional equation

The Dirichlet series that defines
L(z,\chi_{\gamma})
does not converge absolutely on the real line
\Re(z)=1
(which is where it is being evaluated).
To continue the analysis, we show that the regular elliptic part of the trace formula can be written as

\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\in L(u)}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}(\tau,u)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}(\tau,u)\right),

where
\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})
is a quadratic character whose primitivization yields
\chi_{\gamma}
, and
\Phi_{\pm}(\tau,u),\Psi_{\pm}(\tau,u)
are certain smooth functions with compact support arising from smoothing the interpolation function
\theta^{\pm}
.
The sum over
{\mathfrak{a}}
comes from the Dirichlet series expansion of the
L
-function of
\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})
.
This verification is carried out in §
5
and §
6
.

To prove this, we have to apply the approximate functional equation.
In particular, we need to verify that the hypothesis of the approximate functional equation is satisfied.
Recall that the approximate functional equation applies to
L
-functions whose functional equation is already known.
For us, there are two
L
-functions of interest, namely

L(z,\gamma)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Norm_{K}(S_{\gamma})^{z}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{(1-2z)}\prod_{{\mathfrak{q}}\mid(S_{\gamma}/{\mathfrak{d}})}\left(1-\frac{\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{q}})}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{q}})^{z}}\right),

and its completion

\Lambda(z,\gamma)=\Lambda(z,\chi_{\gamma})L(z,\gamma).

Here,
\Lambda(z,\chi_{\gamma})
is the standard completion of the
L
-function attached to the quadratic sign character.
It is an entire function with a functional equation.
In Theorem
5.3
, we prove the functional equation

\Lambda(z,\gamma)=\Lambda(1-z,\gamma),

which follows from verifying that
L(z,\gamma)
admits a functional equation.
Our proof
1
1

1

Another way to prove the functional equation of this (Zagier) zeta function is via local methods; see
[
19
, Theorem 4]
.
This local approach is more suited for studying the nature of this function as conjectured in
[
9
]
.

involves an algebraic manipulation similar to the one in
[
41
]
.
In Proposition
5.5
we verify that the product of the finite orbital integrals coincides with the value of an appropriate
L
-function (which admits a functional equation) when evaluated at
z=1
.
This allows us to now apply the approximate functional equation.

The step of applying the approximate functional equation introduces two functions; namely, an odd Bessel function (denoted by
F
) whose residues we understand precisely and another related function
H_{\gamma}
for which we know the poles and residues.
Furthermore, similar to the interpolation functions above we can extend
H_{\gamma}
to a function
H_{(x,y)}
.

Since there are several real places, there are several Gamma factors that need to be evaluated.
The main technical estimate is recorded in Lemma
5.15
.
In Theorem
5.10
we write the expansion we get from the approximate functional equation, as a function of a complex variable
z
.
More precisely,

Theorem B
.

The following expression holds for any choice of real
A>0
,

L(z,{\gamma})=\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2z-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}{({\mathfrak{a}})}}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{z}}F\left(\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}{A}\right)\\
+\left(\Norm_{K}(S_{\gamma})\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}\Norm_{K}(\Delta_{\gamma})^{1/2}\right)^{1-2z}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{1-2z}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{1-z}}H_{\gamma}\left(z,\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})A}{\Norm_{K}(S_{\gamma})^{2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}\Norm_{K}(\Delta_{\gamma})}\right),

2.2.1.
Recap: the situation over
{\mathbb{Q}}

Recall that at this stage (see
[
4
, Proposition 3.4]
) Altuğ obtains

\sum_{d^{2}\mid N}^{{}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{d^{2z-1}}\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{a^{s}}\left(\frac{N/d^{2}}{a}\right)F\left(\frac{ad^{2}}{A}\right)+\left(\frac{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N\right\rvert}}{\pi}\right)^{1/2-z}\sum^{{}^{\prime}}_{d^{2}\mid N}\frac{1}{d^{1-2z}}\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{a^{1-z}}\left(\frac{N/d^{2}}{a}\right)H_{N}\left(z,\frac{ad^{2}A}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N\right\rvert}}\right).

Here,
N=\tau^{2}\pm 4p^{k}
and the
′
over the sum refers to some congruence conditions we discuss later.
A quick comparison convinces us that the two expressions are almost the same—except how the
L
-functions at infinity are displayed.
A crucial difference is the appearance of
\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})
in the expression in Theorem
B
while Altuğ’s expression has the Kronecker symbol
\left(\frac{N/d^{2}}{a}\right)
.
These two objects though essentially the same, are constructed (and expressed) differently.
Altuğ’s expression is more explicit because of the availability of the quadratic reciprocity law over
{\mathbb{Q}}
.

2.2.2.
The situation over a general number field

At this stage, while rewriting the regular elliptic part of the trace formula, we encounter cancellation.
More precisely, the Euler factors associated to the primes dividing
S_{\gamma}/{\mathfrak{d}}
in the expansion of the
L
-function associated to the volume term gets cancelled by the factors arising in
L(z,\gamma)
above.
What remains is the Euler product expansion of a
non-primitive
character.

When the Kronecker symbol is available (e.g. over
{\mathbb{Q}}
) the conductor of the non-primitive character becomes the ‘new upper entry’ of the symbol; otherwise we leave the change of conductor of the ‘new’ character implicit.
Set
\partial=\tau^{2}-4\det(\gamma)
.
After evaluating
L(z,\gamma)
at
z=1
we see that the regular elliptic part can be expressed as

\begin{split}&{\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\in L(u)}\theta^{\pm}(\tau,u)\left(\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}{({\mathfrak{a}})}}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}F\left(\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}{\Norm_{K}(\partial)^{\alpha}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{\alpha}}\right)\right)\\
&+\frac{\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{\Norm_{K}(\partial)^{1/2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}}\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\in L(u)}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})H_{\gamma}\left(1,\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}{\Norm_{K}(\partial)^{1-\alpha}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1-\alpha}}\right).\end{split}

For the final step, we define two smooth functions
\Phi_{\pm}(x,y)
and
\Psi_{\pm}(x,y)
with compact support in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2n-1}
in terms of the interpolation function
\theta^{\pm}
, the Bessel function
F
, and
H_{\gamma}
.
Using Proposition
6.6
we conclude with the statement that (up to constants depending on the field
K
) the regular elliptic part can be expressed as

{\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\in L(u)}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}(\tau,u)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}(\tau,u)\right).

The approximate functional equation yields series and integrals that are absolutely convergent, and so they can be appropriately swapped when required.
At this stage the analytic issues have been resolved.

2.3.
Problem of completion and Poisson summation

2.3.1.
Recap: problem of completion over
{\mathbb{Q}}

To understand the gravity of the challenge this step poses, we recall how the issue is resolved in
[
4
]
.
Over
{\mathbb{Q}}
, the regular elliptic part can be rewritten as

\sum_{\mp}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in\mathbb{Z}\\
\tau^{2}-4p^{k}\neq\square\end{subarray}}\sum^{{}^{\prime}}_{d^{2}\mid\tau^{2}\pm 4p^{k}}\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}\theta^{\mp}_{\infty}\left(\frac{\tau}{2p^{k/2}}\right)\frac{1}{ad}\left(\frac{(\tau^{2}\pm 4p^{k})/f^{2}}{a}\right)\left(F\left(\frac{ad^{2}}{A}\right)+\frac{ad^{2}}{\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\tau^{2}\pm 4p^{k}\right\rvert}}}H\left(\frac{ad^{2}A}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\tau^{2}\pm 4p^{k}\right\rvert}}\right)\right).

To perform Poisson summation, one needs a lattice.
In this case, the lattice required is
\mathbb{Z}
, but since several integers are ruled out by imposing
\tau^{2}-4p^{k}\neq\square
(non-square), we do not have a lattice.
To have the lattice, we add the missing integers; i.e. we consider the sum

\sum_{\mp}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in\mathbb{Z}\\
\tau^{2}-4p^{k}=\square\end{subarray}}\sum^{{}^{\prime}}_{d^{2}\mid\tau^{2}\pm 4p^{k}}\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}\theta^{\mp}_{\infty}\left(\frac{\tau}{2p^{k/2}}\right)\frac{1}{ad}\left(\frac{(\tau^{2}\pm 4p^{k})/f^{2}}{a}\right)\left(F\left(\frac{ad^{2}}{A}\right)+\frac{ad^{2}}{\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\tau^{2}\pm 4p^{k}\right\rvert}}}H\left(\frac{ad^{2}A}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\tau^{2}\pm 4p^{k}\right\rvert}}\right)\right).

Summing both these expressions yields a sum over
\tau\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
After relatively straight forward manipulations, one obtains a sum of the values of a smooth function with compact support over a complete lattice.
This allows for performing Poisson summation.

2.3.2.
Problem of completion over a general number field

Naïvely, one would hope that one can mimic the same idea as above.
In other words, we have to consider the following piece to complete the lattice

\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon=\square}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}(\tau,u)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}(\tau,u)\right).

We immediately have a problem—what do
S_{\gamma}
and
\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})
mean?
These were constructed from the knowledge of the quadratic extension associated to
\gamma
.
Without such a quadratic extension, there is no quadratic character or an ideal
S_{\gamma}
; thus, the terms have no meaning.
Over
{\mathbb{Q}}
, both these problems do not arise and the terms involved can be defined regardless of whether their parameters define a regular elliptic class or not because the Kronecker character and the congruence conditions always make sense.

Concretely, we have to settle the following two issues:

Extension of the quadratic symbol:

For any unit
u
, any integer
\tau
, and any integral ideals
{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}
, define a symbol that coincides with
\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})
whenever there exists a regular elliptic matrix
\gamma
parameterized by
(\tau,u)
and
{\mathfrak{d}}
divides
S_{\gamma}
, but that makes sense for any choice of parameters.

Equivalent explicit conditions for
{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}
:

Find an alternative description of the indexing set
\{{\mathfrak{d}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}\}
that depends on the parameters
\tau,u
alone and that makes sense when
\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon=\square
.

The second problem is addressed by the second named author in
[
19
]
which is discussed in §
7
.
We state the relevant result below but refer the reader to the original article for the proof.

Theorem C
.

For a regular elliptic matrix
\gamma
, the following two conditions are equivalent:

(i)

{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}
,

(ii)

{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}\mid\tau^{2}-4\det(\gamma)
and (in
{\mathcal{O}}_{K_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}}}
) the following congruence holds

\frac{\tau^{2}-4\det(\gamma)}{\pi_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}}^{2\val_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}}({\mathfrak{d}})}}\equiv\square\pmod{\pi_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}}^{2}}\ \text{ for each }i=1,\dots,n.

Note that over
{\mathbb{Q}}
, the second condition simplifies and becomes

\frac{\tau^{2}-4\det(\gamma)}{4d^{2}}\equiv 0,1\pmod{4},

which is precisely the congruence conditions we have been referring to so far.
This congruence condition over
{\mathbb{Q}}
was already available to Altuğ from the work of D. Zagier.

2.3.3.
Modified Hilbert symbol

Another major obstacle was in choosing the right symbol which could uncouple the variables
\tau
,
u
,
{\mathfrak{d}}
, and
{\mathfrak{a}}
.
None of the obvious choices such as the Artin symbol, the power residue symbol, the Frobenius symbol, and the Hilbert symbol could do this job.
We define a
modified Hilbert symbol
in terms of the restricted Hilbert symbol; see Definitions
7.3
and
7.6
.
This new symbol recovers the quadratic sign character and upon appropriate twisting also recovers the imprimitive character; see Theorem
7.9
.

The vanishing conditions defining the
restricted
Hilbert symbol guarantee that we work with units (whereas the general Hilbert Symbol is defined for all non-zero entries).
A crucial property for our symbol is that it makes explicit the dependence on
\tau
for
all
the imprimitive characters that can be obtained from
all
the quadratic sign characters of quadratic extensions of
K

simultaneously
.
For the Kronecker symbol, this property is immediate since

\chi_{d}(a)=\left(\frac{(\tau^{2}\pm 4p^{k})/d^{2}}{a}\right).

It is not possible to replace the Kronecker symbol with one of the other choices mentioned above because there is incompatibility with the division at this stage.
The quantity
\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon
is an algebraic integer, and must remain so because any conjugacy class is parameterized by a pair of algebraic integers.
Over
{\mathbb{Q}}
, the class number is
1
and there is only one unit which is a square, so the problem gets resolved immediately.

Remark 2.2
.

To prove the desired properties of the modified Hilbert symbol, we needed to appeal to Artin’s reciprocity law despite our best efforts.
We see this as a positive sign because it suggests that the strategy is sensitive to reciprocity laws which are in great part a motivation for the work in the area.

After resolving both these issues, the
completed regular elliptic part
of the trace formula has the following shape,

\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}\mid\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon}^{{}^{\prime}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\binom{\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}(\tau,u)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}(\tau,u)\right).

2.3.4.
Poisson summation

We show that for any unit
v

\binom{\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}=\binom{(\tau v)^{2}-4uv^{2}\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}.

Exploiting this, we divide the units
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}
into their cosets modulo
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*,2}
and rearrange the sum over the units as a double sum.
The character depends on the coset representative of the outer sum (called
Y
) and is independent of the coset representative of the inner sum (a parameter denoted by
y
).
The additive periodicity modulo
4{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}
allows us to divide the
\tau
-sum according to the congruence
\mu\pmod{4{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}}
.
In Theorem
8.10
we show that the completed regular elliptic part becomes

\frac{\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{2^{3n-1}}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\sum_{Y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}}\sum_{\mu\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}/4{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}}^{{}^{\prime}}\binom{\mu^{2}-4{u(Y)}\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}\\
\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\sum_{\eta\in\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}^{1}(\xi,\eta)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}^{1}(\xi,\eta)\right).

Next, we need to show that the sum coming from the units but not contributing to the Poisson summation (i.e. the sum over
\pm Y
) is a finite sum.
We can (and will) apply Poisson summation to the inner (double) sum.
The main contribution to the spectral side of Poisson summation is when
(\xi,\eta)=(0,0)
since there is no exponential factor mitigating its growth.
Restricting to only this part proves the main theorem of this section which is Theorem
8.14
.

Theorem D
.

The dominant part of the spectral side of the Poisson summation is

\frac{\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{2^{3n-1}}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{Y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}^{1}(0,0)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{3}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{2}}+\frac{\Psi_{\pm}^{1}(0,0)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})}\right)K_{{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}}(\pm u(Y)).

where
\Phi_{\pm}^{1}
and
\Psi_{\pm}^{1}
are the modified Fourier transform in
\mathbb{R}^{2n-1}
of
\Phi_{\pm}
and
\Psi_{\pm}
.
Given ideals
{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
and a unit
u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}
, the function
K_{{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}}(u)
is a
Kloosterman-type sum
.

2.4.
Evaluation of Kloosterman-type sums

This is the content of §
9
and the proof is presented in Appendix
A
.
In Theorem
9.3
we show that the Dirichlet series of a complex parameter
z=\sigma+it
with
\sigma>1
defined as

{\mathbb{D}}(z,u)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2z+1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{K_{{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}}(u)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{z+1}}

admits an analytic continuation to a meromorphic function in the whole complex plane with a pole at
z=\frac{1}{2}
.
Very crucially, we show that this Dirichlet series is in fact independent of
u
.
More precisely,

{\mathbb{D}}(z,u)=4^{n}\frac{\zeta_{K}(2z)}{\zeta_{K}(z+1)}\cdot\frac{1-1/{\mathsf{p}}^{z(k+1)}}{1-1/{\mathsf{p}}^{z}}.

Remark 2.3
.

When
z=1
, the above expression becomes

{\mathbb{D}}(z,u)=4^{n}\cdot\frac{1-1/{\mathsf{p}}^{(k+1)}}{1-1/{\mathsf{p}}}.

This will recover the
{\mathfrak{p}}
-adic contribution of the trace of the trivial representation (the
4^{n}
gets canceled with other terms).
At
z=0
the residue can be computed explicitly and is connected to the value of the special representation.
This concrete expression gives us exactly the contributions of both expected representations.
However, the proof of the above theorem is computational and does not provide insight of
why
should this happen.

Using the
Fundamental Theorem of Arithmetic
and the local nature of the modified Hilbert symbol we factor the above sum into its local pieces.
These local factors look slightly different for primes above 2 and the odd primes.
This motivates the introduction (see Lemma
A.7
) of the local Dirichlet series
{\mathbb{D}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(z,u)
satisfying

{\mathbb{D}}(z,u)=\prod_{{\mathfrak{q}}}{\mathbb{D}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(z,u).

The proof of evaluating
{\mathbb{D}}(z,u)
proceeds by evaluating explicitly
{\mathbb{D}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
for each prime
{\mathfrak{q}}
.
The evaluation of the
{\mathbb{D}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
is a long computation which we do not discuss here.
We limit our discussion to some highlights:

•

the Kloosterman-type sum definition involves the restricted Hilbert symbol but after evaluating the Dirichlet series, this dependence disappears.
We know this
after
the computation; this begs the question, why does the symbol disappear?

•

when
{\mathfrak{q}}\nmid 2
, evaluating
{\mathbb{D}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
is precisely counting the number of solutions of a certain quadratic form modulo
\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{q}})^{b}
for varying values of
b
.
Hensel’s Lemma applies and it suffices to understand the situation modulo
{\mathfrak{q}}
, which is a field and lift the solutions appropriately.

•

when
{\mathfrak{q}}\mid 2
, to apply Hensel’s Lemma we need to consider solutions modulo
{\mathfrak{q}}^{2e_{{\mathfrak{q}}}+1}
, where
e_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
is the ramification degree of
{\mathfrak{q}}
over
2
.
Also, the counting problem is complicated because the local Kloosterman-type sum at primes above
2
have a congruence condition modulo
4
for a certain quotient.
These two issues together make the computation of the corresponding counting problem quite challenging.
Despite our best efforts to tame these Kloosterman-type sums, we were unable to do so.
It is for avoiding this calculation we make the hypothesis that the prime 2 splits in
K
; see
(Hyp-split)
.

These complications are already visible over
\mathbb{Q}
.
Here, the counting problem is to determine the number of solutions to a system of congruences.
When
{\mathfrak{q}}\nmid 2
, the quadratic character
is
the modified Hilbert symbol; hence, the nature of one implies the behaviour of the other.
This is not the case for primes above
2
—not even for the rational numbers.
The quadratic character assigns the value
-1
to
3,5,7\pmod{8}
and the value
1
to
1\pmod{8}
.
The Kronecker symbol on the other hand assigns the value
-1
to
3,5\pmod{8}
and the value
1
to
1,7\pmod{8}
.
This discrepancy at the value of
7
makes the reciprocity laws work for the Kronecker symbol but not recover the quadratic character.
As a consequence, the behaviour of the solutions to the system is erratic as the two symbols contribute differently.

For more general number fields, this problem at primes above
2
grows.
The behaviour of the square class is now dictated modulo
2e_{{\mathfrak{q}}}+1
, but its behaviour has influence from both ramification degree as well as the inertia degree.
Also the formulas of the local Hilbert symbol at primes above
2
are significantly more complicated.
It is unclear how to proceed to get closed formulas for these counting problems.
Nevertheless, the fact that we know what to expect should hint at the possibility that better methods should be available for this evaluation.

Remark 2.4
.

We wish to emphasize that these discussions and computations do not appear in
[
3
,
4
]
where they are left as exercises for the reader.
This might create the false impression that evaluating Kloosterman-types sums at the primes above
2
and at the odd primes have the same level of difficulty.
An important part of our contribution here lies in elucidating that this is not the case.
We are able to do it in the case of the rationals because of the explicit knowledge of how to evaluate the Kloosterman-type sums.
We can also tackle a few other cases—but it is still not in sufficient generality to get the desired formula over a general number field.

2.5.
Isolation of the contribution of the trivial representation

In the last two sections of the paper, we apply tools from complex analysis and calculate the residues of the two integrals arising from the approximate functional equation and the Fourier transform in the Poisson summation formula.
We first isolate the contribution of the
trivial representation
.
Concretely, the value of this representation is computed in Theorem
11.3
(i)
using the Weyl integration formula and it turns out to be

(2)

{{\mathsf{p}}}^{-k/2}R_{K}\frac{1-{{\mathsf{p}}}^{-(k+1)}}{1-{{\mathsf{p}}}^{-1}}\sum_{\pm}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2n-1}}\theta^{\pm}(x,y)dxdy,

Here,
x
(resp.
y
) is viewed as a vector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
(resp.
{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}
) and
R_{K}
is the regulator of
K
.
As in the case of
[
4
]
, we can also find the trace of the special representation; this is done in Theorem
11.3
(ii)
.

We now explain the strategy of our proof which is a generalization of calculations done by Langlands.
However, there are some subtle differences and pitfalls to which we would like to draw the reader’s attention.
Set
G_{\infty}=\GL(2,{\mathbb{R}})\times\cdots\times\GL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
and set
Z_{+}
to be the central subgroup of scalar matrices with positive entries.
As a Lie group, this is one dimensional; hence,
Z_{+}\setminus G_{\infty}
has dimension
4n-1
.
Let
f\in C_{c}(Z_{+}\setminus G_{\infty})
.
Since the group
Z_{+}
is not a product group we cannot consider the action of
Z_{+}
on
G_{\infty}
component-wise.
In other words, it is not enough to compute the value of the trace at the archimedean places by doing local computations in each real embedding.

A crucial observation is that the dimensional reduction attributed to the
Z_{+}
-action on the spectral side corresponds to the fact that rank of the units is
n-1
.
The remaining
n
dimensions come from the trace lattice.

After performing the Weyl integration formula for our choice of the (archimedean) test function, the innermost integral in the Weyl formula becomes the orbital integral while the outer sum over tori gives a partition of
\mathbb{R}^{2n-1}
.
Our careful computations show that as
T
varies over the maximal tori, the inner integral varies over a region
S_{T}
which (up to a set of measure 0) partitions
\{\pm 1\}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2n-1}
.
For example, for the trivial representation, we show in Section
11.4
that the trace
\Tr({\bf 1}(f))
is given by (
2
).
An analogous result for the case of the special representation is shown in Section
11.5
.
We emphasize that if the units were not taken into account by the Poisson summation and were fixed throughout the process by having an outer sum that runs over the units, we would be unable to arrive at this desired conclusion.
This is because the sum over the units might be indexed by an infinite set while the sum on the spectral side is indexed by a finite one.
Our approach ensures that all that remains from the units in the indexing set is the sign.

In §
10
, we identify the contribution of the trivial representation and its twists as well as the contribution of the special representation and its twists by grossencharacters on the geometric side.
To complete the proof of the main theorem (see Theorem
E
below) we then isolate the contribution of the special representation.
The innermost term in the expression in Theorem
D
consists of two sums, one with
\Psi_{\pm}^{1}(0,0)
and another with
\Phi_{\pm}^{1}(0,0)
.
We explicitly write down the (modified) Fourier transform of
\Psi_{\pm}^{1}(0,0)
and
\Phi_{\pm}^{1}(0,0)
, invoke absolute convergence, and change the order of integrals with the sums over
{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}
.
More precisely, in (
16
) we get an expression with three integrals for the sum corresponding to
\Phi_{\pm}^{1}(0,0)
: the two outer ones come from the Fourier transform—one of which corresponds to the variable
x
and the other to the variable
y
—together they correspond to an integral over
\mathbb{R}^{2n-1}
.
The complex line integral comes from the definition of
F
in the approximate functional equation.
We can shift the contour of integration to the left and pass over the poles introduced by the zeta functions and by the Mellin transform of
F
.
This introduces three factors: two residues and the new contour integral.
In Lemma
10.4
we calculate these values precisely and the first of the three pieces is

2^{n-1}h_{K}R_{K}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\frac{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-(k+1)}}{1-{{\mathsf{p}}^{-1}}}\sum_{\pm}\int\int\theta^{\pm}(x,y)dydx.

A similar analysis for
\Psi_{\pm}^{1}(0,0)
is done in Lemma
10.5
.
After appropriate cancellations we at last obtain our main theorem which is Theorem
11.20
in the main text.

Theorem E
.

Let
f
be the fixed test function.
Writing
\mathbf{1}
to denote the trivial representation and
\xi_{0}
to denote the special representation, the dominant part of the spectral side of the Poisson summation is equal to

\sum_{\chi}\Tr((\boldsymbol{1}\otimes\chi)(f))-2\sum_{\chi}\Tr((\xi_{0}\otimes\chi)(f))+\textrm{remainder terms}.

Remark 2.5
.

This is the exact generalization of Altuğ’s result.
To see this, observe that the final expression obtained in
[
4
, Theorem 6.1]
is substituted using the spectral terms that are computed in
[
4
, Lemma 6.2]
.
Altuğ’s Theorem 6.1, in the notation used there, can be expressed as
(13)_{\xi_{0}}=\Tr({\bf{1}}(f^{p,k}))-2\Tr(\xi_{0}(f)^{p,k})+
remainder terms.
The main theorem
[
4
, Theorem 1.1]
is then simply a restatement of this.

From the perspective of Langlands’ original Beyond Endoscopy proposal, the key result here is the cancellation of the contribution of the trivial representation which in this case is the only non-tempered representation of
\GL(2)
in the discrete spectrum assuming the Ramanujan conjecture.
That the second term appears with the same constant multiple as Altuğ’s is a surprise (as it was in the original case), but is no obstruction in itself as the distribution is tempered.
Though as noted by Arthur
[
8
, §3.V]
, in higher rank the associated terms can indeed be non-tempered, so being able to identify it on the geometric side is valuable for further studies.

2.6.
Future Directions and Motivation for Further Research

Our intention of writing this long introduction is to convince the reader that the quest of generalizing
[
4
]
to general number fields was full of surprises and that the mathematics that lurks behind all of this is beautiful.
We made our best efforts to encapsulate these ideas in the questions we propose.
We hope they are the right questions.

2.6.1.
Formula for the non-archimedean orbital integral

This precise formula for the product of non-archimedean orbital integrals is a fundamental problem when rewriting the regular elliptic part of the trace formula.
Obtaining such a precise formula for
\GL(n)
when
n\geq 3
and for other groups is an open problem.
In
[
9
]
, J. Arthur put forth a conjecture regarding what it must be in the case of
\GL(n)
.
For the case of
\GL(2)
, this conjecture was verified in two papers by the second named author, see
[
19
,
18
]
and was crucially used in this work.

2.6.2.
Relaxing the hypothesis on the number field being being totally real and positive

As explained before, we made a technical assumption that the number field
K
is totally real and positive so as to define an interpolation function.
We are optimistic that our results can be proved without this hypothesis.
However, we used both these conditions in significant ways and the process of its removal will enlighten us on how the number theoretic aspects of
K
inform the representation theory at the archimedean places.
It appears that settling the issue on the existence of an appropriate interpolating function is a problem of independent interest.
This depends on the field
K
and is unrelated to its application to the strategy of Beyond Endoscopy.

2.6.3.
Reinterpreting the congruence conditions to complete the lattice

When
G=\GL(n)
for
n\geq 3
, it is not clear to the authors how to rewrite the condition
{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}
in an explicit way so that it depends on the parameters that produced the conjugacy class.
We remind the reader that establishing such an explicit dependence is inherently essential if we want to complete the lattice for performing Poisson summation.

It would be interesting if the answer to the question is given as
congruence conditions of some sort as in the case of
\GL(2)
.
In fact, Langlands already hints at the existence of the congruence
conditions for
\GL(2,{\mathbb{Q}})
in
[
31
]
.
He writes
“The sum over
r\in{\mathbb{Z}}
that occurs in the elliptic term will be replaced by sums over
r
satisfying a congruence condition.
This will entail that whatever behaviour we find for
S=\{\infty\}
should remain valid when congruence conditions are imposed.
Such an assertion, which implies a greater theoretical regularity that may make the proofs easier to come by, has to be tested further, but these are the principles that justify confining myself at first to
m=1
and to representations unramified at all finite places.”

The congruence conditions referred to by Langlands are precisely those given by the congruence conditions we have discussed earlier.
The regularity is manifested in the periodicity which is intrinsic to the symbol.
Our investigations show that such congruence conditions still hold for all symmetric powers (i.e. all choices of
k
, which Langlands calls
m
).
However, if class field theory is to be any guide, we think that the congruence conditions might not be the appropriate way to describe the equivalent form of the divisibility conditions.
As we know, the splitting behaviour of primes in non-abelian extensions cannot be described by congruences alone.
In essence, we might be looking for more complicated representation theory conditions which, in the abelian case, collapse to congruences.

2.6.4.
Relaxing the hypothesis involving splitting of 2 and more general Kloosterman-like sums

The condition on the prime 2 is a technical hypothesis.
We are optimistic that it should be possible to compute the value of the Kloosterman-like sums for the primes above 2 when the ramification or inertia degree are not both 1.

Another question to ask is what are these Kloosterman-like sums for other cases?
Giving a complete description in the general case is beyond our reach since the shape that the regular elliptic part of the trace formula will take is still unknown to us.
However, we might succeed in making educated guesses for situations when the base field
K
is well behaved (e.g. a Kummer extension of
\mathbb{Q}
) and the prime splitting is well-understood.

Finally, we reiterate that the value of
{\mathbb{D}}(z,u)
does not depend on the unit
u
.
This makes us believe that the computations might be doable in a better way that elucidates why this parameter disappears.

2.6.5.
The appearance of
-2
in Theorem
E

At the time of writing this article, the authors are perplexed by the appearance of
-2
in Theorem
E
as it matches Altuğ’s calculations exactly.
We note that the negative sign is expected because it appears in the trace formula computations.
However, the appearance of 2 in our result and in
[
4
]
is surprising.
A common feature in the trace formula theory is to build objects with ‘nicer’ properties out of others which lack the property (for example, stable orbital integrals from invariant ones).
In this process, each of the building blocks appears with some multiplicity.
Is this
-2
a foreshadowing of a construction of a new trace formula with ‘nicer’ properties?
If so, what is it that we are constructing that requires the special representation to have multiplicity
-2
as opposed to
-1
?

For the ease of reading this paper, we now summarize notation that is used throughout.

p
-adic local field

n
.

.

u=u(\pm,y)=\pm\beta_{1}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1}^{y_{n-1}}

If the sign is fixed, we write

•

L(u)=\{\tau\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon\neq\square\}
.

•

R
is any ring

3.
The Main Setup

This section is preliminary in nature.
The main goal is to set the stage, give precise definitions, and describe the main setup of our work.
We clarify our first main hypothesis on a certain divisibility condition that must be satisfied.

3.1.
Definition of the test function and its implications

For any ring
R
, set
G(R)=\GL(2,R)
to denote the algebraic group of
2\times 2
invertible matrices over
R
.
Throughout this paper,
K
is a totally real number field
2
2

2

Many statements we prove in this paper do not require that the number field is totally real.
However, this is a technical hypothesis we need to impose for certain calculations which will be clarified in §
4.2
.
.

Definition 3.1
.

An element
\gamma\in G(K)
is called a
regular elliptic element
if its characteristic polynomial is irreducible over
K
.
Given such a
\gamma
, define its
characteristic polynomial
as

P_{\gamma}(X)=P(X)=X^{2}-\Tr(\gamma)X+\det(\gamma)

where
\Tr(\gamma)
and
\det(\gamma)
are the trace and determinant of
\gamma
, respectively.

Notation
.

We henceforth write
\tau=\Tr(\gamma)
.

This polynomial
P_{\gamma}(X)
generates a quadratic extension
L=K(\gamma)
of
K
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
\gamma\in G(K)
be a regular elliptic element.

(i)

The algebra centralizer

{\Mat}_{\gamma}(K)=\{X\in{\Mat}_{2\times 2}(K)\mid\ X\gamma=\gamma X\}

is (field) isomorphic to
L
.
Also, the diagonal matrices are identified with
K
.

(ii)

The group centralizer
G_{\gamma}(K)=\GL(2,K)_{\gamma}\subset{\Mat}_{\gamma}(K)
coincides with the invertible elements
L^{*}
of
L
.

(iii)

Under the previous identifications,
\gamma
is a root of its characteristic polynomial, allowing identifying the matrix
\gamma
with an element of
L
.

Proof.

See
[
26
, §5.9]
.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

(i)

Even though
\gamma
is a priori a matrix, Proposition
3.2
allows us to view it as an element of
L
.
We will shift between both perspectives without specifying when there is no possibility of confusion.

(ii)

When
\tau
and
\det(\gamma)
are algebraic integers of
K
, the regular elliptic element
\gamma
is an algebraic integer of
K(\gamma)
.

Throughout this paper,
we fix a prime ideal
{\mathfrak{p}}
in
K
.
Let
{\mathfrak{q}}
be any prime ideal of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
, which depending on the situation may or may not be equal to
{\mathfrak{p}}
.
The
{\mathfrak{q}}
-adic completion of
K
is denoted by
K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
.
A regular elliptic element
\gamma\in G(K)
is an element of
G(K_{{\mathfrak{q}}})=\GL(2,K_{{\mathfrak{q}}})
.
It generates a quadratic extension of reduced
K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
-algebras and there are three possibilities for these extensions:

(a)

the split case (i.e. the extension is
K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\times K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
),

(b)

the non-split unramified case (i.e. the extension is unramified),

(c)

the non-split ramified case (i.e. the extension is ramified).

This behaviour parallels the splitting behaviour of
{\mathfrak{q}}
in the quadratic extension
L/K
.
In particular,
\gamma
can be identified with an element of the (local) extension of
K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
.

3.1.1.
Choice of Measures

For ease of calculations, in this section we follow the choices of measures made by Langlands, and we explain them below:

•

At a finite place
{\mathfrak{q}}
, we equip
G(K_{{\mathfrak{q}}})
with the Haar measure such that the maximal compact set
G({\mathcal{O}}_{K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}})
has measure 1.
For a regular elliptic element
\gamma
, the centralizer
G_{\gamma}(K_{{\mathfrak{q}}})
is also endowed with a Haar measure such that
G_{\gamma}({\mathcal{O}}_{K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}})
has measure 1.

•

At an infinite place
\nu
, we equip
G(K_{\nu})
with the standard Lebesgue measure.
For a regular elliptic element
\gamma
, the centralizer
G_{\gamma}(K_{\nu})
is endowed with a measure that depends on whether this torus is split or elliptic.
Representatives of these two tori are, respectively,

\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}&0\\
0&\lambda_{2}\\
\end{pmatrix}\mid\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\neq 0\right\}\ \textrm{ and }\ \left\{\begin{pmatrix}r\cos(\theta)&r\sin\theta\\
-r\sin(\theta)&r\cos\theta\\
\end{pmatrix}\mid r>0,\;0\leq\theta\leq\pi\right\}.

The elliptic torus can also be described as

\left\{\begin{pmatrix}a&-b\\
b&a\\
\end{pmatrix}\mid a,b\in{\mathbb{R}},\;a^{2}+b^{2}\neq 0\right\}.

For each case our choice of measure is as follows:

–

split case:
we endow it with the measure
\frac{d\lambda_{1}d\lambda_{2}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\right\rvert}}
, where
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
are the eigenvalues of elements in the split torus
G_{\gamma}(K_{\nu})
.

–

elliptic case:
we parameterize letting
0<r<\infty
; and the invariant Haar measure is
2drd\theta/r
.
This measure coincides, in the other coordinates, with
2dadb/(a^{2}+b^{2})
.

In each situation, it follows from the general theory of reductive groups that the space
G_{\gamma}\setminus G
admits a unique right invariant measure that makes the integration in stages formula true without constants.

3.1.2.
Definition of the Test Function

As mentioned in the introduction, we need to manipulate the regular elliptic part for a specific choice of test function.
We now explain its construction.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
{\mathfrak{a}}
be any ideal of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
and write
\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})
to denote the absolute norm of
{\mathfrak{a}}
from
K
to
{\mathbb{Q}}
.
Let
k
be a fixed non-negative integer and let
{\mathfrak{q}}
be a prime ideal of
K
(possibly equal to
{\mathfrak{p}}
).
Write
\mathbbm{1}
to denote the indicator function of the maximal compact subgroup
G({\mathcal{O}}_{K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}})
.
For a non-negative integer
k
define

\displaystyle f_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{(k)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbbm{1}\left(X\in\Mat({\mathcal{O}}_{K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}})\mid\;\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\det(X)\right\rvert}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}=\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{q}})^{-k}\right)\text{ and }

\displaystyle f_{\mathfrak{q}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\begin{cases}f_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{(0)}=\mathbbm{1}\left(X\in\Mat({\mathcal{O}}_{K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}})\mid\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\det(X)\right\rvert}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}=1\right)&\text{ if }{\mathfrak{q}}\neq{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{p}})^{-k/2}f_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{(k)}=\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{p}})^{-k/2}\mathbbm{1}\left(X\in\Mat({\mathcal{O}}_{K_{{\mathfrak{p}}}})\mid\;\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\det(X)\right\rvert}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}=\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{p}})^{-k}\right)&\text{ if }{\mathfrak{q}}={\mathfrak{p}}.\end{cases}

At the archimedean places, we denote the test functions (at each real place) by
f_{\nu_{i}}
.
We also define

f_{\infty}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}f_{\nu_{i}}.

The
only
conditions we impose on the test functions is that their orbital integrals must have compact support as functions of the trace and the determinant (see Section
3.5
for a more detailed discussion on what this means) and that

f_{\infty}\in C_{c}(Z_{+}\backslash G_{\infty}).

With this, we now construct our test function.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
be a totally real number field of degree
n
.
Fix a positive integer
k
as in Definition
3.4
.
The test function used throughout this paper is the following

f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\prod_{{\mathfrak{q}}}f_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\times f_{\infty}=\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{p}})^{-k/2}f_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{(k)}\times\prod_{{\mathfrak{q}}\neq{\mathfrak{p}}}f_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{(0)}\times f_{\infty}.

Set

f_{\textrm{fin}}=\prod_{{\mathfrak{q}}}f_{{\mathfrak{q}}}=\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{p}})^{-k/2}f_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{(k)}\times\prod_{{\mathfrak{q}}\neq{\mathfrak{p}}}f_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{(0)}.

Since this function can be expressed as a product, its adèlic orbital integral can be split into the product of the local orbital integrals.
More precisely, let
\gamma
represent a regular elliptic element in
G(K)
; then

{\mathcal{O}}(f,\gamma)=\prod_{{\mathfrak{q}}}{\mathcal{O}}(f_{{\mathfrak{q}}},\gamma)\times{\mathcal{O}}(f_{\infty},\gamma)={\mathcal{O}}(f_{\fin},\gamma)\times{\mathcal{O}}(f_{\infty},\gamma).

3.1.3.
Definition of the orbital integral

In view of our choice of measures, the orbital integrals are defined as follows:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}(f_{{\mathfrak{q}}},\gamma)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{G_{\gamma}(K_{{\mathfrak{q}}})\setminus G(K_{{\mathfrak{q}}})}f_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(x^{-1}\gamma x)dx,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}(f_{\infty},\gamma)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{G_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}})\setminus G({\mathbb{R}})}f_{\infty}(x^{-1}\gamma x)dx.

These orbital integrals are invariant under conjugation by
\gamma
.
If this integral is not
0
, then at some point the integrand must not be
0
itself.
Hence, there exists
x\in G(K_{{\mathfrak{q}}})
such that

x^{-1}\gamma x\in G({\mathcal{O}}_{K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}})

and satisfies the condition of the indicator function appearing in the definition of
f_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
.
Since the determinant is invariant under conjugation, we conclude that

\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\det(x^{-1}\gamma x)\right\rvert}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}=\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\det(\gamma)\right\rvert}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{p}})^{-k}&\text{if }{\mathfrak{q}}={\mathfrak{p}}\\
1&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

This specifies the value of the
{\mathfrak{q}}
-adic norm of
\gamma
at every finite place
{\mathfrak{q}}
.
The following result is now almost immediate.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
\gamma\in G(K)
be a regular elliptic element such that
{\mathcal{O}}(f_{\fin},\gamma)\neq 0
.
Then
\det(\gamma)\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
and
\tau\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
.
Fix an integer
k
as in Definition
3.5
.
As ideals,

(\det(\gamma))={\mathfrak{p}}^{k}.

Proof.

The only part that requires a proof is the claim that
\tau\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
.
Let
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
be the roots of
P_{\gamma}(X)
in
L/K
.
Since they are Galois conjugates, they have the same norm (in
L
).
If
{\mathfrak{q}}_{L}\mid{\mathfrak{q}}
is a prime ideal in
L
, the
{\mathfrak{q}}
-adic norm can be extended to a
{\mathfrak{q}}_{L}
-adic norm.

\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\right\rvert}_{{\mathfrak{q}}_{L}}\leq\max\left\{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\lambda_{1}\right\rvert}_{{\mathfrak{q}}_{L}},\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\lambda_{2}\right\rvert}_{{\mathfrak{q}}_{L}}\right\}=\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\lambda_{1}\right\rvert}_{{\mathfrak{q}}_{L}}=\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\right\rvert}_{{\mathfrak{q}}_{L}}^{1/2}=\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\det(\gamma)\right\rvert}_{{\mathfrak{q}}_{L}}^{1/2}\leq 1.

We deduce that
\tau=\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{L}
.
However,
\tau
is an element of
K
, so
\tau\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
.
∎

Definition 3.7
.

Conjugacy classes with
{\mathcal{O}}(f_{\fin},\gamma)\neq 0
will be referred to as
contributing conjugacy classes
.

Corollary 3.8
.

If
\gamma
represents a contributing conjugacy class, then it is an algebraic integer in
L=K(\gamma)
and in
L_{\mathfrak{q}}=K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\gamma)
for all primes
{\mathfrak{q}}
.

Definition 3.9
.

Set
{\mathbb{A}}_{K}
to denote the ring of adèles of
K
.
Let
Z_{+}
be the connected central subgroup of
G_{\gamma}(K)
.
The
volume
of the regular elliptic element
\gamma
is defined as

\vol(\gamma)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\vol\left(Z_{+}G_{\gamma}\left(K\right)\setminus G_{\gamma}\left({\mathbb{A}}_{K}\right)\right).

Our object of study is the regular elliptic part of the trace formula for
\GL(2,K)
.

Proposition 3.10
.

The regular elliptic part of the trace formula for
\GL(2,K)
is

R(f)=\sum_{\gamma}\vol(\gamma){\mathcal{O}}(f,\gamma),

where the sum runs over all conjugacy classes in
\GL(2,K)
of regular elliptic elements.

Proof.

Technically, this statement follows immediately from definitions.
We make references to the literature so that the reader can chase the appropriate definitions for a general number field.

Within this short proof we follow the notation of
[
7
]
.

In (27.1) it is stated that the regular elliptic part of the invariant trace formula, for a general reductive group
G
over
F
, is

\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\textrm{reg, ell}}(G)}a^{G}(\gamma)f_{G}(\gamma).

Here,
a^{G}(\gamma)
stands for the volume we defined in Definition
3.9
and
f_{G}(\gamma)={\mathcal{O}}(f,\gamma)
.

As explained in the line above this equation in
op. cit.
,
\Gamma_{\textrm{reg},{\el}}(G)
consists of the equivalence classes of regular elliptic conjugacy classes in
\Gamma(G)_{S}
, where
S
denotes the set of archimedean places.

In (22.1), it is defined that

\Gamma(G)_{S}=(G(F))_{G,S},

and the notation on the left consists of conjugacy classes under
(G,S)
-equivalence.
This concept is defined in p. 113, and consists of two conditions that must be satisfied (the second of which refers to a definition on unipotent elements given on p. 110).
These conditions
never
identify two different regular elliptic conjugacy classes.
Hence, each regular conjugacy class contributes a different term to the regular elliptic part of the trace formula.
The result follows.
∎

Now we can prove the following result.

Theorem 3.11
.

Let
f
be a test function as before.
If the order of
{\mathfrak{p}}
in the class group of
K

does not
divide the non-negative integer
k
, then the regular elliptic part of the trace formula for the test function
f
vanishes.
Equivalently,

\sum_{\gamma}\vol(\gamma){\mathcal{O}}(f,\gamma)=0.

Proof.

If the regular elliptic part of the trace formula for the function
f
does not vanish, then Proposition
3.6
asserts that
(\det(\gamma))={\mathfrak{p}}^{k}
.
Hence,
{\mathfrak{p}}^{k}
is a principal ideal and the result follows.
∎

Remark 3.12
.

(i)

Even when
\gamma
is a contributing conjugacy class, it is possible that the archimedean contribution of the orbital integral is zero.
In other words, when
\gamma
is a contributing conjugacy class, the summand
\vol(\gamma){\mathcal{O}}(f,\gamma)
can vanish precisely when the infinite orbital integral does.

(ii)

Set
h_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
to denote the order of
{\mathfrak{p}}
in the class group of
K
.
In view of Theorem
3.11
, we assumpe
h_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\mid k
throughout this paper.
This allows manipulating the regular elliptic part and finding the contribution of the trivial representation in it.

(Hyp-div)

With notation as above, we assume
h_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\mid k
and refer to this as (Hyp-div).

Notation
.

Fix a positive integer
k
satisfying
(Hyp-div)
.
Since
{\mathfrak{p}}^{h_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
is principal, we fix a generator
\rho_{{\mathfrak{p}}}=\rho
of
{\mathfrak{p}}^{h_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}

and suppose that
k={h_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}k^{\prime}
.
Then

(3)

\left(\det\left(\gamma\right)\right)={\mathfrak{p}}^{k}={\mathfrak{p}}^{{h_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}k^{\prime}}=\left(\rho\right)^{k^{\prime}}=\left(\rho^{k^{\prime}}\right).

Set
\varepsilon=\rho^{k^{\prime}}
.
Note that
\rho
depends on the prime
{\mathfrak{p}}
, up to units of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
, and
k^{\prime}
depends on
k
and
{\mathfrak{p}}
.
There is no dependence on
\gamma
.
In particular, it is possible to define and fix
\varepsilon
without any reference to
\gamma
.

3.2.
The analytic class number formula

So far we have shown that

\sum_{\gamma}\vol\left(\gamma\right){\mathcal{O}}\left(f,\gamma\right)=\sum_{\left(\det\left(\gamma\right)\right)={\mathfrak{p}}^{k}}\vol\left(\gamma\right){\mathcal{O}}\left(f,\gamma\right).

Next, we simplify the volume term and express it in terms of arithmetic quantities associated with the number fields
K
and
L=K(\gamma)
.
We first quote the following well-known result.

Proposition 3.13
.

For a regular elliptic element
\gamma
, there exists a measure preserving isomorphism

Z_{+}G_{\gamma}(K)\setminus G_{\gamma}({\mathbb{A}}_{K})\cong L^{*}\setminus{\mathbb{I}}_{L}^{1},

where
{\mathbb{I}}_{L}^{1}
are the unit idèles.

Proof.

See
[
25
, Proposition 26.1]
.
∎

The right hand side of the above isomorphism is the object of study when one proves the analytic class number formula via idèlic methods.
The following proposition calculates its measure.
Thus, by Proposition
3.13
we know its precise value.

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
\gamma
be a regular elliptic element and let
\chi_{\gamma}
denote the quadratic sign character associated the quadratic extension
L/K
generated by
\gamma
.
Then, by transferring the idèlic measure from Proposition
3.13
, we have

\vol(\gamma)=\frac{2^{r_{1}}h_{K}R_{K}}{\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}}\omega_{K}}L(1,\chi_{\gamma})\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{L}\right\rvert}},

where
h_{K}
is the class number of
K
,
R_{K}
is the regulator of
K
, and
D_{F}
is the discriminant of the number field
F\in\{K,L\}
.

Proof.

Let
r_{1}^{\prime}
(resp.
r_{2}^{\prime}
) denote the number of real (resp. complex) embeddings of
L
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\vol(\gamma)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\vol\left(Z_{+}G_{\gamma}\left(K\right)\setminus G_{\gamma}\left({\mathbb{A}}_{K}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\vol\left(L^{*}\setminus{\mathbb{I}}_{L}^{1}\right)\qquad\textrm{by Proposition~\ref{centralizer idele equation}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{r_{1}^{\prime}}(2\pi)^{r_{2}^{\prime}}h_{L}R_{L}}{\omega_{L}\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{L}\right\rvert}}}\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{L}\right\rvert}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{z\rightarrow 1}(z-1)\zeta_{L}(z)\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{L}\right\rvert}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{z\rightarrow 1}(z-1)\zeta_{K}(z)L(z,\chi_{\gamma})\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{L}\right\rvert}}\qquad\textrm{by \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{IwanamiSeries}{}{}, Theorem 8.15]}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{r_{1}}h_{K}R_{K}}{\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}}\omega_{K}}\lim_{z\rightarrow 1}L(z,\chi_{\gamma})\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{L}\right\rvert}}

\displaystyle=\kappa L(1,\chi_{\gamma})\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{L}\right\rvert}},

where
\kappa=\frac{2^{r_{1}}h_{K}R_{K}}{\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}}\omega_{K}}
is the residue of
\zeta_{K}
at
z=1
.
The second equality follows from Tate’s computation of the measure of
L^{*}\backslash{\mathbb{I}}^{1}_{L}

[
38
, Theorem 4.3.2]
(see also
[
33
, §7.5]
), adjusted for our normalization of the local measures: we take
\vol({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{*})=1
at the finite places, whereas J. Tate uses the self-dual normalization with
\vol({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{*})=(\Norm{\mathfrak{d}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}})^{-1/2}
, and the global discrepancy
\prod_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\Norm{\mathfrak{d}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}})^{1/2}=\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{L}\right\rvert}}
accounts for the factor of
\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{L}\right\rvert}}
.
∎

Since
K
is totally real of degree
n
, we have
r_{1}=n
and
\omega_{K}=2
.
Consequently,

\kappa=\frac{2^{n-1}h_{K}R_{K}}{\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}}}.

This value will be repeatedly used in our explicit calculations throughout the paper.
The trace formula becomes

\sum_{\gamma}\vol\left(\gamma\right){\mathcal{O}}\left(f,\gamma\right)=\kappa\sum_{\left(\det\left(\gamma\right)\right)={\mathfrak{p}}^{k}}L(1,\chi_{\gamma})\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{L}\right\rvert}}{\mathcal{O}}\left(f,\gamma\right).

From now on, we will work with the right hand side of the above equation.

3.3.
{\mathfrak{q}}
-adic Orbital Integrals

We begin by recording a result of Langlands from
[
31
, Lemma 1]
which involves computing orbital integrals.
His proof is explained over
{\mathbb{Q}}
but it can be extended to number fields in general.
Throughout, we will assume that
\gamma
is a contributing conjugacy class.

Lemma 3.15
.

Fix a positive integer
3
3

3

This lemma is true for a general positive integer
k
which need not necessarily satisfy
(Hyp-div)
.

k
.
Let
{\mathfrak{q}}
a prime ideal of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
and let
K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
denote its
{\mathfrak{q}}
-adic completion.
Set
{\mathsf{q}}=\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{q}})
.
Then

{\mathcal{O}}(f_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{(k)},\gamma)=0,

unless
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\det(\gamma)\right\rvert}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}={\mathsf{q}}^{-k}
.
In the latter case, there exists a non-negative integer
n_{\gamma}=n_{\gamma,{\mathfrak{q}}}
such that

(i)

if the extension
K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\gamma)/K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}

splits
, then

{\mathcal{O}}(f_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{(k)},\gamma)={\mathsf{q}}^{n_{\gamma}}.

(ii)

if the extension
K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\gamma)/K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
does not split and is
unramified
then

{\mathcal{O}}(f_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{(k)},\gamma)={\mathsf{q}}^{n_{\gamma}}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{q}}+1}{{\mathsf{q}}-1}\right)-\frac{2}{{\mathsf{q}}-1}.

(iii)

if the extension
K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\gamma)/K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
does not split and is
ramified
then

{\mathcal{O}}(f_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{(k)},\gamma)=\left(\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{n_{\gamma}+1}}{{\mathsf{q}}-1}\right)-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}-1}.

In particular, when
n_{\gamma}=0
the orbital integral
{\mathcal{O}}(f_{{\mathfrak{q}}},\gamma)=1
is independent of the splitting behaviour.

Proof.

See
[
31
]
for an approach counting lattices in the building of
\SL(2,K_{{\mathfrak{q}}})
.
For a different approach see
[
19
, Proposition 5]
.
∎

The contributing conjugacy classes are precisely the ones where
{\mathcal{O}}(f_{{\mathsf{q}}}^{(k)},\gamma)\neq 0
.
This is because
(\det(\gamma))={\mathfrak{p}}^{k}
.
At the fixed prime
{\mathfrak{p}}
, we have that
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\det(\gamma)\right\rvert}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}=\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{p}})^{-k}
and at primes
{\mathfrak{q}}\neq{\mathfrak{p}}
, we have that
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\det(\gamma)\right\rvert}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}=\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{q}})^{0}=1
.

Notation
.

As in the above lemma, we often write
\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{p}})={\mathsf{p}}
and
\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{q}})={\mathsf{q}}
.

We now define a new ideal of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
.

Definition 3.16
.

Consider the regular elliptic element
\gamma
(viewed as an element of
L
).
Write
D_{L/K}(\gamma)
to denote the discriminant of the element
\gamma
and
\Delta_{\gamma}
to denote the relative discriminant of the quadratic extension
L/K
.
Define the ideal
S_{\gamma}
as follows

S_{\gamma}^{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{D_{L/K}(\gamma)}{\Delta_{\gamma}}.

The ideal
S_{\gamma}
encodes ramification behaviour and in turn is related to the orbital integral.
More precisely,

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
{\mathfrak{q}}
be a any prime ideal of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
.
Then,

n_{\gamma,{\mathfrak{q}}}=\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(S_{\gamma}).

Proof.

See
[
19
, Proposition 4]
.
∎

The next result follows immediately from the above discussion.

Proposition 3.18
.

Let
\gamma
be a contributing matrix.
Then,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}(f_{\fin},\gamma)

\displaystyle={\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\prod_{{\mathfrak{q}}\mid S_{\gamma}}{\mathcal{O}}(f_{{\mathfrak{q}}},\gamma)

\displaystyle={\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\prod_{{\mathfrak{q}}\mid{\mathfrak{d}}}\left(1-\frac{\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{q}})}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{q}})}\right)

\displaystyle={\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\Norm_{K}(S_{\gamma})\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})}\prod_{{\mathfrak{q}}\mid{\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime}}\left(1-\frac{\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{q}})}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{q}})}\right),

where
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=S_{\gamma}/{\mathfrak{d}}
.

Proof.

See
[
19
, Theorem 3]
.
The presence of the factor of
{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}
is due to the slight difference in the definition of the test function; see
[
19
, line above (24)]
.
∎

3.4.
Extracting the unit sum.

Recall that we have fixed a prime ideal
{\mathfrak{p}}
and an integer
k
satisfying
(Hyp-div)
such that
{\mathfrak{p}}^{h_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
is generated by
\rho
and
\varepsilon=\rho^{k^{\prime}}
is a generator of
{\mathfrak{p}}^{k}
.
We now manipulate the regular elliptic part.

Proposition 3.19
.

A contributing conjugacy class
\gamma
is determined precisely by a pair
(\tau,u)
, where
\tau
is in
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
,
u
is in
{\mathcal{O}}^{*}_{K}
, and
\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon
is not a square in
K
.

Proof.

Given
\gamma
, we view it as a matrix with
\Tr(\gamma)=\tau
.
By Proposition
3.6
, we know that
\tau
is an integer in
K
.
Since
(\det(\gamma))={\mathfrak{p}}^{k}=(\varepsilon)
, we obtain that
\det(\gamma)=u\varepsilon
for some unit
u
.
Since
\gamma
is a regular elliptic element, we also know that
