\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon
is not a square; otherwise, the roots of the characteristic polynomial would be in
K
.

Conversely, given such a
\tau
and
u
, consider the matrix

\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-u\varepsilon&\tau\end{pmatrix}\in G(K)
.
The associated characteristic polynomial is
X^{2}-\tau X+u\varepsilon
, which is irreducible over
K
.
Hence, this represents a regular elliptic class, which is in fact a contributing class since
u\varepsilon
generates
{\mathfrak{p}}^{k}
.
∎

Proposition 3.20
.

With notation and assumptions introduced above, define the set

L(u)=\{\tau\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon\neq\square\}.

Then

R(f)=\sum_{\gamma}\vol(\gamma){\mathcal{O}}(f,\gamma)=\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\in L(u)}\vol\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-u\varepsilon&\tau\end{pmatrix}\right){\mathcal{O}}\left(f,\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-u\varepsilon&\tau\end{pmatrix}\right).

Proof.

We have seen that

\sum_{\gamma}\vol\left(\gamma\right){\mathcal{O}}\left(f,\gamma\right)=\sum_{\left(\det\left(\gamma\right)\right)={\mathfrak{p}}^{k}}\vol\left(\gamma\right){\mathcal{O}}\left(f,\gamma\right).

Using Proposition
3.19
and rearranging the sum, we obtain

\sum_{\gamma}\vol(\gamma){\mathcal{O}}(f,\gamma)=\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\in L(u)}\vol\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-u\varepsilon&\tau\end{pmatrix}\right){\mathcal{O}}\left(f,\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-u\varepsilon&\tau\end{pmatrix}\right).

This re-grouping can be done because this part of the trace formula converges absolutely.
∎

Notation
.

We continue to write

\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\in L(u)}\vol(\gamma){\mathcal{O}}(f,\gamma)

instead of

\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\in L(u)}\vol\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-u\varepsilon&\tau\end{pmatrix}\right){\mathcal{O}}\left(f,\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-u\varepsilon&\tau\end{pmatrix}\right).

But, we keep in mind that
\gamma
is now a specific conjugacy class that depends on
u
and
\tau
.

3.5.
Archimedean Orbital Integrals

We begin with a brief review of what happens in
G({\mathbb{R}})=\GL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
, so that our next computations are clearer.

3.5.1.
Overview of Germ Expansion

Let
h
be a smooth function of compact support in
G({\mathbb{R}})
and let
\gamma
be a regular elliptic element.
Its orbital integral defined as

{\mathcal{O}}(h,\gamma)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{G_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}})\setminus G({\mathbb{R}})}h(x^{-1}\gamma x)dx

is a class function, i.e. it only depends on the conjugacy class of
\gamma
.
Following Langlands, we define the map

\begin{split}\ch\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}G({\mathbb{R}})&\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{*}\\
\gamma&\mapsto(\Tr(\gamma),4\det(\gamma)),\end{split}

and use the coordinates
(r,N)
for the codomain.
The center of
G({\mathbb{R}})
becomes the parabola
N=r^{2}
minus the origin, which divides the plane into two disjoint regions.
Away from the parabola, the map
\ch\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}G({\mathbb{R}})\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
is a submersion and its fibers are the regular semisimple conjugacy classes.
A matrix in
G({\mathbb{R}})
maps to a point in the interior of the parabola precisely when the matrix corresponds to an element of the elliptic torus (denoted by
T_{\el}
).
Whereas, the matrix maps to a point in the exterior of the parabola when it corresponds to an element of the split torus (denoted by
T_{\spl}
).

These tori are defined as follows:

T_{\spl}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}&0\\
0&\lambda_{2}\\
\end{pmatrix}\mid\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\neq 0\right\}\ \textrm{ and }\ T_{\el}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}r\cos(\theta)&r\sin\theta\\
-r\sin(\theta)&r\cos\theta\\
\end{pmatrix}\mid r>0,\;0\leq\theta\leq\pi\right\}.

Depending on the nature of the regular elliptic element
\gamma
, its centralizer
G_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}})
is either conjugate to
T_{\spl}
or
T_{\el}
(often denoted as
A,B
in the literature).

In what follows, we follow
[
35
]
closely and use her notation.

Recall that the unipotent matrices are the ones of the form

\begin{pmatrix}1&x\\
0&1\\
\end{pmatrix}

with standard Haar measure
dn=dx
.
Let
K_{0}
be the circle group of matrices

\begin{pmatrix}\cos(\theta)&\sin\theta\\
-\sin(\theta)&\cos\theta\\
\end{pmatrix},

with Haar measure
dk=d\theta
.
Further define the integrals

\displaystyle h_{0}(x)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{K_{0}}h(kxk^{-1})dk,

\displaystyle H_{r}(u,v)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{K_{0}}h\left(rk\exp\begin{pmatrix}0&u\\
-v&0\\
\end{pmatrix}k^{-1}\right)dk.

Let
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
be the two roots of the polynomial
P_{\gamma}
.
Define the
Weyl discriminant

D(\gamma)=D(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=(1-\lambda_{1}/\lambda_{2})(1-\lambda_{2}/\lambda_{1})=\frac{-(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})^{2}}{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}.

Then, D. Shelstad
[
35
]

has shown that

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D(\gamma)\right\rvert}^{\frac{1}{2}}{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\spl}}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{1}}{\lambda_{2}}\right\rvert}^{\frac{1}{2}}\int_{N}h_{0}(n\gamma)dn\text{ and}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\el}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}r\cos(\theta)&r\sin\theta\\
-r\sin(\theta)&r\cos\theta\\
\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\int_{0}^{\infty}(H_{r}(e^{t}\theta,e^{-t}\theta)+H_{r}(-e^{t}\theta,-e^{-t}\theta))(e^{t}-e^{-t})dt.

Since the orbital integrals are class functions, there exist two functions
g_{1},g_{2}
in the coordinates
(r,N)
which depend on
h
, are defined everywhere away from the parabola
N=r^{2}
and away from
N=0
(which would correspond to non-invertible matrices).
Moreover, both
{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\spl}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\el}}
can be written in terms of these two smooth functions
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
.
More precisely,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\spl}}(r,N)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{r^{2}}{N}-1\right\rvert}^{-1/2}g_{2}(r,N)\text{ and}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\el}}(r,N)

\displaystyle=g_{1}(r,N)+\frac{1}{2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{r^{2}}{N}-1\right\rvert}^{-1/2}g_{2}(r,N).

Since
h
has compact support, the supports of
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
are relatively compact in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Also,
g_{2}
is supported in the elliptic torus (i.e. inside the parabola).

For consistency in notation, we write the right hand side of both
{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\spl}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{T_{\el}}
as

g_{1}(r,N)+\frac{1}{2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{r^{2}}{N}-1\right\rvert}^{-1/2}g_{2}(r,N),

with the understanding that
g_{1}\equiv 0
outside the parabola.
The singularity of the orbital integral is detected by

\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{r^{2}}{N}-1\right\rvert}^{-1/2}

which diverges as we approach the ‘singular set’ given by the parabola.

Consider the orbital integral
{\mathcal{O}}(h,\gamma)
.
After performing an inner automorphism the centralizer of
\gamma
becomes either
T_{\spl}
or
T_{\el}
.
These automorphisms do not change the class
\ch(\gamma)
.
We conclude that as a function in the
(r,N)
coordinates

(4)

{\mathcal{O}}(h,(r,N))=g_{1}(r,N)+\frac{1}{2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{r^{2}}{N}-1\right\rvert}^{-1/2}g_{2}(r,N).

3.5.2.
Calculations over
{\mathbb{Q}}
: Langlands and Altuğ

The standing assumption that Langlands and Altuğ have is that the function
h
is invariant under
Z_{+}
.
This invariance property is inherited by the orbital integral, and by the functions
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
.
In the
(r,N)
coordinates it means that for positive
\lambda

g_{m}(\lambda r,\lambda^{2}N)=g_{m}(r,N),\ \text{ for }m=1,2.

Setting
\lambda=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N\right\rvert}}}
, (
4
) becomes

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{h}(r,N)

\displaystyle=g_{1}(r,N)+\frac{1}{2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{r^{2}}{N}-1\right\rvert}^{-1/2}g_{2}(r,N)

\displaystyle=g_{1}\left(\frac{r}{\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N\right\rvert}}},\sgn(N)\right)+\frac{1}{2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{r^{2}}{N}-1\right\rvert}^{-1/2}g_{2}\left(\frac{r}{\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N\right\rvert}}},\sgn(N)\right).

Thus, we are led to four functions:
g_{1}(r,1)
,
g_{1}(r,-1)
,
g_{2}(r,1)
and
g_{2}(r,-1)
.
Looking at the lines
N=1
(blue) and
N=-1
(orange) in the
(r,N)
plane in Figure
1
we conclude:

Figure 1.
The case of
K={\mathbb{Q}}
.

(a)

the orbital integrals are functions of the trace and depend only on the four functions
g_{m}(r,\pm 1)
with
m=1,2
.

(b)

the functions
g_{m}(r,-1)
are smooth and of compact support since their support lies entirely on the line
N=-1
which is in the split torus.
On the other hand, the functions
g_{m}(r,1)
are also compactly supported but might not be smooth as they might have singularities as they approach the value
r=\pm 1
, precisely because they change from the elliptic torus to the split one at those points.

3.5.3.
Totally Real Fields

Our archimedean test function is

f_{\infty}=f_{\nu_{1}}\times\cdots\times f_{\nu_{n}},

where
n=[K\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\mathbb{Q}}]
.
For each real place (which we generically denote by)
\nu
, we can perform the analysis as before and conclude the existence of two functions
g_{\nu,1}
and
g_{\nu,2}
in the coordinates
(r,N)
with

{\mathcal{O}}(f_{\nu},(r,N))=g_{\nu,1}(r,N)+\frac{1}{2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{r^{2}}{N}-1\right\rvert}^{-1/2}g_{\nu,2}(r,N).

Picking a unit
u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}
and
\tau\in L(u)
fixes
\gamma
.
We can then write the archimedean orbital integral as

{\mathcal{O}}(f_{\infty},\gamma(\tau,u))=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(g_{\nu_{i},1}(\tau_{i},4u_{i}\varepsilon_{i})+\frac{1}{2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{\tau_{i}^{2}}{4u_{i}\varepsilon_{i}}-1\right\rvert}^{-1/2}g_{\nu_{i},2}(\tau_{i},4u_{i}\varepsilon_{i})\right),

where
\tau_{i},u_{i},\varepsilon_{i}
stand for the embedding of
\tau,u,\varepsilon
into the
i
-th real place via
\sigma_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}K\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
, respectively.
We can conclude this section by recording the following result.

Theorem 3.21
.

The regular elliptic part of the trace formula is

R(f)=\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\in L(u)}\vol(\gamma(\tau,u)){\mathcal{O}}(f_{\fin},\gamma(\tau,u))\times\\
\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(g_{\nu_{i},1}(\tau_{i},4u_{i}\varepsilon_{i})+\frac{1}{2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{\tau_{i}^{2}}{u_{i}\varepsilon_{i}}-1\right\rvert}^{-1/2}g_{\nu_{i},2}(\tau_{i},4u_{i}\varepsilon_{i})\right).

Remark 3.22
.

The functions
g_{\nu_{i},1},g_{\nu_{i},2}
depend only on
f_{\nu_{i}}
.
Contrary to what was done in
[
4
]
, we do not make the change of variables to transform this into a function that only depends on the trace.
This is because
Z_{+}
affects all entries in
the same way
but the change of variable would require a different scaling factor at each place.

Our objective in the next sections is to analytically study these expressions.

3.6.
A partition of
\mathbb{R}^{2n-1}

To make the manipulation clearer at later stages, we partition
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
into different subsets.
Recall from §
3.5
that in the
(r,N)
coordinates the
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
plane is partitioned into different regions by the parabola
r^{2}=N
.
For calculations in this section, we consider the following partition of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Definition 3.23
.

Define the subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by

\displaystyle S_{0}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{(r,N)\mid N=0\mbox{ or }r^{2}-N=0\},

\displaystyle S_{\spl}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{(r,N)\mid N\neq 0\mbox{ and }r^{2}-N>0\},

\displaystyle S_{\ell}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{(r,N)\mid N\neq 0\mbox{ and }r^{2}-N<0\}.

Let
j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\in\{0,{\spl},{\el}\}
and
J=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{n})
.
Define

S_{J}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=S_{j_{1}}\times S_{j_{2}}\times\cdots\times S_{j_{n}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2n}.

Remark 3.24
.

The regions
S_{\el}
and
S_{\spl}
get their subindex notation because they are the images under
\ch
of the elliptic and split torus, respectively.
The partition they form, together with
S_{0}
, is shown in Figure
2
.

S_{\spl}
S_{\el}

Figure 2.
The subsets
S_{j}
of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Remark 3.25
.

Notice that
S_{J}
has measure zero if and only if one of its
j_{i}=0
(i.e. if in one of the coordinates is a set of measure zero).
In particular, the integral of any integrable function over such a set is zero.

Notation
.

Set
G_{\infty}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\GL(2,{\mathbb{R}})\times\cdots\times\GL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
, where there are
n
factors
\GL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
.

Let
z
be a positive real number.
The
Z_{+}
action on
G_{\infty}
is given by

z\cdot(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})=(z\gamma_{1},\dots,z\gamma_{n}).

This action corresponds to the action of
{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
on
(r_{1},N_{1},\ldots,r_{n},N_{n})
given by

z\cdot(r_{1},N_{1},\ldots,r_{n},N_{n})=(zr_{1},z^{2}N_{1},\ldots,zr_{n},z^{2}N_{n}).

In other words, there is a commutative map for every
z>0
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\CH}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\CH}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z\cdot}

The following lemma is straightforward.

Lemma 3.26
.

Keep the notation introduced above.

(i)

Let
j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\in\{0,{\spl},{\el}\}
and
J=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{n})
.
Then
S_{J}
is invariant under the action of
Z_{+}
.

(ii)

The quotient space
Z_{+}\setminus G_{\infty}
is a Lie group with
2^{n}
connected components.

(iii)

Z_{+}\backslash S_{J}
form a partition of
Z_{+}\setminus G_{\infty}
.

3.7.
Splitting behaviour at infinity

We now define the following function:

Definition 3.27
.

Let
\CH\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\ch\otimes\cdots\otimes\ch\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}G_{\infty}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times\cdots\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be given by

\CH(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n})=(r_{1},N_{1},\dots,r_{n},N_{n}).

We now define an equivalence relation in points of
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
that will simplify our computations and explanations significantly.

Definition 3.28
.

Let
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
be two points in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
.
They are said to be
equivalent at infinity
if
v_{1},v_{2}
belong to the same region
S_{J}
.
The notation is

v_{1}\siminf v_{2}.

The point
v\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
is
completely split
at infinity or
not split at infinity
according as
v\in S_{\spl}\times\cdots\times S_{\spl}
or not.

By Lemma
3.26
the equivalence class of
v
under the above equivalence class is well defined on
Z_{+}\backslash G_{\infty}
.
Thus, we extend the definition to the quotient space
Z_{+}\backslash G_{\infty}
.

Finally, we extend the definitions to the regular elliptic matrices.

Definition 3.29
.

Let
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
be two regular elliptic matrices in
\GL(2,K)
.
They are called
equivalent at infinity
when

\CH(\sigma_{1}(\gamma_{1}),\ldots,\sigma_{n}(\gamma_{1}))\siminf\CH(\sigma_{1}(\gamma_{2}),\ldots,\sigma_{n}(\gamma_{2})).

The matrix
\gamma_{1}
is said to be
completely split
or
not split
at infinity according as

\CH(\sigma_{1}(\gamma_{1}),\ldots,\sigma_{n}(\gamma_{1}))

is
completely split
or
not split
at infinity.

The following will be useful later for explicit computations.

Definition 3.30
.

Write
C\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{2n}\longrightarrow\{0,1\}
to be the indicator function of

S_{\spl}\times\cdots\times S_{\spl}.

By abuse of notation, using the fact that this set is
Z_{+}
invariant, we also set
C
to denote the indicator function after quotienting by
Z_{+}
.

We provide an example when
n=2
to get a feeling for the subsets defined here.
We will consider a totally real quadratic field
K
, but we do not impose the condition on totally positive units for this example.

Example 3.31
.

Let
K={\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3})
.
Its two real embeddings are

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}(a+b\sqrt{3})=a+b\sqrt{3},

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}(a+b\sqrt{3})=a-b\sqrt{3}.

This number field has class number
1
; we can pick
k=1
and an ideal
{\mathcal{P}}=(1+2\sqrt{3})
.
This ideal is prime and its norm is
p=13
.
Set
\varepsilon=1+2\sqrt{3}
.

Consider the matrix

\gamma=\begin{pmatrix}3\sqrt{3}&1-3\sqrt{3}\\
1+6\sqrt{3}&1-2\sqrt{3}\\
\end{pmatrix}\in\GL(2,{\mathcal{O}}_{K}).

One can compute

\det(\gamma)=-1-2\sqrt{3}=-\varepsilon.

Thus,
\gamma
will be a contributing matrix as long as it is regular elliptic.
The discriminant of its characteristic polynomial is

\Tr(\gamma)^{2}-4\det(\gamma)=(1+\sqrt{3})^{2}+4(1+2\sqrt{3})=2(4+5\sqrt{3}).

This is not a square because
4+5\sqrt{3}
is a prime in
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3})
relatively prime to
2
.
By Proposition
3.19
, we conclude
\gamma
is a contributing matrix that corresponds to the unit
u=-1
and
\tau=1+\sqrt{3}
.

The trace-determinant coordinates we have defined
(r_{1},N_{1},r_{2},N_{2})
for this matrix are

(1+\sqrt{3},-1-2\sqrt{3},1-\sqrt{3},-1+2\sqrt{3}).

Computing explicitly gives

N_{1}N_{2}=-13.

Recall that
z
is positive by definition, so we conclude that the coordinates of this point in
\{\pm 1\}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
are precisely

(-1)\times(1+\sqrt{3},1-\sqrt{3},-1+2\sqrt{3}).

Let us now understand where does this point lie in the subsets.
We must understand whether the embeddings
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
send the discriminant to positive or negative numbers; this will determine whether the matrix has two distinct real eigenvalues or a complex pair of eigenvalues.
We have

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}(\Tr(\gamma)^{2}-4\det(\gamma))

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}(8+10\sqrt{3})=8+10\sqrt{3}>0,

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}(\Tr(\gamma)^{2}-4\det(\gamma))

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}(8+10\sqrt{3})=8-10\sqrt{3}<0.

Under the first embedding the matrix is split, whereas under the second embedding it is elliptic.
Consequently, this point is in
S_{\spl}\times S_{\el}
.

4.
Manipulation of the Regular Elliptic Part

In this section the goal is to rewrite the expression of the regular elliptic part of the trace formula obtained in Theorem
3.21
in a way so that we can apply the Approximate Functional Equation.
We carry out the manipulation by substituting the volume term with the expression obtained in Proposition
3.14
, the finite orbital integral with the expression obtained in Proposition
3.18
, and finally getting a better handle on the infinite orbital integral by studying the units.

A key difficulty in generalizing Altuğ’s result over
{\mathbb{Q}}
to the general number field case was in handling the units.
The first step towards solving this issue is done by defining an appropriate
interpolation function
in §
4.2
.
It is because of how we define this function that we crucially need to assume that our base field is totally real.

4.1.
The First Simplification

Let
\gamma
be a contributing conjugacy class.
By earlier discussion we know that
\left(\det(\gamma)\right)={\mathfrak{p}}^{k}
.
By the product formula,

\prod_{\nu}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\det(\gamma)\right\rvert}_{\nu}=1.

Since the only finite prime contributing to its factorization is
{\mathfrak{p}}
, we have

\prod_{\nu\mid\infty}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\det(\gamma)\right\rvert}_{\nu}={\mathsf{p}}^{k}.

Given two different complex numbers
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
, recall that we have defined

\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\right\rvert}^{-1/2}=\frac{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\right\rvert}^{1/2}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}\right\rvert}}.

This factor determines the singularity of the orbital integrals.
It is also the inverse of the Weyl discriminant that appears in the Weyl integration formula.

We prove the next result only for totally real number fields.
The proof remains unchanged in the general case, except the need to account for the complex embeddings.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
be the roots of
P_{\gamma}
and define
\partial=\tau^{2}-4\det(\gamma)
.
Then,

{\mathsf{p}}^{k/2}=\sqrt{\Norm_{K}\left(\partial\right)}\prod_{i=1}^{r_{1}}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D\left(\sigma_{i}\left(\lambda_{1}\right),\sigma_{i}\left(\lambda_{2}\right)\right)\right\rvert}^{-1/2},

where
\sigma_{i}
denotes the real embeddings of
K
.

Proof.

Using the product formula and the definition of the norms given by the archimedean valuations,

(5)

\begin{split}{\mathsf{p}}^{k}&=\prod_{\nu\mid\infty}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\det(\gamma)\right\rvert}_{\nu}\\
&=\prod_{i=1}^{r_{1}}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\sigma_{i}(\det(\gamma))\right\rvert}\\
&=\prod_{i=1}^{r_{1}}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\sigma_{i}(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2})\right\rvert}\\
&=\prod_{i=1}^{r_{1}}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\sigma_{i}(\lambda_{1})\sigma_{i}(\lambda_{2})\right\rvert}.\end{split}

Moreover, in
L
we have

\partial=\tau^{2}-4\det(\gamma)=(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2})^{2}-4\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}=(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})^{2}.

Applying any real embedding
\sigma
to
\partial
and taking the absolute value, we deduce

\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\sigma(\partial)\right\rvert}=\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\sigma(\lambda_{1})-\sigma(\lambda_{2})\right\rvert}^{2}.

For a (real) place this implies

\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\sigma(\lambda_{1})\sigma(\lambda_{2})\right\rvert}=D\left(\sigma\left(\lambda_{1}\right),\sigma\left(\lambda_{2}\right)\right)^{-1}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\sigma\left(\partial\right)\right\rvert}.

Substituting this in (
5
), we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathsf{p}}^{k}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{r_{1}}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\sigma_{i}\left(\lambda_{1}\right)\sigma_{i}\left(\lambda_{2}\right)\right\rvert}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{r_{1}}D\left(\sigma_{i}\left(\lambda_{1}\right),\sigma_{i}\left(\lambda_{2}\right)\right)^{-1}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\sigma_{i}(\partial)\right\rvert}

\displaystyle=\Norm_{K}(\partial)\prod_{i=1}^{r_{1}}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D\left(\sigma_{i}\left(\lambda_{1}\right),\sigma_{i}\left(\lambda_{2}\right)\right)\right\rvert}^{-1}.

Since
\partial\in K
, we have used the product formula in
K
for the above calculation.
Taking square root,

{\mathsf{p}}^{k/2}=\sqrt{\Norm_{K}\left(\partial\right)}\prod_{i=1}^{r_{1}}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D\left(\sigma_{i}\left(\lambda_{1}\right),\sigma_{i}\left(\lambda_{2}\right)\right)\right\rvert}^{-1/2}.\qed

4.1.1.

Fix a contributing regular elliptic matrix
\gamma
corresponding to the unit
u
and trace
\tau
.
By Theorem
3.21
, the regular elliptic part of the trace formula can be written as

R(f)=\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\in L(u)}\vol(\gamma(\tau,u)){\mathcal{O}}(f_{\fin},\gamma(\tau,u))\times\\
\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(g_{\nu_{i},1}(\tau_{i},4u_{i}\varepsilon_{i})+\frac{1}{2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{\tau_{i}^{2}}{4u_{i}\varepsilon_{i}}-1\right\rvert}^{-1/2}g_{\nu_{i},2}(\tau_{i},4u_{i}\varepsilon_{i})\right).

Substituting the values we have obtained Proposition
3.14
, the above expression equals

\kappa\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\in L(u)}L(1,\chi_{\gamma})\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{L}\right\rvert}}{\mathcal{O}}(f_{\fin},\gamma(\tau,u))\times\\
\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(g_{\nu_{i},1}(\tau_{i},4u_{i}\varepsilon_{i})+\frac{1}{2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{\tau_{i}^{2}}{4u_{i}\varepsilon_{i}}-1\right\rvert}^{-1/2}g_{\nu_{i},2}(\tau_{i},4u_{i}\varepsilon_{i})\right).

We will now rewrite
\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{L}\right\rvert}}
in a way that will be more useful.
Recall that

\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{L}\right\rvert}}=\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}\sqrt{\Norm_{K}\left(\Delta_{\gamma}\right)}.

Using the definition
\Delta_{\gamma}S_{\gamma}^{2}=(\partial)
and Proposition
4.1
, we conclude

\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{L}\right\rvert}}=\frac{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}\sqrt{\Norm_{K}\left(\partial\right)}}{\Norm_{K}\left(S_{\gamma}\right)}=\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{\frac{k}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}D\left(\sigma_{i}\left(\lambda_{1}\right),\sigma_{i}\left(\lambda_{2}\right)\right)^{1/2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}}{\Norm_{K}\left(S_{\gamma}\right)}.

Hence, each term arising in the regular elliptic part of the trace formula is of the form

{\mathsf{p}}^{\frac{k}{2}}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}\frac{L(1,\chi_{\gamma}){\mathcal{O}}(f_{\fin},\gamma(\tau,u))\prod_{i=1}^{n}D\left(\sigma_{i}\left(\lambda_{1}\right),\sigma_{i}\left(\lambda_{2}\right)\right)^{1/2}}{\Norm_{K}\left(S_{\gamma}\right)}\times\\
\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(g_{\nu_{i},1}(\tau_{i},4u_{i}\varepsilon_{i})+\frac{1}{2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{\tau_{i}^{2}}{4u_{i}\varepsilon_{i}}-1\right\rvert}^{-1/2}g_{\nu_{i},2}(\tau_{i},4u_{i}\varepsilon_{i})\right).

4.1.2.

For a fixed unit
u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}
, we now study

\prod_{i=1}^{n}D\left(\sigma_{i}\left(\lambda_{1}\right),\sigma_{i}\left(\lambda_{2}\right)\right)^{1/2}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(g_{\nu_{i},1}(\tau_{i},4u_{i}\varepsilon_{i})+\frac{1}{2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{\tau_{i}^{2}}{4u_{i}\varepsilon_{i}}-1\right\rvert}^{-1/2}g_{\nu_{i},2}(\tau_{i},4u_{i}\varepsilon_{i})\right).

Recall that
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
are the roots of
P_{\gamma}(X)
.
Notice that by definition

\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D\left(\sigma_{i}\left(\lambda_{1}\right),\sigma_{i}\left(\lambda_{2}\right)\right)\right\rvert}^{1/2}=2\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{\tau_{i}^{2}}{4u_{i}\varepsilon_{i}}-1\right\rvert}^{1/2}.

By substituting these terms and by putting
D\left(\sigma_{i}\left(\lambda_{1}\right),\sigma_{i}\left(\lambda_{2}\right)\right)^{1/2}
with the
i
-th real orbital integral, we deduce that

R(f)=\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\in L(u)}{\mathsf{p}}^{\frac{k}{2}}\frac{L(1,\chi_{\gamma}){\mathcal{O}}(f_{\fin},\gamma(\tau,u))}{\Norm_{K}\left(S_{\gamma}\right)}\times\\
\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(2\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{\tau_{i}^{2}}{4u_{i}\varepsilon_{i}}-1\right\rvert}^{1/2}g_{\nu_{i},1}(\tau_{i},4u_{i}\varepsilon_{i})+g_{\nu_{i},2}(\tau_{i},4u_{i}\varepsilon_{i})\right).

4.2.
Interpolation function
\theta^{\pm}

We begin by defining two maps: the first will allow us to handle the traces
\tau
in the regular elliptic part of the trace formula, while the second will give a handle on the units
u
.

Define the map

\displaystyle T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}K

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(\sigma_{1}(\alpha),\dots,\sigma_{n}(\alpha)\right).

We know that
T({\mathcal{O}}_{K})
is a lattice in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
By Dirichlet’s Unit Theorem (for a totally real field) we have,

{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}\simeq\{\pm 1\}\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{n-1}.

Let
W\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be the
n-1
-dimensional subspace with

x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}=0.

The embedding
S\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is given by

S(x)=(\log{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert x\right\rvert}_{1}},\dots,\log{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert x\right\rvert}_{n}}),

where
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert x\right\rvert}_{i}
is the absolute value with respect to the
i
-th archimedean place.
Let us fix once and for all a system of fundamental units
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n-1}
in
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}
that realizes the above isomorphism.
For all
1\leq i\leq n-1
, we see from the proof of the Dirichlet unit theorem that
S(\beta_{i})
is a basis for a lattice in
W
.
We write the coordinates of
W
with respect to these generators as
y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n-1})
by which we mean that a unit
u\in{\mathcal{O}}^{*}_{K}
can be written as

u=\pm\beta_{1}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1}^{y_{n-1}}.

To proceed with the calculations, we are required to make an assumption here, namely that the system of fundamental units is ‘totally positive’.

(Hyp-pos)

Let
K
be a totally real number field that has a totally positive system of fundamental units.

Recall that a system of fundamental units is called totally positive if
T(\beta_{i})>0
for all
1\leq i\leq n-1
.
This is essentially the only place in the paper where we require that the base field
K
is totally real.
If
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
is a totally real
quadratic field
then it was observed by H. Stark that a density of
100\%
have a system of totally positive fundamental units; see
[
17
, p. 684]
.
When
[K\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\mathbb{Q}}]>2
, there are fewer fields satisfying this property, but such fields exist.

Definition 4.2
.

Define the
interpolation function

\theta^{\pm}
from
{\mathbb{R}}^{2n-1}
to
{\mathbb{C}}
as

\theta^{\pm}(r_{1},\ldots,r_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n-1})=\\
{\mathsf{p}}^{\frac{k}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(2\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{r_{i}^{2}}{\pm 4\beta_{1,i}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,i}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{i}}-1\right\rvert}^{1/2}g_{\nu_{i},1}(r_{i},\pm 4\beta_{1,i}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,i}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{i})+g_{\nu_{i},2}(r_{i},\pm 4\beta_{1,i}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,i}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{i})\right),

where
\beta_{t,i}=\sigma_{i}(\beta_{t})
and the
\varepsilon_{i}=\sigma_{i}(\varepsilon)
.

The interpolation function is only defined on a dense subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2n-1}
.
However, for succinctness of exposition we say that
Moreover, its support is relatively compact in
\mathbb{R}^{2n-1}
because the
g_{\nu,j}
have relatively compact support in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Notation
.

To simplify notation, we follow the following conventions:

•

we write
\theta^{\pm}(r,y)
instead of
\theta^{\pm}(r_{1},\ldots,r_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n-1})
.

•

for
y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n-1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
define
\beta^{y}=\beta_{1}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1}^{y_{n-1}}
.
The units of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
are
\pm\beta^{y}
.

•

we continue to write
u
to denote a unit in
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}
with the understanding that if we are evaluating the function
\theta^{\pm}(x,y)
we are referring to its
\beta
-component under the isomorphism induced by the Dirichlet unit theorem.

•

we identify
\tau
and
T(\tau)
.

The regular elliptic part of the trace formula can then be written as

\displaystyle R(f)

\displaystyle=\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\in L(u)}\frac{L(1,\chi_{\gamma}){\mathcal{O}}(f_{\fin},\gamma(\tau,u))}{\Norm_{K}\left(S_{\gamma}\right)}\theta^{\pm}(\tau,u)

\displaystyle=\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{\beta}\sum_{\tau\in L(u)}\frac{L(1,\chi_{\gamma}){\mathcal{O}}(f_{\fin},\gamma(\tau,u))}{\Norm_{K}\left(S_{\gamma}\right)}\theta^{\pm}(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n-1}).

Note that
\tau\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
can be viewed as an element of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, so it makes sense to set
r=\tau
as an input for
\theta^{\pm}
.
On the other hand, when we write
y=u
, we mean that
y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n-1})
corresponding to the unit
u=\beta^{y}
.

We wish to perform Poisson summation on the variables
(\tau,u)
; but we need to address the following issues:

(a)

the function
\theta^{\pm}
has singularities.

(b)

the lattice on which we will perform the Poisson Summation lives in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2n-1}
; but it is unclear if the factor
\frac{L(1,\chi_{\gamma}){\mathcal{O}}(f_{\fin},\gamma(\tau,u))}{\Norm_{K}\left(S_{\gamma}\right)}
in front of the interpolation function
\theta^{\pm}
– which should be treated as an arithmetic weight – can itself be interpolated by a smooth function in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2n-1}
.

(c)

L(u)
is not a lattice.

This will be done in §
6
and §
7
.

We conclude this section with the following result

Theorem 4.3
.

With notation and assumptions introduced so far, the regular elliptic part of
G(K)
can be written as

R(f)=\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\in L(u)}\theta^{\pm}(\tau,u)L(1,\chi_{\gamma})\left(\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})}\prod_{{\mathfrak{p}}\mid(S_{\gamma}/{\mathfrak{d}})}\left(1-\frac{\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{p}})}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{p}})}\right)\right).

Proof.

This follows from substituting Theorem
3.18
into our previous expansion of the regular elliptic part.
∎

In the next part, we carry out a detailed study of the sum over incomplete
L
-functions and the sum over the finite orbital integrals via the
Approximate Functional Equation
.

5.
The Approximate Functional Equation

Let
z
be a complex number and consider a non-square integer
\delta\equiv 0,1\pmod{4}
.
In this setting, the
Zagier zeta function
introduced in
[
41
]
is defined as follows

L(z,\delta)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sum_{d^{2}\mid\delta}^{\prime}\frac{1}{d^{2z-1}}L\left(z,\binom{\frac{\delta}{d^{2}}}{\cdot}_{K}\right).

Here, we use the notation
\binom{\frac{\delta}{d^{2}}}{\cdot}_{K}
to denote the Kronecker symbol.
The sum is taken only over those
d
such that
\delta/d^{2}\equiv 0,1\pmod{4}
.
This congruence condition is indicated by the
\prime
on top of the summation sign.

The Zagier zeta function has the following two pivotal roles in
[
4
]
:

(a)

it recovers the values of the finite orbital integrals when evaluated at
z=1
.

(b)

an appropriate completion of the Zagier zeta function satisfies a functional equation to which it is possible to apply the approximate functional equation.

This allows us to fix the issues preventing us from performing Poisson summation.

Remark 5.1
.

For a general number field
F
, Arthur conjectures in
[
9
, §3]
that the generalization of the Zagier zeta function for GL
(n,F)
is obtained via the work of Z. Yun, see
[
40
]
.
This is verified by the second named author in
[
19
]
in the case
n=2
.
We make use of this construction below.
For more details about the Zagier zeta function over a general number field, we refer the reader to
[
19
]
.

5.1.
Multiplicative formula of Langlands

In this section, we define the Zagier zeta function for a totally real number field and prove a functional equation.
As before, let
\chi_{\gamma}
be the quadratic sign character associated to the extension generated by the regular elliptic element
\gamma
.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
K
be a totally real number field
4
4

4

The definition does not require that
K
is totally real and can be made for all number fields.
and let
z
be a complex parameter with
\Re(z)>1
.
Set

{\mathcal{O}}(z,\gamma)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Norm_{K}(S_{\gamma})^{z}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{(1-2z)}\prod_{{\mathfrak{q}}\mid{\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime}}\left(1-\frac{\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{q}})}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{q}})^{z}}\right),

where
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{S_{\gamma}}{{\mathfrak{d}}}
for any ideal
{\mathfrak{d}}
such that
{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}
.
Writing
L(z,\chi_{\gamma})
for the Hecke
L
-function of the character
\chi_{\gamma}
, define

L(z,\gamma)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=L(z,\chi_{\gamma})\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{(1-2z)}\prod_{{\mathfrak{q}}\mid{\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime}}\left(1-\frac{\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{q}})}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{q}})^{z}}\right).

This is the
(generalized) multiplicative formula of Langlands
for
\GL(2,K)
.
Next, we define the completion of
{\mathcal{O}}(z,\gamma)
as

{\Lambda}(z,\gamma)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Lambda(z,\chi_{\gamma}){\mathcal{O}}(z,\gamma)

where
\Lambda(z,\chi_{\gamma})
is defined below.
This is the
(generalized) completed multiplicative formula of Langlands
for
\GL(2,K)
.

For a totally real field
K
, the completed Dedekind zeta function is defined as

\xi_{K}(z)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{z/2}L_{{\mathbb{R}}}(z)^{n}\zeta_{K}(z).

We define

\Lambda(z,\chi_{\gamma})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{z/2}\Norm(\Delta_{\gamma})^{z/2}L(z,\chi_{\gamma})\prod_{\nu\mid\infty}L_{\mathbb{R}}(z+\delta_{\nu,\gamma}).

Also, the
L
-factors at the archimedean places for
\chi_{\gamma}
are given by

L_{\nu}(z,\chi_{\gamma})=L_{{\mathbb{R}}}(z+\delta_{\nu,\gamma})=\pi^{(-z+\delta_{\nu,\gamma})/2}\Gamma((z+\delta_{\nu,\gamma})/2)

with
\delta_{\nu,\gamma}=1
if
\chi_{\gamma}
is ramified at
\nu
and 0 otherwise.

We first show that
\Lambda(z,\gamma)
behaves nicely.
More precisely,

Theorem 5.3
.

The completed multiplicative formula of Langlands
{\Lambda}(z,\gamma)
, admits an analytic continuation to an entire function and satisfies the functional equation

{\Lambda}(1-z,\gamma)={\Lambda}(z,\gamma).

Proof.

Writing
\mu(\cdot)
to denote the Möbius function of an ideal of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
, we have

(6)

\prod_{{\mathfrak{q}}\mid{\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime}}\left(1-\frac{\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{q}})}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{q}})^{s}}\right)=\sum_{{\mathfrak{r}}\mid{\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime}}\frac{\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{r}})\mu({\mathfrak{r}})}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{r}})^{s}}.

On the left hand side, the primes are not repeated in the product.
So, we only obtain terms with different prime factors whose sign depends on the number of such factors - this is controlled by the Möbius function on the right.

Substituting (
6
) into definition of the completed multiplicative formula yields,

\displaystyle{\Lambda}(z,\gamma)

\displaystyle=\Norm_{K}(S_{\gamma})^{z}\Lambda(z,\chi_{\gamma})\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2z-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{r}}\mid{\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime}}\frac{\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{r}})\mu({\mathfrak{r}})}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{r}})^{z}}

\displaystyle=\Norm_{K}(S_{\gamma})^{z}\Lambda(z,\chi_{\gamma})\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma},{\mathfrak{r}}\mid{\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2z-1}}\frac{\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{r}})\mu({\mathfrak{r}})}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{r}})^{z}}

\displaystyle=\Norm_{K}(S_{\gamma})^{z}\Lambda(z,\chi_{\gamma})\sum_{{\mathfrak{r}}\mid S_{\gamma},{\mathfrak{d}}\mid{\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2z-1}}\frac{\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{r}})\mu({\mathfrak{r}})}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{r}})^{z}}\qquad\text{ where }{\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime}=\frac{S_{\gamma}}{{\mathfrak{r}}}

\displaystyle=\Norm_{K}(S_{\gamma})^{z}\Lambda(z,\chi_{\gamma})\sum_{{\mathfrak{r}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{r}})\mu({\mathfrak{r}})}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{r}})^{z}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid{\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2z-1}}

\displaystyle=\Norm_{K}(S_{\gamma})^{z}\Lambda(z,\chi_{\gamma})\sum_{{\mathfrak{r}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{r}})\mu({\mathfrak{r}})}{\Norm_{K}(S_{\gamma})^{z}}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime})^{z}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid{\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2z-1}}

\displaystyle=\Lambda(z,\chi_{\gamma})\sum_{{\mathfrak{r}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{r}})\mu({\mathfrak{r}})}{\sqrt{\Norm_{K}(S_{\gamma})}}\sqrt{\Norm_{K}(S_{\gamma})}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime})^{z}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid{\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2z-1}}

\displaystyle=\Lambda(z,\chi_{\gamma})\Norm_{K}(S_{\gamma})^{1/2}\sum_{{\mathfrak{r}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{r}})\mu({\mathfrak{r}})}{\sqrt{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{r}}{\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime})}}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime})^{z}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid{\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2z-1}}

\displaystyle=\Lambda(z,\chi_{\gamma})\Norm_{K}(S_{\gamma})^{1/2}\sum_{{\mathfrak{r}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{r}})\mu({\mathfrak{r}})}{\sqrt{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{r}})}}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime})^{z-1/2}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid{\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2z-1}}.

Shifting our focus to the term

\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime})^{z-1/2}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid{\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2z-1}},

we see it remains unchanged when we replace
z
by
1-z
.
Indeed

\displaystyle\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime})^{(1-z)-1/2}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid{\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2(1-z)-1}}

\displaystyle=\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime})^{1/2-z}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid{\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{1-2z}}

\displaystyle=\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime})^{1/2-z}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid{\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}\left(\frac{{\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime}}{{\mathfrak{d}}}\right)^{1-2z}}

\displaystyle=\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime})^{1/2-z-(1-2z)}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid{\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2z-1}}

\displaystyle=\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime})^{z-1/2}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid{\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2z-1}}.

Hence, when we substitute
1-z
for
z
in the whole expression we obtain

\displaystyle{\Lambda}(1-z,\gamma)

\displaystyle=\Lambda(1-z,\chi_{\gamma})\Norm_{K}(S_{\gamma})^{1/2}\sum_{{\mathfrak{r}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{\chi({\mathfrak{r}})\mu({\mathfrak{r}})}{\sqrt{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{r}})}}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime})^{(1-z)-1/2}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid{\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2(1-z)-1}}

\displaystyle=\Lambda(z,\chi_{\gamma})\Norm_{K}(S_{\gamma})^{1/2}\sum_{{\mathfrak{r}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{\chi({\mathfrak{r}})\mu({\mathfrak{r}})}{\sqrt{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{r}})}}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime})^{z-1/2}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid{\mathfrak{r}}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2z-1}}

\displaystyle={\Lambda}(z,{\gamma}).

This manipulation required using the functional equation for the primitive quadratic Hecke character.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

Theorem
5.3
is an adaptation of
[
36
, Equations (4), (5), and Lemma 2.1]
, where they work over
\mathbb{Z}
.

The following result connects this construction with our work on the regular elliptic part of the trace formula.

Proposition 5.5
.

With notation as introduced above,

{\mathcal{O}}(f_{\fin},\gamma)={\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}{\mathcal{O}}(1,\gamma).

Proof.

This is a reformulation of Proposition
3.18
.
∎

We now define certain quadratic characters that arise naturally in our calculations.

Definition 5.6
.

Let
{\mathfrak{d}}
be an ideal dividing
S_{\gamma}
.
Define an
imprimitive character

\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}
, obtained from
\chi_{\gamma}
, by setting
\chi({\mathfrak{q}})=0
for any
{\mathfrak{q}}\mid\frac{S_{\gamma}}{{\mathfrak{d}}}
.
Associated to this imprimitive quadratic Hecke character is an
L
-function

L(z,\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}})=L(z,\chi_{\gamma})\prod_{{\mathfrak{q}}\mid\frac{S_{\gamma}}{{\mathfrak{d}}}}\left(1-\frac{\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{q}})}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{q}})^{z}}\right),

where
L(z,\chi_{\gamma})
is the Hecke
L
-function associated to the quadratic character
\chi_{\gamma}
.

Notice that

(7)

L(z,\gamma)=L(z,\chi_{\gamma})\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2z-1}}\prod_{{\mathfrak{p}}\mid(S_{\gamma}/{\mathfrak{d}})}\left(1-\frac{\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{p}})}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{p}})^{z}}\right)=\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2z-1}}L(z,\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}).

Recall from the expression of
R(f)
obtained in Theorem
4.3
the presence of the factor

L(1,\chi_{\gamma})\left(\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})}\prod_{{\mathfrak{p}}\mid(S_{\gamma}/{\mathfrak{d}})}\left(1-\frac{\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{p}})}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{p}})}\right)\right),

which is precisely
L(1,\gamma)
.
We now rewrite Theorem
4.3
as follows.

Proposition 5.7
.

The regular elliptic part of the trace formula is

R(f)=\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\in L(u)}\theta^{\pm}(\tau,u)L(1,\gamma).

Remark 5.8
.

For
K=\mathbb{Q}
the above decomposition becomes

\sum_{\pm}\sum_{\tau\in L(\pm 1)}\theta^{\pm}(\tau)L(1,\tau^{2}\pm 4p^{k}),

which corresponds to
[
4
, (unnumbered) equation at the start of §4]
.
An important deviation from the work of Altuğ is that in order to define
L(z,\tau^{2}\pm 4p^{k})
, he uses a sum which has some congruence condition as opposed to the condition
d\mid S_{\gamma}
.
We will discuss this at length in §
7.1
.

When
K={\mathbb{Q}}
, the sum over
\pm
comes from the Dirichlet unit theorem (but there is no
\beta
-sum, i.e. sum over fundamental units).
In the general case, we have to deal with the extra
\beta
-sum.
The factor
{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}
that we have but Altuğ does not, is just a manifestation of the slight difference between our interpolation functions.

Another fundamental difference is that
\chi_{d}
coincides with the Kronecker symbol which is well-understood and can be easily computed in
\mathbb{Q}
.
For a general number field, calculating the symbols is non-trivial, which we explore in §
7.2
.

5.2.
Statement and Proof of Approximate Functional Equation

5.2.1.

The approximate functional equation requires the use of a smooth cut-off function.
While it is not necessary to specify this function precisely, it is possible for us to do so as in
[
4
]
.
For example, consider the smooth function

(8)

F(x)=\frac{1}{2K_{0}(2)}\int_{x}^{\infty}e^{-y-1/y}\frac{dy}{y}\textrm{ where }x>0,

where
K_{0}
is the zero-th Bessel function of the second kind.
We know from
[
23
, p. 257]
that
0<F(x)<\frac{e^{-x}}{2K_{0}(2)}
.

Define its Mellin transform,

\widetilde{F}(z)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}F(x)x^{z}\frac{dx}{x}.

Moreover, the following bound will be required.

Lemma 5.9
.

The Mellin transform
\widetilde{F}(z)
is meromorphic with a unique simple pole at
z=0
with residue
1
.
Furthermore, it is odd and writing
z=\sigma+it
, the implied constant in the following bound is absolute

\widetilde{F}(z)\ll\mathinner{\!\left\lvert z\right\rvert}^{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert}-1}e^{-\frac{\pi\mathinner{\!\left\lvert t\right\rvert}}{2}}.

Proof.

This follows from the properties of
F_{1}
in
[
23
, pp. 257–258]
.
Note that the residue of
\widetilde{F}_{1}(z)
at
z=0
is
1
, and the bounds
[
23
, (10.57)]
also hold for
\widetilde{F}
.
∎

5.2.2.

We are now in a position to state and prove the
Approximate Functional Equation
.

As in §
5.1
, we need to work with the
L
-factors at the infinite places which are defined in terms of local Gamma factors; see,
[
34
, Lecture 2]
or
[
23
, Equation (5.3)]
.

Notation
.

Let
z
be a complex number and define

L_{\infty}(z,\chi_{\gamma})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\prod_{\nu\mid\infty}L_{\mathbb{R}}(z+\delta_{\nu,\gamma})

where
\delta_{\nu,\gamma}=1
if
\chi_{\gamma}
is ramified at
\nu
and 0 otherwise.

Theorem 5.10

(Approximate Functional Equation)
.

Let
\gamma
be a regular elliptic element and let
\chi_{\gamma}
denote the quadratic sign character associated the quadratic extension
L/K
generated by
\gamma
.
Let
F
be a smooth function as defined above.
For any
y\in{\mathbb{R}}
, further define
5
5

5

In this definition (and later on in the proof), note that
u
is a choice of variable and is not to be confused with the unit
u
, which does not appear in this section.

(9)

H_{\gamma}(z,y)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Re(u)=1}y^{-u}\widetilde{F}(u)\frac{L_{\infty}(1-z+u,\chi_{\gamma})}{L_{\infty}(z-u,\chi_{\gamma})}du.

Let
A
be any positive real number.
Then
L(z,{\gamma})
equals

\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}\left({\mathfrak{d}}\right)^{2z-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}{({\mathfrak{a}})}}{\Norm_{K}\left({\mathfrak{a}}\right)^{z}}F\left(\frac{\Norm_{K}\left({\mathfrak{d}}\right)^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}{A}\right)\\
+\left(\Norm_{K}\left(S_{\gamma}\right)\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}\Norm_{K}(\Delta_{\gamma})^{1/2}\right)^{1-2z}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}\left({\mathfrak{d}}\right)^{1-2z}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\left({\mathfrak{a}}\right)}{\Norm_{K}\left({\mathfrak{a}}\right)^{1-z}}H_{\gamma}\left(z,\frac{\Norm_{K}\left({\mathfrak{d}}\right)^{2}\Norm_{K}\left({\mathfrak{a}}\right)A}{\Norm_{K}\left(S_{\gamma}\right)^{2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}\Norm_{K}(\Delta_{\gamma})}\right),

where the sum over
{\mathfrak{a}}
runs over all ideals of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
.

Proof.

Fix
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
and a positive real number
\sigma
such that
\sigma+\Re(z)>1
and
\Re(z)-\sigma<0
.
Define the integral

(10)

I\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Re(u)=\sigma}{L}(z+u,\gamma)\widetilde{F}(u)A^{u}du.

By the choice of
\sigma
, this integral is absolutely convergent.
By equation (
7
),

\displaystyle{L}(z+u,\gamma)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2z+2u-1}}L(z+u,\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2z+2u-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{z+u}}.

The last equality follows since we are in the half-plane of absolute convergence of the Dirichlet series for each
\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}
.
Substituting this in (
10
) gives

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Re(u)=\sigma}{L}(z+u,\gamma)\widetilde{F}(u)A^{u}du

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Re(u)=\sigma}\left(\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2z+2u-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{z+u}}\right)\widetilde{F}(u)A^{u}du

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Re(u)=\sigma}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2z+2u-1}}\frac{\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{z+u}}\widetilde{F}(u)A^{u}du

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\int_{\Re(u)=\sigma}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2z+2u-1}}\frac{\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{z+u}}\widetilde{F}(u)A^{u}du

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2z-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}{({\mathfrak{a}})}}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{z}}\int_{\Re(u)=\sigma}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2u}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{u}}\widetilde{F}(u)A^{u}du

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2z-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}{({\mathfrak{a}})}}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{z}}\int_{\Re(u)=\sigma}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{-2u}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{-u}\widetilde{F}(u)A^{u}du.

It is possible to interchange the order of integration as the integrand is absolutely convergent.
Using the Mellin inversion formula for the last integral gives

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Re(u)=\sigma}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{-2u}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{-u}\widetilde{F}(u)A^{u}du=F\left(\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}{A}\right).

Hence,

I=\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2z-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}{({\mathfrak{a}})}}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{z}}F\left(\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}{A}\right).

Shifting the contour from
\Re(u)=\sigma
to
\Re(u)=\sigma^{\prime}
for any
\sigma^{\prime}<0
, we get a residue from the pole of
\widetilde{F}(u)
at
u=0
.
This pole is simple, so the residue is

\lim_{u\rightarrow 0}u\widetilde{F}(u)
.
We deduce the equality

I=L(z,\gamma)+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Re(u)=\sigma^{\prime}}{L}(z+u,\gamma)\widetilde{F}(u)A^{u}du,

or equivalently, writing
I^{\prime}
to denote the integral running over
\sigma^{\prime}
instead of
\sigma
, we have that

{L}(z,\gamma)=I-I^{\prime}.

Next we evaluate
I^{\prime}
.
Making the change of variables
u\rightarrow-u
and using that
\widetilde{F}
is odd

\displaystyle I^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Re(u)=\sigma^{\prime}}{L}(z-u,\gamma)\widetilde{F}(-u)A^{-u}du

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Re(u)=\sigma^{\prime}}{L}(z-u,\gamma)\widetilde{F}(u)A^{-u}du.

Evaluating
{L}(z,\gamma)
at
1-(z-u)
, a point in the half plane of absolute convergence, we obtain the Dirichlet series

{L}(1-z+u,\gamma)=\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{1-2z+2u}}L(1-z+u,\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}).

Substituting this into
I^{\prime}
and using the functional equation of the completed Zagier zeta function,

\displaystyle-I^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Re(u)=\sigma^{\prime}}{L}(z-u,\gamma)\widetilde{F}(u)A^{-u}du.

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Re(u)=\sigma^{\prime}}\Norm_{K}(S_{\gamma})^{1-2(z-u)}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{(1-2(z-u))/2}\Norm_{K}(\Delta_{\gamma})^{(1-2(z-u))/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 144.54pt\times\frac{L_{\infty}(1-z+u,\chi_{\gamma})}{L_{\infty}(z-u,\chi_{\gamma})}{L}(1-z+u,\gamma)\widetilde{F}(u)A^{-u}du

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Re(u)=\sigma^{\prime}}\Norm_{K}(S_{\gamma})^{1-2(z-u)}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{(1-2(z-u))/2}\Norm_{K}(\Delta_{\gamma})^{(1-2(z-u))/2}\frac{L_{\infty}(1-z+u,\chi_{\gamma})}{L_{\infty}(z-u,\chi_{\gamma})}

\displaystyle\hskip 158.99377pt\times\widetilde{F}(u)A^{-u}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{1-2z+2u}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{1-z+u}}du

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Re(u)=\sigma^{\prime}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{\Norm_{K}(S_{\gamma})^{1-2(z-u)}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{(1-2(z-u))/2}\Norm_{K}(\Delta_{\gamma})^{(1-2(z-u))/2}\widetilde{F}(u)A^{-u}}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{1-2z+2u}}

\displaystyle\hskip 216.81pt\times\frac{L_{\infty}(1-z+u,\chi_{\gamma})}{L_{\infty}(z-u,\chi_{\gamma})}\frac{\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{1-z+u}}du.

As the Dirichlet series converges absolutely and uniformly in a half-plane away from the abscissa of convergence, we may interchange the order of integration.
Shifting the integral to
\sigma=1
since there are no poles in between,

\displaystyle-I^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\int_{\Re(u)=\sigma^{\prime}}\frac{\Norm_{K}(S_{\gamma})^{1-2(z-u)}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{(1-2(z-u))/2}\Norm_{K}(\Delta_{\gamma})^{(1-2(z-u))/2}\widetilde{F}(u)A^{-u}}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{1-2z+2u}}

\displaystyle\hskip 216.81pt\times\frac{L_{\infty}(1-z+u,\chi_{\gamma})}{L_{\infty}(z-u,\chi_{\gamma})}\frac{\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{1-z+u}}du

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\Norm_{K}\left(S_{\gamma}\right)\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}\Norm_{K}(\Delta_{\gamma})^{1/2}\right)^{1-2z}}{2\pi i}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}\left({\mathfrak{d}}\right)^{1-2z}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{1-z}}

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt\times\int_{\Re(u)=\sigma^{\prime}}\frac{\Norm_{K}(S_{\gamma})^{2u}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{u}\Norm_{K}(\Delta_{\gamma})^{u}\widetilde{F}(u)A^{-u}}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2u}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{u}}\frac{L_{\infty}(1-z+u,\chi_{\gamma})}{L_{\infty}(z-u,\chi_{\gamma})}du.

Collecting terms inside the last integral, it is possible to write it as

\int_{\Re(u)=1}\left(\frac{\Norm_{K}\left({\mathfrak{d}}\right)^{2}\Norm_{K}\left({\mathfrak{a}}\right)A}{\Norm_{K}\left(S_{\gamma}\right)^{2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}\Norm_{K}(\Delta_{\gamma})}\right)^{-u}\frac{L_{\infty}\left(1-z+u,\chi_{\gamma}\right)}{L_{\infty}\left(z-u,\chi_{\gamma}\right)}\widetilde{F}\left(u\right)du.

This motivates the definition in the theorem

H_{\gamma}(z,y)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Re(u)=1}y^{-u}\widetilde{F}(u)\frac{L_{\infty}(1-z+u,\chi_{\gamma})}{L_{\infty}(z-u,\chi_{\gamma})}du.

Therefore, we have proven

-I^{\prime}=\left(\Norm_{K}\left(S_{\gamma}\right)\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}\Norm_{K}(\Delta_{\gamma})^{1/2}\right)^{1-2z}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}\left({\mathfrak{d}}\right)^{1-2z}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\left({\mathfrak{a}}\right)}{\Norm_{K}\left({\mathfrak{a}}\right)^{1-z}}H_{\gamma}\left(z,\frac{\Norm_{K}\left({\mathfrak{d}}\right)^{2}\Norm_{K}\left({\mathfrak{a}}\right)A}{\Norm_{K}\left(S_{\gamma}\right)^{2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}\Norm_{K}(\Delta_{\gamma})}\right).

Hence, putting together our evaluations of
I
and
I^{\prime}
we get

\begin{split}&{L}(z,\gamma)=\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}\left({\mathfrak{d}}\right)^{2z-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}{({\mathfrak{a}})}}{\Norm_{K}\left({\mathfrak{a}}\right)^{z}}F\left(\frac{\Norm_{K}\left({\mathfrak{d}}\right)^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}{A}\right)\\
&+\left(\Norm_{K}\left(S_{\gamma}\right)\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}\Norm_{K}(\Delta_{\gamma})^{1/2}\right)^{1-2z}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}\left({\mathfrak{d}}\right)^{1-2z}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\left({\mathfrak{a}}\right)}{\Norm_{K}\left({\mathfrak{a}}\right)^{1-z}}H_{\gamma}\left(z,\frac{\Norm_{K}\left({\mathfrak{d}}\right)^{2}\Norm_{K}\left({\mathfrak{a}}\right)A}{\Norm_{K}\left(S_{\gamma}\right)^{2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}\Norm_{K}(\Delta_{\gamma})}\right).\end{split}

∎

5.3.
The function
H_{(r,y)}

The following result connects the archimedean behavior of the quadratic character
\chi_{\gamma}
with the regions we defined in §
3.6
.

Theorem 5.11
.

Let
\gamma\in\GL(2,K)
be a regular elliptic matrix and
\nu
be a real place of
K
.
Then,
\chi_{\gamma}
is unramified at
\nu
if and only if
(r_{\nu},N_{\nu})\in S_{\spl}
.

Notation
.

In this proof we write
\chi
in place of
\chi_{\gamma}
for ease of notation.

Proof.

Let
\nu
denote a real place of
K
and let
K_{\nu}={\mathbb{R}}
be the completion.
Let
\tilde{\nu}
be some (and hence any) extension of
\nu
to
K_{\gamma}
,
and let
\rho_{\gamma,\nu}\colon K_{\nu}^{*}\rightarrow\Gal(K_{\gamma,\tilde{\nu}}/K_{\nu})

be the local Artin map, so that
\chi_{\nu}
is the character of
K_{\nu}^{*}
attached to

K_{\gamma,\tilde{\nu}}/K_{\nu}
.
Recall that

\displaystyle\chi\textrm{ is unramified at }\nu

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\chi_{\nu}(-1)=1\textrm{ by definition}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\rho_{\gamma,\nu}(-1)=1

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow-1\in\ker(\rho_{\gamma,\nu})=\Norm_{K_{\gamma,\tilde{\nu}}/K_{\nu}}(K_{\gamma,\tilde{\nu}}^{*})\textrm{ by the local reciprocity law.}

We have
\Norm_{K_{\gamma,\tilde{\nu}}/K_{\nu}}(K_{\gamma,\tilde{\nu}}^{*})={\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
if
\nu
ramifies (i.e.
K_{\gamma,\tilde{\nu}}={\mathbb{C}}
) and
\Norm_{K_{\gamma,\tilde{\nu}}/K_{\nu}}(K_{\gamma,\tilde{\nu}}^{*})={\mathbb{R}}^{*}
if
\nu
splits
(i.e.
K_{\gamma,\tilde{\nu}}={\mathbb{R}}
).
Thus,
\chi
is unramified at
\nu
if and only if
K_{\gamma,\tilde{\nu}}={\mathbb{R}}
.
We know that
K_{\gamma,\tilde{\nu}}=K_{\nu}\!\left(\sqrt{\tau_{\nu}^{2}-4\delta_{\nu}}\right)
, which equals
{\mathbb{R}}
if and only if
\tau_{\nu}^{2}-4\delta_{\nu}>0
.

Finally, recall that in the
(r_{\nu},N_{\nu})
-coordinates we have

r_{\nu}=\tau_{\nu}
and
N_{\nu}=4\delta_{\nu}
, so that
\tau_{\nu}^{2}-4\delta_{\nu}>0
if and only if
r_{\nu}^{2}-N_{\nu}>0
.
Since
\gamma\in\GL(2,K)
we have
\delta_{\nu}\neq 0
, whence
N_{\nu}\neq 0
.
So,
\chi
is unramified at
\nu
if and only if
(r_{\nu},N_{\nu})
lies in
S_{\spl}
.
∎

Proposition 5.12
.

Let
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
be regular elliptic matrices.
If
\gamma_{1}\siminf\gamma_{2}
then
H_{\gamma_{1}}=H_{\gamma_{2}}
.

Proof.

By definition

H_{\gamma}(z,y)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Re(u)=1}y^{-u}\widetilde{F}(u)\frac{L_{\infty}(1-z+u,\chi_{\gamma})}{L_{\infty}(z-u,\chi_{\gamma})}du.

The dependence of
\gamma
is on the factors at infinity.
But, these factors are determined by the ramification of
\gamma
at infinity.
Theorem
5.11
precisely states that the ramification behaviour of
\chi_{\gamma}
remains invariant if
\gamma_{1}\siminf\gamma_{2}
.
∎

Definition 5.13
.

Let
(r,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
be such that
(r,\beta^{y})\in S_{J}
for the multi-index
J=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{n})
where each
j_{t}\in\{{\spl},{\el}\}
.
Define
H_{(r,y)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
by

H_{(r,y)}(z,t)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=H_{\gamma}(z,t)

for any regular elliptic matrix with
\gamma\in\GL(2,K)
with
\CH(\gamma)\in S_{J}
.

Remark 5.14
.

(i)

The above definition is well-defined because all maximal tori of
G_{\infty}
span all possible splitting behaviour and Proposition
5.3
asserts that the function
H_{\gamma}
is invariant under splitting behaviour.

(ii)

Definition
5.13
allows us to extend the definition of
H_{\gamma}=H_{(\tau,u)}
to
all
points
(r,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2n-1}
.
[Here we are using the fact that
\gamma
is characterized by
(\tau,u)
where
u
is once again a unit in
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
.]

(iii)

Implicit in the above definition is the fact that
\gamma_{1}\siminf\gamma_{2}
implies

\frac{L_{\infty}(1-z+u,\chi_{\gamma_{1}})}{L_{\infty}(z-u,\chi_{\gamma_{1}})}=\frac{L_{\infty}(1-z+u,\chi_{\gamma_{2}})}{L_{\infty}(z-u,\chi_{\gamma_{2}})}.

Indeed, recalling the notation in §
Notation
we see that these factors depend on the behavior of
\chi_{\gamma}
at infinity which in turn only depends on the subset
S_{J}
(as proved in Theorem
5.12
).
When we want to emphasize the independence we suppress the subscript
\gamma
and write

\frac{L_{\infty}(1-z+u,\chi)}{L_{\infty}(z-u,\chi)}.

In particular, we use this notation in Corollary
10.2
.

5.4.
Estimates on
H_{\gamma}(1,x)

The difference for each
H_{\gamma}(1,x)
as defined in Theorem
5.10
comes from the
L_{\infty}
factors.
We need to calculate the bound when
z=1
.

Lemma 5.15
.

For
x\geq 1
,

H_{\gamma}(1,x)\ll\frac{1}{x}e^{-(\sqrt[n+1]{x})}

where the implied constant is absolute.

Notation
.

In this proof we write
u
to denote a variable and it should not be confused with a unit of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
.

Proof.

We begin the proof by recalling Stirling’s formula for the Gamma function (see
[
37
, p. 326]
), for
|u|\rightarrow\infty
and
|\arg u|<\pi
we have

\Gamma(u)=\sqrt{2\pi}u^{u-1/2}e^{-u}\left(1+O\left(\mathinner{\!\left\lvert u\right\rvert}^{-1/2}\right)\right).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\Gamma\left((u+\delta_{\nu,\gamma})/2\right)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2\pi}\left((u+\delta_{\nu,\gamma})/2\right)^{(u+\delta_{\nu,\gamma}-1)/2}e^{(-u-\delta_{\nu,\gamma})/2}\left(1+O\left(\mathinner{\!\left\lvert(u+\delta_{\nu,\gamma})/2\right\rvert}^{-1/2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\Gamma\left((1-u+\delta_{\nu,\gamma})/2\right)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2\pi}\left((1-u+\delta_{\nu,\gamma})/2\right)^{(-u+\delta_{\nu,\gamma})/2}e^{(-1+u-\delta_{\nu,\gamma})/2}\left(1+O\left(\mathinner{\!\left\lvert(1-u+\delta_{\nu,\gamma})/2\right\rvert}^{-1/2}\right)\right).

Combining the above two expressions,

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\left((u+\delta_{\nu,\gamma})/2\right)}{\Gamma\left((1-u+\delta_{\nu,\gamma})/2\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-u+1/2}\left(\frac{u+\delta_{\nu,\gamma}}{2}\right)^{u-1/2}\left(\frac{u+\delta_{\nu,\gamma}}{2}\right)^{-u/2+\delta_{\nu,\gamma}/2}\left(\frac{1-u+\delta_{\nu,\gamma}}{2}\right)^{(u-\delta_{\nu,\gamma})/2}\left(1+O\left({\mathinner{\!\left\lvert u\right\rvert}^{-1/2}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{u+\delta_{\nu,\gamma}}{2e}\right)^{u-1/2}\left(\frac{1-u+\delta_{\nu,\gamma}}{u+\delta_{\nu,\gamma}}\right)^{(u-\delta_{\nu,\gamma})/2}\left(1+O\left({\mathinner{\!\left\lvert u\right\rvert}^{-1/2}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{u}{2e}\right)^{u-1/2}\left(\frac{u+\delta_{\nu,\gamma}}{u}\right)^{u-1/2}\left(\frac{u}{u+\delta_{\nu,\gamma}}\right)^{(u-\delta_{\nu,\gamma})/2}\left(\frac{1-u+\delta_{\nu,\gamma}}{u}\right)^{(u-\delta_{\nu,\gamma})/2}\left(1+O\left({\mathinner{\!\left\lvert u\right\rvert}^{-1/2}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{u}{2e}\right)^{u-1/2}\left(1+\frac{\delta_{\nu,\gamma}}{u}\right)^{u/2+(\delta_{\nu,\gamma}-1)/2}\left(\frac{1+\delta_{\nu,\gamma}}{u}-1\right)^{(u-\delta_{\nu,\gamma})/2}\left(1+O\left({\mathinner{\!\left\lvert u\right\rvert}^{-1/2}}\right)\right).

Since
\delta_{\nu,\gamma}\in\{0,1\}
, the two quotients above are precisely the quotients
\Gamma\left(\frac{u}{2}\right)/\Gamma\left(\frac{1-u}{2}\right)
and
\Gamma\left(\frac{1+u}{2}\right)/\Gamma\left(\frac{2-u}{2}\right)
that are bounded in the proof of
[
4
, Lemma 3.6]
.
The bound is applied only for
u
on the vertical lines
\Re(u)=\sigma
with
\sigma\geq 1
(namely on
\Re(u)=1
and on the shifted contour
\Re(u)=\max\{1,\sqrt[n+1]{x}\}
below), and on such a line the Möbius transformation
u\mapsto(1+\delta_{\nu,\gamma})/u
maps
\Re(u)=\sigma
onto the circle through the origin of radius
(1+\delta_{\nu,\gamma})/2\sigma\leq 1
.
Consequently,

\mathinner{\!\left\lvert 1+\delta_{\nu,\gamma}/u\right\rvert}\leq 2\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\mathinner{\!\left\lvert(1+\delta_{\nu,\gamma})/u-1\right\rvert}\leq 1,

which is the observation establishing equation
(\dagger)
in the proof of

[
4
, Lemma 3.6]
.
By
loc. cit.
we conclude that on every line

\Re(u)=\sigma\geq 1
,
which gives

\frac{\Gamma\left((u+\delta_{\nu,\gamma})/2\right)}{\Gamma\left((1-u+\delta_{\nu,\gamma})/2\right)}\ll\left(\frac{u}{\sqrt{2}e}\right)^{u-1/2}

where the implied constant is absolute.
Hence we have

\frac{L_{\infty}(u,\chi_{\gamma})}{L_{\infty}(1-u,\chi_{\gamma})}=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\Gamma\left((u+\delta_{\nu,\gamma})/2\right)}{\Gamma\left((1-u+\delta_{\nu,\gamma})/2\right)}\ll\left(\frac{u}{\sqrt{2}e}\right)^{n(u-1/2)}.

Substituting into the definition for
H_{\gamma}(1,x)
and using the bound on
\widetilde{F}(x)
given in Lemma
5.9
,

\displaystyle H_{\gamma}(1,x)

\displaystyle\ll\int_{\Re(u)=1}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{u}{\sqrt{2}e}\right\rvert}^{n(\Re(u)-1/2)}x^{-\Re(u)}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert u\right\rvert}^{\Re(u)-1}e^{-\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\Im(u)\right\rvert}\pi/2}

\displaystyle\ll\int_{\Re(u)=1}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert u\right\rvert}^{(n+1)\Re(u)-(n+2)/2}(e^{n+1}x)^{-\Re(u)}e^{-\pi\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\Im(u)\right\rvert}/2}du.

Shifting the contour to
\Re(u)=\max\{1,\sqrt[n+1]{x}\}
gives

\displaystyle H_{\gamma}(1,x)

\displaystyle\ll\frac{1}{x^{\frac{n+2}{2(n+1)}}}e^{-(\sqrt[n+1]{x})}

\displaystyle\ll\frac{1}{x}e^{-(\sqrt[n+1]{x})}.\qed

Corollary 5.16
.

For
t\geq 1
,

H_{(r,y)}(1,t)\ll\frac{1}{t}e^{-(\sqrt[n+1]{t})}

where the implied constant is absolute.

5.5.
Manipulation using the Approximate Functional Equation

At this point, we have the following expression for the regular elliptic part of the trace formula

R(f)=\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\in L(u)}\theta^{\pm}(\tau,u)L(1,\gamma).

Using the approximate functional equation (see Theorem
5.10
) at
z=1
, we find

\displaystyle L(1,\gamma)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}{({\mathfrak{a}})}}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}F\left(\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}{A}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}(S_{\gamma})\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}\Norm_{K}(\Delta_{\gamma})^{1/2}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})H_{\gamma}\left(1,\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})A}{\Norm_{K}(S_{\gamma})^{2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}\Norm_{K}(\Delta_{\gamma})}\right).

Recall that
\partial=\tau^{2}-4\det(\gamma)
, therefore

\Norm_{K}(\partial)=\frac{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{L}\right\rvert}\Norm_{K}(S_{\gamma}^{2})}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{2}}=\Norm_{K}(\Delta_{\gamma})\Norm_{K}(S_{\gamma})^{2}.

Combining these expressions, we obtain that

\begin{split}R(f)&=\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}^{*}_{K}}\sum_{\tau\in L(u)}\theta^{\pm}(\tau,u)\left(\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}{({\mathfrak{a}})}}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}F\left(\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}{A}\right)\right.\\
&+\left.\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}(\partial)^{1/2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})H_{\gamma}\left(1,\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})A}{\Norm_{K}(\partial)\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}}\right)\right).\end{split}

Remark 5.17
.

At this stage two sums have appeared: one runs over all ideals
{\mathfrak{a}}
(this sum arises from the Dirichlet series of the imprimitive character).
The other runs over
{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}
(this arises from the definition of the generalized Multiplicative Formula of Langlands).

6.
Smoothing the Singularities

Throughout this section we fix a totally positive system of fundamental units of
K
denoted by
\{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n-1}\}
.
Let
\gamma
be a contributing regular elliptic matrix that corresponds to the unit
u
and the trace
\tau
.
Recall from (
3
) that
\varepsilon=\rho^{k^{\prime}}
is a fixed generator for the principal ideal
\left(\det(\gamma)\right)={\mathfrak{p}}^{k}
with
\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{p}})={\mathsf{p}}
.

Notation
.

We write
\varepsilon_{i}=\sigma_{i}(\varepsilon)
and
\beta_{t,i}=\sigma_{i}(\beta_{t})
for
1\leq t\leq n-1
, where
\sigma_{i}
is the
i
-th real embedding of
K
.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
r=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n-1})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}
.
Define the discriminant function
\mathcal{P}^{\pm}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(r,y)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert r_{i}^{2}\pm 4\beta_{1,i}^{y_{1}}\dots\beta_{n-1,i}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{i}\right\rvert}^{-1/2}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert 4\beta_{1,i}^{y_{1}}\dots\beta_{n-1,i}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{i}\right\rvert}^{-1/2}}\right).

Next, we evaluate the above defined function at
r=\tau
and
y=u
.
Our notation here will be similar to the one adapted for
\theta^{\pm}
.
We explain it once again for convenience.
Since
\tau\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
, it can be viewed as an element of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
On the other hand, when we write
y=u
, we mean that
y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n-1})
corresponding to the unit
u=\pm\beta_{1}^{y_{1}}\dots\beta_{n-1}^{y_{n-1}}
.
The relevance of the functions
\mathcal{P}^{\pm}
is clear from the following result.

Proposition 6.2
.

With notation as above,

\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(\tau,u)=\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{k/2}}{\sqrt{\Norm_{K}(\partial)}}.

Proof.

By hypothesis we are evaluating the functions
\mathcal{P}^{\pm}
at
r=\tau
and
y=u
.
Since there exists a regular elliptic matrix corresponding to
(\tau,u)
, for each
i
we can write

\frac{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert r_{i}^{2}\pm 4\beta_{1,i}^{y_{1}}\dots\beta_{n-1,i}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{i}\right\rvert}^{-1/2}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert 4\beta_{1,i}^{y_{1}}\dots\beta_{n-1,i}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{i}\right\rvert}^{-1/2}}=\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{\tau_{i}}{4u_{i}\varepsilon_{i}}\pm 1\right\rvert}^{-1/2}=2D(\sigma_{i}(\lambda_{1}),\sigma_{i}(\lambda_{2}))^{-1/2},

where
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
are the eigenvalues of
\gamma(\tau,u)
.
By Proposition
4.1

\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(\tau,u)=\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{k/2}}{\sqrt{\Norm_{K}(\partial)}}.\qed

Recall that our interpolation function
\theta^{\pm}
has singularities.
In what follows we define smoothed-out versions of this interpolation function which then appear more naturally in the expression for the regular elliptic part.

Definition 6.3
.

Let
F
be the smooth function defined in (
8
).
For a parameter
0<\alpha<1
, and for
(r,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
such that
(r,\beta^{y})
is in the union of the open regions
S_{J}
define the functions

\displaystyle\Phi_{(\pm,\alpha)}(r,y)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Phi_{\pm}(r,y)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\theta^{\pm}(r,y)F\left(\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}}){\mathsf{p}}^{-k\alpha}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{\alpha}}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(r,y)^{2\alpha}\right),

\displaystyle\Psi_{(\pm,\alpha)}(r,y)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Psi_{\pm}(r,y)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\theta^{\pm}(r,y)\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(r,y)\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{-1/2}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}H_{(r,y)}\left(1,\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}}){\mathsf{p}}^{-k(1-\alpha)}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1-\alpha}}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(r,y)^{2(1-\alpha)}\right).

Suppose that
r=\tau
and
y=u
.
Then

(11)

\begin{split}\Phi_{\pm}(\tau,u)&=\theta^{\pm}(\tau,u)F\left(\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}}){\mathsf{p}}^{-k\alpha}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{\alpha}}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(\tau,u)^{2\alpha}\right)\\
&=\theta^{\pm}(\tau,u)F\left(\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}}){\mathsf{p}}^{-k\alpha}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{\alpha}}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{k/2}}{\Norm_{K}(\partial)^{1/2}}\right)^{2\alpha}\right)\quad\textrm{by Proposition}~\ref{Prop: restatement}\\
&=\theta^{\pm}(\tau,u)F\left(\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}{\Norm_{K}(\partial)^{\alpha}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{\alpha}}\right).\end{split}

Recall the definition of the function
H_{\gamma}(z,y)
introduced in (
9
).
A similar calculation as the one above yields

(12)

\begin{split}\Psi_{\pm}(\tau,u)&=\theta^{\pm}(\tau,u)\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(\tau,u)\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{-1/2}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}H_{\gamma}\left(1,\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}}){\mathsf{p}}^{-k(1-\alpha)}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1-\alpha}}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(\tau,u)^{2(1-\alpha)}\right)\\
&=\theta^{\pm}(\tau,u)\left(\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{k/2}}{\Norm_{K}(\partial)^{1/2}}\right)\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{-1/2}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}H_{\gamma}\left(1,\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}}){\mathsf{p}}^{-k(1-\alpha)}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1-\alpha}}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{k/2}}{\Norm_{K}(\partial)^{1/2}}\right)^{2(1-\alpha)}\right)\\
&=\frac{\theta^{\pm}(\tau,u)}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}\Norm_{K}(\partial)^{1/2}}H_{\gamma}\left(1,\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}{\Norm_{K}(\partial)^{1-\alpha}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1-\alpha}}\right),\end{split}

where
\gamma=\gamma(\tau,u)
is the corresponding regular elliptic matrix.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
\alpha>0
and let
\phi
be a Schwartz function
6
6

6

For our purposes,
\phi
will either be the function
F
or the function
H_{\gamma}
.
on
\mathbb{R}
.
Then

\theta^{\pm}(r,y)\phi(\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(r,y)^{2\alpha}),\qquad\theta^{\pm}(r,y)\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(r,y)\phi(\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(\tau,u)^{2(1-\alpha)})

are smooth in
(r,y)
and compactly supported.

Proof.

The proof is essentially that of
[
22
, Theorem 4.1]
, also
[
4
, Proposition 4.1]
, in the setting of
\GL(n,\mathbb{Q})
and
\GL(2,\mathbb{Q})
respectively.
Following the discussion of
[
22
, §4]
, the singularities of the archimedean orbital integral
\theta^{\pm}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2n-1}
are precisely the zero locus of the discriminant, which is given by the vanishing of
\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(\tau,u)
, and are at most
\|(r,y)\|^{-\beta}
for some
\beta\geq 0
.
Then for any singular point
a
of
\theta^{\pm}
and
\alpha>0
we have
\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(r,y)^{2\alpha}\to\infty
as
v=(r,y)\to a
, and

\phi(\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(r,y)^{2\alpha})\ll_{M}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(r,y)^{-2\alpha M}

for any
M>0.
In particular, taking
M>(\beta+1)/\alpha
and arguing as in the proof of
[
22
, Theorem 4.1]
, we see that the product
\theta^{\pm}(r,y)\phi(\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(\tau,u)^{2\alpha})
and its partial derivatives vanish as
(r,y)
approaches
a
.
The argument for the second product
\theta^{\pm}(r,y)\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)\phi(\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(v,y)^{2(1-\alpha)})
follows in a similar manner.

Finally, at the archimedean places, we had imposed the condition that the test functions have compact support and the orbital integrals have relatively compact support.
This ensures that the functions of interest are compactly supported.
∎

Remark 6.5
.

The proof shows these functions are now defined in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
and not only in the regular set.

Upon choosing a precise value of
A
in Theorem
5.10
, it is possible to ensure that
\Phi_{\pm}
and
\Psi_{\pm}
emerge in our manipulation of the regular elliptic part.

Proposition 6.6
.

For a regular elliptic class
\gamma
and a parameter
0<\alpha<1
,

\displaystyle L(1,\gamma)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}{({\mathfrak{a}})}}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}F\left(\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}{\Norm_{K}(\partial)^{\alpha}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{\alpha}}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Norm_{K}(\partial)}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})H_{\gamma}\left(1,\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}{\Norm_{K}(\partial)^{1-\alpha}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1-\alpha}}\right),

Proof.

This follows from the Approximate Functional Equation upon choosing

A=\Norm_{K}(\partial)^{\alpha}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{\alpha}.\qed

Using Proposition
6.6
and the definitions of
\Psi_{\pm}
and
\Phi_{\pm}
, we get

(13)

\begin{split}R(f)&=\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\in L(u)}\theta^{\pm}(\tau,u)\left(\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}{({\mathfrak{a}})}}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}F\left(\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}{\Norm_{K}(\partial)^{\alpha}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{\alpha}}\right)\right)\\
&+\frac{\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{\Norm_{K}(\partial)^{1/2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}}\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\in L(u)}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})H_{\gamma}\left(1,\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}{\Norm_{K}(\partial)^{1-\alpha}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1-\alpha}}\right)\\
&={\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\in L(u)}\left(\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}{({\mathfrak{a}})}}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}\Phi_{\pm}(\tau,u)+\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})\Psi_{\pm}(\tau,u)\right)\\
