&=\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\in L(u)}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\left(\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}{({\mathfrak{a}})}}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}\Phi_{\pm}(\tau,u)+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})\Psi_{\pm}(\tau,u)\right)\\
&=\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\in L(u)}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}(\tau,u)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}(\tau,u)\right).\end{split}

7.
The Problem of Completion

In this section, we perform two main tasks.
First, we replace the sum over ideals
{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}
arising in the expression of the regular elliptic part by a congruence condition.
Second, we introduce a modified Hilbert symbol which correctly detects the character
\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}(-)
in our case and clarifies the dependence on
\tau
.
These two steps will then allow us to ‘complete the lattice’ and set the stage for Poisson summation.

7.1.
The Congruence Conditions

So far we have dealt with the condition
{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}
.
However, to perform Poisson summation over the variables
u,\tau
we need to be explicit regarding its role.
We now aim to replace this divisibility condition by a congruence condition as was done by Altuğ.
The main result of this section is stated below.

Let
{\mathfrak{p}}_{1},\dots,{\mathfrak{p}}_{m}
be the (possibly repeated) primes above
2
in
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
, that is,

2{\mathcal{O}}_{K}={\mathfrak{p}}_{1}\cdots{\mathfrak{p}}_{m}.

We assume
\pi_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}}
is a uniformizer.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
\gamma
be a contributing conjugacy class.
The following two conditions are equivalent

(i)

{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}
,

(ii)

{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}\mid\tau^{2}-4\det(\gamma)
and for each
i=1,\dots,m
the following congruence condition holds in
{\mathcal{O}}_{K_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}}}
,

\frac{\tau^{2}-4\det(\gamma)}{\pi_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}}^{2\val_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}}({\mathfrak{d}})}}\equiv\square\pmod{\pi_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}}^{2}}.

Proof.

This is
[
19
, Proposition 9]
.
∎

Using this result we have

R(f)=\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\in L(u)}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}\mid\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon}^{{}^{\prime}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}(\tau,u)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}(\tau,u)\right),

where following Altuğ we use
′
to denote the congruence conditions given by Theorem
7.1
.

7.2.
The Normalized Character

The primary goal of this section is to clarify the dependence of
\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}
on
\tau
.

Remark 7.2
.

Over
{\mathbb{Q}}
, the ideals can be replaced by rational elements and the Kronecker symbol satisfies

\chi_{d}(a)=\binom{\frac{\tau^{2}-4p^{k}}{d^{2}}}{a}_{K},

making the following step unnecessary; see
[
4
, (4)]
.
However, we want to provide a uniform proof and obtain an expression similar to the one above for
\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}
.

7.2.1.

We begin by recalling the definition of the Hilbert Symbol in our context.

Definition 7.3
.

Let
K
be a number field and let
{\mathfrak{q}}
be a prime of
K
.
The
Hilbert Symbol
of
K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
is defined in
K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{*}\times K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{*}
given by

\binom{a,b}{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{H}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\text{ if }z^{2}=ax^{2}+by^{2}\text{ has solutions in }{\mathcal{O}}_{K_{q}}\\
-1&\text{ otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Fix a uniformizer
\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
and define the
restricted Hilbert Symbol
as the function on
{\mathcal{O}}_{K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}
given by

\binom{a}{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{rH}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\binom{a,\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\tiny{H}}&\text{ if }a\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}^{*}\\
0&\text{ otherwise}\end{array}\right.

and we extend it to
K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
as
0
for all
a
with
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert a\right\rvert}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\neq 1
.

Remark 7.4
.

For primes
{\mathfrak{q}}\nmid 2
, the restricted Hilbert Symbol coincides with the quadratic character of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}^{*}
, i.e. the character whose value is
\pm 1
according as
a
is a square or not.
It is independent of the choice of the uniformizer
\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
; see
[
32
, chapter 4]
.
For the primes above
2
this is not the case but the above definition will allow us make explicit the character
\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}
.

We record the following fact of the Hilbert Symbol that will be useful for us.
We include the proof of (ii) as we were not able to find it written in the literature.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
{\mathfrak{q}}
be a prime of
K
and
a_{1},a_{2}
elements of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}^{*}
.
Then

(i)

If
{\mathfrak{q}}\nmid 2
and
a_{1}\equiv a_{2}\pmod{\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}
, then

\binom{a_{1},\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{H}=\binom{a_{2},\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{H}.

(ii)

If
{\mathfrak{q}}\mid 2
, has ramification degree
e_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
, and
a_{1}\equiv a_{2}\pmod{\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{2e_{{\mathfrak{q}}}+1}}
, then

\binom{a_{1},\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{H}=\binom{a_{2},\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{H}.

Proof.

Both these assertions are applications of Hensel’s Lemma.
We show (ii) and leave (i) as an exercise for the reader.
If
{\mathfrak{q}}
ramifies, then Hensel’s lemma asserts that
u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}^{*}
is a square if and only if
u
is a square modulo
\pi^{2e_{{\mathfrak{q}}}+1}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
.

If
\binom{a_{1},\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{H}=1
then there exist
X_{0},Y_{0},Z_{0}
(not all equal to 0) satisfying

Z_{0}^{2}=a_{1}X_{0}^{2}+\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}Y_{0}^{2}.

Using an infinite descent argument, we assume without loss of generality that
Z_{0},X_{0}
are in
{\mathcal{O}}_{K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}^{*}
.
In particular,

a_{2}X_{0}^{2}+\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}Y_{0}^{2}\equiv a_{1}X_{0}^{2}+\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}Y_{0}^{2}\equiv Z_{0}^{2}\pmod{\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{2e_{{\mathfrak{q}}}+1}},

where the first congruence follows from the assumptions.
In view of Hensel’s Lemma, we deduce that
a_{2}X_{0}^{2}+\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}Y_{0}^{2}
is a square; i.e. there exists
Z_{1}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}^{*}
with

Z_{1}^{2}=a_{2}X_{0}^{2}+\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}Y_{0}^{2}.

Thus, the Hilbert symbol on the right is also
1
.
Both sides play symmetric roles so we deduce that both sides are either
\pm 1
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

7.2.2.

Now, we define a symbol which allows us to recover the characters
\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}
.
It also clarifies the role of
\tau
and
u
.

Definition 7.6
.

Let
{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b}
be any ideals of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
and let
\alpha
be an element of
K
.
For each prime ideal
{\mathfrak{q}}
of
K
, let
\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
be a uniformizer of
K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
.
Define the
modified Hilbert symbol
, with respect to the
\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
, as

\binom{\alpha,{\mathfrak{a}}}{\mathfrak{b}}=\prod_{{\mathfrak{q}}\mid\mathfrak{b}}\binom{\alpha\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathfrak{a}})}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\mathfrak{b})}_{rH}.

Remark 7.7
.

The modified Hilbert symbol does not depend on the choice of the uniformizer because from the definition of the Artin symbol

\binom{\alpha,\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\sqrt{\alpha}=\left(\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}},K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\gamma)/K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)_{A}\sqrt{\alpha}.

and the Artin Symbol does not depend on uniformizers.

Now we prove a crucial property of the modified symbol:
it recovers the quadratic Hecke character at the primes.

Lemma 7.8
.

For
u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}
and
\tau\in L(u)
, let
\gamma
be the regular elliptic class corresponding to the pair
(\tau,u)
.
Let
{\mathfrak{q}}
be any prime of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
.
Then,

\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{q}})=\binom{\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon,S_{\gamma}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}=\binom{(\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon)\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(S_{\gamma})}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{rH}.

Proof.

First, consider the case that
{\mathfrak{q}}\nmid 2
.
Since

(\tau^{2}-4u\det(\gamma))=(\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon)=S_{\gamma}^{2}\Delta_{\gamma},

we deduce

\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon)-2\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(S_{\gamma})=\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\Delta_{\gamma}).

The upper entry of the modified Hilbert symbol has
{\mathfrak{q}}
-valuation equal to that of
\Delta_{\gamma}
.

Note that if
{\mathfrak{q}}
is a ramified prime, then both sides of the equality (we wish to prove) are
0
.
If
{\mathfrak{q}}
is not ramified, then
\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon){\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(S_{\gamma})}}
is an element of
{\mathcal{O}}^{*}_{K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}
.
By Hensel’s Lemma (applied to the polynomial
X^{2}-\alpha
) we know that
\alpha
is a square in
{\mathcal{O}}_{K_{\mathfrak{q}}}
if and only if
\alpha
is a square modulo
{\mathfrak{q}}
.
Recall that
\alpha
is a square in
{\mathcal{O}}_{K_{\mathfrak{q}}}
precisely when the extension
K(\sqrt{\alpha})/K
is either degree
1
or
2
; i.e.
{\mathfrak{q}}
is either split or inert.
On the other hand, the condition
\alpha
is a square (resp. non-square) modulo
{\mathfrak{q}}
is equivalent to the symbol being
1
(resp.
-1
) by definition.
This concludes the proof for odd primes.

Now, suppose that
{\mathfrak{q}}\mid 2
.
If
{\mathfrak{q}}
is ramified, the proof is the same as before.
So, we only consider the case that
{\mathfrak{q}}\mid 2
and is unramified.
It follows from the definition of the Artin symbol that

\binom{\alpha,\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\sqrt{\alpha}=\left(\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}},K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\gamma)/K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)_{A}\sqrt{\alpha}.

Since
\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
is a uniformizer, the Artin symbol
\left(\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}},K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\gamma)/K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)_{A}
is the Frobenius element of
{\mathfrak{q}}
.
This takes the value
1
or
-1
according as
{\mathfrak{q}}
is split or inert.
This is precisely the definition of
\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{q}})
.
Hence,

\binom{\alpha,\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}=\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{q}}).

This completes the proof.
∎

Theorem 7.9
.

Let
u
be an element of
{\mathcal{O}}^{*}_{K}
and let
\tau\in L(u)
.
Denote by
\gamma
the regular elliptic class that corresponds to the pair
(\tau,u)
.
Let
{\mathfrak{d}}
be an ideal of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
satisfying
{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}
and let
{\mathfrak{a}}
be any ideal of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
.
Write

{\mathfrak{a}}=\prod{\mathfrak{q}}^{v_{{\mathfrak{q}}}},\;{\mathfrak{d}}=\prod{\mathfrak{q}}^{r_{{\mathfrak{q}}}},

where
v_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
or
r_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
may be equal to 0.
Then,

\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})=\binom{\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}.

Proof.

By definition

\binom{\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}=\prod_{{\mathfrak{q}}\mid{\mathfrak{a}}}\binom{(\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon)\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{v_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}_{rH}.

As before, set
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime}=S_{\gamma}/{\mathfrak{d}}
.
Recall that the definition of
\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}
is

\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{a}})&\mbox{if }({\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime})=1\\
0&\mbox{if }({\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime})\neq 1.\end{array}\right.

Hence, we divide the proof into two cases depending on
({\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime})
.

Case 1:

If
({\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime})\neq 1
then
\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})=0
by definition and for some
{\mathfrak{q}}
both
r_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
and
v_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
are non-zero.
Hence

\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}((\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon)\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r_{\mathfrak{q}}})=\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime}\Delta_{\gamma})\geq 1.

Hence,
(\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon)\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r_{\mathfrak{q}}}
is not a unit in
{\mathcal{O}}_{K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}
; and since
v_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\geq 1

\binom{(\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon)\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{v_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}_{rH}=0.

By the definition of the modified Hilbert symbol, we have

\binom{\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}=0.

Case 2:
Let us now suppose
({\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime})=1
and pick a prime
{\mathfrak{q}}\mid{\mathfrak{a}}
.
When
{\mathfrak{q}}
is ramified, i.e.
{\mathfrak{q}}\mid\Delta_{\gamma}
,

\binom{\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}=0.

This is because

\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}((\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon)\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r_{\mathfrak{q}}})=\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime}\Delta_{\gamma})\geq 1.

On the other hand, since a ramified prime divides
{\mathfrak{a}}
,

\chi_{{\mathfrak{d}}}({\mathfrak{a}})=\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{a}})=0.

Thus we are left to consider the case in which all the primes that divide
{\mathfrak{a}}
are not ramified.
In this situation,
(\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon)\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r_{\mathfrak{q}}}
is a unit for each
{\mathfrak{q}}\mid{\mathfrak{a}}
.
We have

\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(S_{\gamma})=\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathfrak{d}})+\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime})=\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathfrak{d}})=r_{\mathfrak{q}}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\binom{\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{{\mathfrak{q}}\mid{\mathfrak{a}}}\binom{(\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon)\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{rH}^{v_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{{\mathfrak{q}}\mid{\mathfrak{a}}}\binom{(\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon)\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(S_{\gamma})}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{rH}^{v_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{{\mathfrak{q}}\mid{\mathfrak{a}}}\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{q}})^{v_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}\quad\text{by Lemma}~\ref{lemma: to check}

\displaystyle=\chi_{\gamma}({\mathfrak{a}}).\qed

Theorem
7.9
together with the congruence conditions from §
7.1
will allow us to clarify how every term of the trace formula depends on
\tau
.
We now prove that the modified Hilbert symbol is periodic.

Lemma 7.10
.

Let
\tau_{1}\equiv\tau_{2}\pmod{4{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}}
in
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
.
Then

\binom{\tau_{1}^{2}-4u\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}=\binom{\tau_{2}^{2}-4u\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}.

Proof.

Let
{\mathfrak{q}}\mid{\mathfrak{a}}
and set
r=\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathfrak{d}})
.
Let
\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
be a uniformizer of
K_{\mathfrak{q}}
.
By definition of the modified Hilbert symbol, it is enough to prove

\binom{(\tau_{1}^{2}-4u\varepsilon)\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}=\binom{(\tau_{2}^{2}-4u\varepsilon)\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}.

Notice that
\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{2r}\mid\tau_{1}^{2}-\tau_{2}^{2}
.
Hence
(\tau_{1}^{2}-4u\varepsilon)\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r}
is an integer if and only if
(\tau_{2}^{2}-4u\varepsilon)\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r}
is an integer.
Since
{\mathfrak{a}}\mid\tau_{1}-\tau_{2}
, we get an extra factor
\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
than those than come from
{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}
.
Whence

\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{2r+1}\mid\tau_{1}^{2}-\tau_{2}^{2}=(\tau_{1}^{2}-4u\varepsilon)-(\tau_{2}^{2}-4u\varepsilon).

So, in case both ‘numerators’ of the symbols are integers, we have

(\tau_{1}^{2}-4u\varepsilon)\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r}\equiv(\tau_{1}^{2}-4u\varepsilon)\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r}\pmod{\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}.

Lemma
7.5
(i)
asserts that if
{\mathfrak{q}}\nmid 2
, then

\binom{(\tau_{1}^{2}-4u\varepsilon)\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}=\binom{(\tau_{2}^{2}-4u\varepsilon)\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}.

On the other hand, if
{\mathfrak{q}}\mid 2
, then

\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(4{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{2})=2e_{{\mathfrak{q}}}+\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathfrak{a}})+r\geq 2e_{{\mathfrak{q}}}+1,

where
e_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
is the ramification of
{\mathfrak{q}}
above
2
.
Equality can occur if
r=0
and
\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathfrak{a}})=1
.
Hence

\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{2e_{{\mathfrak{q}}}+1}\mid(\tau_{1}^{2}-4u\varepsilon)\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r}-(\tau_{2}^{2}-4u\varepsilon)\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r}.

By Lemma
7.5
(ii)
we conclude the proof.
∎

Next, we prove the periodicity of the congruence conditions.

Lemma 7.11
.

Let
{\mathfrak{a}}
and
{\mathfrak{d}}
be ideals in
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
.
If
\tau_{1}\equiv\tau_{2}\pmod{4{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}}
then

{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}\mid\tau_{1}^{2}-4u\varepsilon\Longleftrightarrow{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}\mid\tau_{2}^{2}-4u\varepsilon.

Moreover, if
{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}\mid\tau_{i}^{2}-4u\varepsilon
, then

\frac{\tau_{1}^{2}-4u\varepsilon}{{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}}\equiv\frac{\tau_{2}^{2}-4u\varepsilon}{{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}}\pmod{4}.

Proof.

Recall that
4{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}\mid\tau_{1}^{2}-\tau_{2}^{2}
.
Therefore

4{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}\mid(\tau_{1}^{2}-4u\varepsilon)-(\tau_{2}^{2}-4u\varepsilon).

The result now follows.
∎

7.3.
Completion of the Lattice

Using the modified Hilbert symbol, we can rewrite (
13
) as

R(f)=\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\in L(u)}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}\mid\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon}^{{}^{\prime}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\binom{\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}(\tau,u)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}(\tau,u)\right).

Notice that the expression

\binom{\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}(\tau,u)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}(\tau,u)\right)

makes sense for any value of
\tau,u,{\mathfrak{d}},{\mathfrak{a}}
regardless of whether there is a regular elliptic matrix
\gamma
(corresponding to
\tau
and
u
) whose quadratic character produces the divisors
{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}
.
In particular, we can consider

\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\notin L(u)}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}\mid\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon}^{{}^{\prime}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\binom{\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}(\tau,u)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}(\tau,u)\right).

Introducing the congruence condition in place of the condition
{\mathfrak{d}}\mid S_{\gamma}
allowed us to replace the
{\mathfrak{d}}
-sum in (
13
) with a sum whose condition depends on
\tau
and
u
(but not on whether there is associated to such
{\mathfrak{d}}
a quadratic extension which determines
S_{\gamma}
).

Definition 7.12
.

Define

\displaystyle\Sigma(\square)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\notin L(u)}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}\mid\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon}^{{}^{\prime}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\binom{\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}(\tau,u)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}(\tau,u)\right).

For this definition to make sense, we need to prove the following theorem.

Theorem 7.13
.

The series defining
\Sigma(\square)
is an absolutely convergent sum.

Proof.

The absolute value of the character is bounded by
1
.
Thus, what we must prove is that

\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\notin L(u)}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}\mid\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon}^{{}^{\prime}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\Phi_{\pm}(\tau,u)\right\rvert}}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}=\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\notin L(u)}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\theta^{\pm}(\tau,u)\right\rvert}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}\mid\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon}^{{}^{\prime}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert F\left(\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}{\Norm_{K}(\partial)^{\alpha}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{\alpha}}\right)\right\rvert}}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}

and

\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\notin L(u)}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}\mid\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon}^{{}^{\prime}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\Psi_{\pm}(\tau,u)\right\rvert}=\\
\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\notin L(u)}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{\theta^{\pm}(\tau,u)}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}\Norm_{K}(\partial)^{1/2}}\right\rvert}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}\mid\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon}^{{}^{\prime}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\mathinner{\!\left\lvert H_{\gamma}\left(1,\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}{\Norm_{K}(\partial)^{1-\alpha}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1-\alpha}}\right)\right\rvert},

are both convergent.
If we prove the inner sums over
{\mathfrak{d}}
and
{\mathfrak{a}}
are absolutely convergent we are done, because the outer sums over
u
and
\tau
are finite (since
\theta^{\pm}
are of compact support).
Thus they contribute a finite nonzero terms.

Let us study the interior sums separately.
We know that for
{\mathfrak{d}},{\mathfrak{a}}
large enough

\mathinner{\!\left\lvert F\left(\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}{\Norm_{K}(\partial)^{\alpha}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{\alpha}}\right)\right\rvert}\ll\exp\left(-\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}{\Norm_{K}(\partial)^{\alpha}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{\alpha}}\right)\ll\left(\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}{\Norm_{K}(\partial)^{\alpha}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{\alpha}}\right)^{-1}.

Thus

\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}\mid\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon}^{{}^{\prime}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert F\left(\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}{\Norm_{K}(\partial)^{\alpha}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{\alpha}}\right)\right\rvert}}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}\ll\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}\mid\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon}^{{}^{\prime}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\dfrac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{3}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{2}}<\infty.

Using Lemma
5.15
, we have

\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\mathinner{\!\left\lvert H_{\gamma}\left(1,\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}{\Norm_{K}(\partial)^{1-\alpha}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1-\alpha}}\right)\right\rvert}\ll\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})}{\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}{\Norm_{K}(\partial)^{1-\alpha}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1-\alpha}}}\exp\left(-\left(\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}{\Norm_{K}(\partial)^{1-\alpha}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1-\alpha}}\right)^{1/n+1}\right)

For large enough
\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}}),\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})
the above expression can be bounded by

\ll\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})\left(\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})\right)^{1/n+1}},

where the implied constant only depends on
\tau,u
, and by the finiteness of their contribution, it can be taken to be absolute.
This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 7.14
.

The proof works exactly the same way if we instead use
\tau\in L(u)
, even though we already know that that sum converges absolutely by the approximate functional equation (since it comes from the Zagier Zeta Function which is entire).
We cannot use the approximate functional equation directly for the cases of
\Sigma(\square)
because the character in that case returns the zeta function, and thus has poles itself.

Definition 7.15
.

We define the
completed regular elliptic part
of the trace formula as

\displaystyle\overline{R}(f)

\displaystyle=\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{\tau\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}\mid\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon}^{{}^{\prime}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\binom{\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}(\tau,u)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}(\tau,u)\right)

\displaystyle=R(f)+\Sigma(\square).

Remark 7.16
.

The name comes from the fact that we have added those
\tau\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
that were missing from the sum.
We have
completed
the lattice of integers.

In the following result we show that the order of summation in the completed regular elliptic part can be swapped.

Proposition 7.17
.

The order of summation in the completed regular elliptic part can be swapped.
More precisely,

\overline{R}(f)=\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}\mid\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon}^{{}^{\prime}}\binom{\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}(\tau,u)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}(\tau,u)\right).

Proof.

We have defined
\overline{R}(f)
as the sum of two absolutely convergent series.
Thus we can arrange the series in any alternative order without altering the convergence properties or values.
∎

Remark 7.18
.

In the expression defining
\overline{R}(f)
, we see that there are no conditions on
\tau
and
{\mathfrak{d}}
runs over the possible ideals such that
{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}\mid\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon
and satisfies certain congruence conditions.
After changing the order of summation,
{\mathfrak{d}}
affords no conditions and the innermost sum is now over the
\tau
such that
\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon
has
{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}
as divisor, with the appropriate congruence conditions.
Both sums look the same but it is pertinent to note that they have different free and dependent variables.

8.
Poisson Summation

8.1.
Explicitly writing the lattice

Recall from §
4.2
that we fixed the fundamental units
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n-1}
in
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}
(which are embedded into
W
under the function
S
) satisfying
(Hyp-pos)
.
Any unit
u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}
can be written as

u=\pm\beta_{1}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1}^{y_{n-1}}.

In particular,
u=u(\pm,y)
where
y=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n-1}
.

Notation
.

In what follows, we write
u
in place of
u(\pm,y)
for a unit in
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}
.

The expression obtained in Proposition
7.17
can be rewritten as

(14)

\overline{R}(f)=\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\sum_{y\in\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}\mid\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon}^{{}^{\prime}}\binom{\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}(\tau,u)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}(\tau,u)\right).

Given ideals
{\mathfrak{d}}
and
{\mathfrak{a}}
in
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
, the periodicity of the congruence conditions proved in Lemma
7.11
implies that for a given congruence class modulo
4{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}
, either every term of the arithmetic progression appears in the sum or no term appears.
Therefore, the expression (
14
) can be rewritten as

\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\sum_{y\in\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}}\sum_{\mu\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}/4{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}}^{{}^{\prime}}\sum_{\tau\equiv\mu}\binom{\tau^{2}-4u\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}(\tau,u)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}(\tau,u)\right).

By Lemma
7.11
we know that the symbol is periodic so the above expression can be further rewritten as

\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\sum_{y\in\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}}\sum_{\mu\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}/4{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}}^{{}^{\prime}}\binom{\mu^{2}-4u\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{\tau\equiv\mu}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}(\tau,u)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}(\tau,u)\right).

Within the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}
there is the sub-lattice
(2\mathbb{Z})^{n-1}
and the quotient is
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}
, where
\mathbb{F}_{2}
is the field of two elements.
Let
Y
be a variable that runs over this quotient.
We can rewrite the above expression as

\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\sum_{Y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}}\sum_{y\equiv Y}\sum_{\mu\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}/4{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}}^{{}^{\prime}}\binom{\mu^{2}-4u\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{\tau\equiv\mu}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}(\tau,u)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}(\tau,u)\right).

Fix the sign
\pm
and a vector
Y=(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n-1})\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}
.
If
y\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n-1}
satisfies
y\equiv Y
, there exists another unit
v\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}
such that
u=u(e,y)=u(Y)v^{2}
with

u(Y)=\beta_{1}^{Y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1}^{Y_{n-1}},

where
e
is the fixed sign.
This unit
v
is uniquely determined by
Y
and
y
, if we impose the extra condition that it lies in
W
, which is to say, that the two units have the same sign.
The condition
y\equiv Y
implies the existence of
y^{\prime}=(y^{\prime}_{1},\dots,y^{\prime}_{n-1})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n-1}
such that for each
1\leq t\leq n-1
,

y_{t}=Y_{t}+2y^{\prime}_{t}.

This vector
y^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n-1}
is in one-to-one correspondence with
v=\beta_{1}^{y^{\prime}_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1}^{y^{\prime}_{n-1}}
.
In other words, the unit
v
can be associated with an
(n-1)
-dimensional vector given by

\frac{1}{2}\left(y_{1}-Y_{1},\dots,y_{n-1}-Y_{n-1}\right).

We can then rewrite the completed regular elliptic part as

\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\sum_{Y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}}\sum_{y\equiv Y}\sum_{\mu\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}/4{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}}^{{}^{\prime}}\binom{\mu^{2}-4{u(Y)}v^{2}\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{\tau\equiv\mu}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}(\tau,u)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}(\tau,u)\right).

Since

\binom{\mu^{2}-4{u(Y)}v^{2}\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}=\binom{v^{2}(\mu v^{-1})^{2}-4{u(Y)}v^{2}\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}=\binom{(\mu v^{-1})^{2}-4{u(Y)}\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}},

the completed regular elliptic part is

\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\sum_{Y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}}\sum_{y\equiv Y}\sum_{\mu\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}/4{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}}^{{}^{\prime}}\binom{(\mu v^{-1})^{2}-4{u(Y)}\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{\tau\equiv\mu}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}(\tau,u)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}(\tau,u)\right).

Since
v
is a unit, multiplication by it only permutes the classes modulo
4{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}
.
Relabeling
\mu v^{-1}
as
\mu
, we may remove
v^{-1}
from the symbol at the expense of changing the inner sum to
\tau\equiv\mu v
.
Therefore,

\overline{R}(f)=\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\sum_{Y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}}\sum_{y\equiv Y}\sum_{\mu\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}/4{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}}^{{}^{\prime}}\binom{\mu^{2}-4{u(Y)}\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{\tau\equiv\mu v}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}(\tau,u)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}(\tau,u)\right).

We can record the main theorem of this section.

Theorem 8.1
.

The completed regular elliptic part
\overline{R}(f)
can be rewritten as

\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\sum_{Y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}}\sum_{\mu\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}/4{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}}^{{}^{\prime}}\binom{\mu^{2}-4{u(Y)}\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{y\equiv Y}\sum_{\tau\equiv\mu v}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}(\tau,u)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}(\tau,u)\right).

Remark 8.2
.

When
K={\mathbb{Q}}
, the unit group is a finite set - there are two units, namely, the two roots of unity
\pm 1
.
Hence, the sums over
Y
and
y
are both empty.
The ideals correspond to the integers in this case.
For each ideal, we always pick the positive representative, and we pick
p
to be the representative of
{\mathfrak{p}}
.
Therefore, Theorem
8.1
asserts that the completed regular elliptic part of the trace formula can be written as

\sum_{\pm}\sum_{d}\sum_{a}\sum_{\mu\mod{4ad^{2}}}\binom{(\mu^{2}\pm 4p^{k})/d^{2}}{a}\sum_{\tau\equiv\mu}^{{}^{\prime}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{u}(\tau)}{da}+d\Psi_{u}(\tau)\right).

This is exactly what appears in
[
4
, proof of Theorem 4.2]
upon changing our
a
for his
l
, our
d
for his
f
, our
\mu
for his
a
, and our
\tau
for his
m
, before performing Poisson summation.

We must study the indexing set of the two inner sums appearing in the expression in Theorem
8.1
and rewrite them as sums over a lattice.
The following lemma will ensure that the indexing sets form an honest lattice inside
{\mathbb{R}}^{2n-1}
which will then allow us to perform the Poisson summation in a future step.

Lemma 8.3
.

Fix a set of totally positive fundamental units
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n-1}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}
, a choice of sign, and a vector
Y=(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n-1})\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}
.
Let
u=u(\pm,Y)
be the unit corresponding to this choice.
Fix an integral basis
\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{n}
for the ideal
4{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}
.
Finally, fix a representative
\mu
of some class of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}/4{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}
.

For each embedding
\sigma_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}
, set
A_{i}=\sigma_{i}(u\mu)
and
\omega_{j,i}=\sigma_{i}(\omega_{j})
.
Then the pairs
(\tau,u(y))
that appear in Theorem
8.1
are those with coordinates

\left(A_{1}\beta_{1,1}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,1}^{y_{n}-1}+r_{1}\omega_{1,1}+\cdots+r_{n}\omega_{n,1},\ldots,A_{n}\beta_{1,n}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,n}^{y_{n}-1}+\right.\\
\left.r_{1}\omega_{1,n}+\cdots+r_{n}\omega_{n,n},Y_{1}+2y_{1},\dots,Y_{n-1}+2y_{n-1}\right),

for some
r=(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
and
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n-1}
.

Proof.

The condition
y\equiv Y
implies the existence of
y^{\prime}=(y^{\prime}_{1},\dots,y^{\prime}_{n-1})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n-1}
such that for each
1\leq t\leq n-1
,

y_{t}=Y_{t}+2y^{\prime}_{t}.

This vector
y^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n-1}
is in one-to-one correspondence with
v=\beta_{1}^{y^{\prime}_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1}^{y^{\prime}_{n-1}}
.
Therefore, for
\mu\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}/4{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}
, the equivalence
\tau\equiv\mu v
can be rewritten (component-wise) as

\tau_{i}=\sigma_{i}(\beta_{1}^{y^{\prime}_{1}}\cdot\ldots\cdot\beta_{n-1}^{y^{\prime}_{n-1}}\mu)+z_{i},

for some integer
z_{i}
, where
1\leq i\leq n
.
The result now follows (by relabeling
y_{t}^{\prime}
by
y_{t}
).
∎

In view of the above discussion, we make the following definition.

Definition 8.4
.

Keep the notation introduced above; except now suppose that
(r,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}
.
Define

\displaystyle C_{i}(y)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\beta_{1,i}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,i}^{y_{n-1}}\textrm{ and }

\displaystyle A(r,y)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\left(A_{1}C_{1}(y)+r_{1}\omega_{1,1}+\cdots+r_{n}\omega_{n,1},\ldots,\right.

\displaystyle\left.\hskip 130.08621ptA_{n}C_{n}(y)+\cdots+r_{n}\omega_{n,n},Y_{1}+2y_{1},\dots,Y_{n-1}+2y_{n-1}\right).

Proposition 8.5

(Change of Variables)
.

Keep the notation introduced above and let
(r,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}
.
Then

(i)

A(r,y)
is an invertible function.

(ii)

The absolute value of the determinant of the Jacobian matrix of
A(r,y)
is

2^{3n-1}N({\mathfrak{a}})N({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}.

Proof.

(i)

One can write explicitly the inverse of this function.
This is left as an exercise for the reader.

(ii)

By computing partial derivatives the reader can check that the Jacobian matrix has the form

\begin{pmatrix}\omega_{1,1}&\cdots&\omega_{n,1}&*&\cdots&*\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\omega_{n,1}&\cdots&\omega_{n,n}&*&\cdots&*\\
0&\cdots&0&2&\cdots&0\\
0&\cdots&0&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&0&0&\cdots&2\\
\end{pmatrix}.

Thus, the determinant of the above matrix is

2^{n-1}\det\begin{pmatrix}\omega_{1,1}&\cdots&\omega_{n,1}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\omega_{n,1}&\cdots&\omega_{n,n}\\
\end{pmatrix}

By definition, the determinant of the
n\times n
matrix is
N(4{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{2})\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}=4^{n}N({\mathfrak{a}})N({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}
.
∎

Notation
.

The transformation
A
depends on
{\mathfrak{a}}
,
{\mathfrak{d}}
,
\pm
,
Y
, and
\mu
but we omit this from the notation.

We now rewrite the inner two sums of the completed regular elliptic part.

Proposition 8.6
.

The inner two sums of the expression in Theorem
8.1
are rewritten as

\sum_{y\in\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}\left(A(r,y)\right)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}\left(A(r,y)\right)\right).

8.2.
Performing Poisson Summation.

For
(t,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
, let
G(t,s)
be a smooth function with compact support.
Set
\widehat{G}
to denote the
Fourier transform
of
G
, i.e.,

\widehat{G}(\xi,\eta)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}G(t,s)\exp(-2\pi i(t\cdot\xi+s\cdot\eta))dsdt,

where
(\xi,\eta)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}
.
Then, Poisson summation is the following assertion

\sum_{(t,s)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{n-1}}G(t,s)=\sum_{(\xi,\eta)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{n-1}}\widehat{G}(\xi,\eta).

The following important result essentially follows from unraveling the definitions.

Proposition 8.7
.

Let
A
be the transformation from Definition
8.4
.
Let
G(r,y)=H(A(r,y))
for some smooth function
H
with compact support, then

\widehat{G}(\xi,\eta)=\frac{1}{2^{3n-1}N({\mathfrak{a}})N({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}}\int\int H(t,s)\exp(-2\pi i(\xi,\eta)\cdot A^{-1}(t,s))dsdt.

Proof.

This follows from definition of Fourier transform and the change of variable formula using Proposition
8.5
.
∎

Definition 8.8
.

For the functions
\Phi_{\pm}
and
\Psi_{\pm}
introduced in (
11
) and (
12
), define the
modified Fourier transforms

\displaystyle\Phi_{\pm}^{1}(\xi,\eta)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int\int\Phi_{\pm}(t,s)\exp(-2\pi i(\xi,\eta)\cdot A^{-1}(t,s))dsdt,

\displaystyle\Psi_{\pm}^{1}(\xi,\eta)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int\int\Psi_{\pm}(t,s)\exp(-2\pi i(\xi,\eta)\cdot A^{-1}(t,s))dsdt.

Remark 8.9
.

We worked with these modified Fourier transforms because it allowed us to keep track of the constants after Poisson summation more easily.

Applying Poisson summation, we get the following result.

Theorem 8.10
.

The completed regular elliptic part is

\frac{\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{2^{3n-1}}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\sum_{Y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}}\sum_{\mu\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}/4{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}}^{{}^{\prime}}\binom{\mu^{2}-4{u(Y)}\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}\\
\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\sum_{\eta\in\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}^{1}(\xi,\eta)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}^{1}(\xi,\eta)\right).

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Propositions
8.6
and
8.7
.
∎

Concretely, the Poisson Summation that has been performed is

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}\left(A(r,y)\right)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}\left(A(r,y)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\sum_{\eta\in\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}}\frac{1}{2^{3n-1}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}^{1}(\xi,\eta)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}^{1}(\xi,\eta)\right).

Notice that the right hand side of the above equation is absolutely convergent.
Indeed, because
\Phi_{\pm}
and
\Psi_{\pm}
are smooth and of compact support, by Proposition
6.4
, the bounds

\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\Phi_{\pm}^{1}(\xi,\eta)\right\rvert}\ll\dfrac{1}{(1+\mathinner{\!\left\lvert(\xi,\eta)\right\rvert})^{N}}\quad\text{
and }\quad\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\Psi_{\pm}^{1}(\xi,\eta)\right\rvert}\ll\dfrac{1}{(1+\mathinner{\!\left\lvert(\xi,\eta)\right\rvert})^{N}},

hold for any
N
.
If
N
is big enough these imply the absolute convergence.
In particular, we can rearrange the interior sum on Theorem
8.10
in any way without changing its value.
In other words, the sum of the aforementioned theorem is the sum of

\frac{\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{2^{3n-1}}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\sum_{Y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}}\sum_{\mu\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}/4{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}}^{{}^{\prime}}\binom{\mu^{2}-4{u(Y)}\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}\\
\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}^{1}(0,0)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}^{1}(0,0)\right).

and

\frac{\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{2^{3n-1}}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\sum_{Y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}}\sum_{\mu\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}/4{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}}^{{}^{\prime}}\binom{\mu^{2}-4{u(Y)}\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}\\
\sum_{(\xi,\eta)\neq(0,0)}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}^{1}(\xi,\eta)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}^{1}(\xi,\eta)\right).

In particular, we have not reordered the sums. We have just distributed the outer sums over the inner one. Our analysis will now focus only on the sum obtained when
(\xi,\eta)=0
. This is the same that is done in
[
4
]
, where the corresponding zero term of Poisson summation is analyzed. The rest are studied in
[
1
]
with different means.

Notation
.

We write
\overline{\Sigma_{0}}(f)
to denote the expression obtained in Theorem
8.10
with
\xi=\eta=0
.

Note that the term

\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}^{1}(0,0)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})}+\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})\Psi_{\pm}^{1}(0,0)\right)

is independent of the value of
Y
and
\mu
.
Thus, we can reorder and simplify the expression.

Proposition 8.11
.

With notation as above

\overline{\Sigma_{0}}(f)=\frac{\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{2^{3n-1}}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}^{1}(0,0)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{3}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{2}}+\frac{\Psi_{\pm}^{1}(0,0)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})}\right)\left(\sum_{Y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}}\sum_{\mu\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}/4{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}}^{{}^{\prime}}\binom{\mu^{2}-4{u(Y)}\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}\right).

To further simplify notation, we introduce the following definition.

Definition 8.12
.

Given ideals
{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
and a unit
u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}
, define the
Kloosterman-type sum
as

K_{{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}}(u)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sum_{\mu\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}/4{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}}^{{}^{\prime}}\binom{\mu^{2}-4u\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{d}}}{{\mathfrak{a}}}.

Remark 8.13
.

The above definition requires no hypothesis on
K
.

Proposition
8.11
can now be rewritten as follows.

Theorem 8.14
.

With notation as before,

\overline{\Sigma_{0}}(f)=\frac{\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{2^{3n-1}}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{Y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}^{1}(0,0)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{3}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{2}}+\frac{\Psi_{\pm}^{1}(0,0)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})}\right)K_{{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}}(\pm u(Y)).

Proof.

This follows from the above discussion.
We only notice that we can exchange the
Y
and the
{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}
sum because the former sum is finite.
∎

Our goal in the following section is to evaluate these sums precisely.
Though the sum over
Y
is straightforward, the Kloosterman-type sum over
\mu
presents challenges.

9.
Evaluation of Kloosterman-type Sums

The evaluation of the Kloosterman-type sums is central to the paper, but the calculations are long and technical.
For ease of following the argument, we have deferred them to Appendix
A
.
Here, we only state the main result.

Throughout this section we invoke the following hypothesis.

(Hyp-split)

Let
K
be a number field such that 2 splits completely in
K
.

Let
{\mathfrak{p}}_{1},\dots,{\mathfrak{p}}_{n}
be the primes above
2
in
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
, that is,

2{\mathcal{O}}_{K}={\mathfrak{p}}_{1}\cdots{\mathfrak{p}}_{n}.

By assumption,
K_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}}\cong{\mathbb{Q}}_{2}
.
We assume
\pi_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}}
corresponds to
2
under the above isomorphism.
This fixes the uniformizer uniquely as there is only one field isomorphism
\mathbb{Q}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Q}
.

Remark 9.1
.

The splitting condition at the prime 2 is a technical hypothesis we need to impose to evaluate the Kloosterman-type sums.
We believe that the main result of this section should be true in general.
We note that in this section we do not require the hypothesis that
K
is totally real.

Definition 9.2
.

Let
u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}
be a unit and let
{\mathfrak{d}}
and
{\mathfrak{a}}
be arbitrary ideals of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
.
For a complex parameter
z=\sigma+it
with
\sigma>1
, define

{\mathbb{D}}(z,u)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2z+1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{K_{{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}}(u)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{z+1}}.

The main result of this section is the following theorem.
We provide a proof of this result in Appendix
A
.

Theorem 9.3
.

Let
K
be a number field satisfying
(Hyp-split)
and let
({\mathfrak{p}},k)
be the pair of prime ideal and corresponding positive integer satisfying
(Hyp-div)
fixed in §
3.1
.
Write
\mathsf{p}=\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{p}})
.
Let
u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}
be a unit.
For a complex parameter
z
with
\Re(z)>1
,

{\mathbb{D}}(z,u)=4^{n}\frac{\zeta_{K}(2z)}{\zeta_{K}(z+1)}\cdot\frac{1-1/{\mathsf{p}}^{z(k+1)}}{1-1/{\mathsf{p}}^{z}}.

In particular,
{\mathbb{D}}(z,u)
is independent of
u
.
Moreover, it admits an analytic continuation to a meromorphic function in the whole complex plane with a pole at
z=\frac{1}{2}
with residue
\frac{2^{2n+r_{1}}h_{K}R_{K}}{\zeta_{K}(3/2)\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}}\omega_{K}}\frac{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-(k+1)/2}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-1/2}}
using the terms detailed in Proposition
3.14
.

Notation
.

In Theorem
9.3
we proved that
{\mathbb{D}}(z,u(Y))
is independent of
u(Y)
.
To avoid confusion, we will henceforth drop the dependence on
u
from the notation.

10.
Isolation of the Contribution of the Trivial and Special Representations

In this section of the paper, the goal is to isolate the contribution of the special representation and finish the proof of the main theorem.
Recall from Theorem
8.14
that the dominant term is,

\overline{\Sigma_{0}}(f)=\frac{\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{2^{3n-1}}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{Y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}^{1}(0,0)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{3}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{2}}+\frac{\Psi_{\pm}^{1}(0,0)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})}\right)K_{{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}}(\pm u(Y)).

At this point it is worthwhile to compute the constant in front of the sum more explicitly.
We have

\frac{\kappa\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{2^{3n-1}}=\frac{2^{n-1}h_{K}R_{K}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}}\cdot\frac{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{2^{3n-1}}=\dfrac{h_{K}R_{K}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{4^{n}},

and thus the dominant term is

(15)

\overline{\Sigma_{0}}(f)=\dfrac{h_{K}R_{K}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{4^{n}}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{Y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}^{1}(0,0)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{3}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{2}}+\frac{\Psi_{\pm}^{1}(0,0)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})}\right)K_{{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}}(\pm u(Y)).

10.1.
Some algebraic number theory and a discussion

Let
\gamma\in\GL(2,K)
be a regular elliptic matrix with trace
\tau
and determinant
\delta
.
For

(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})\in G_{\infty},

where
\gamma_{i}
is the embedding of
\gamma
in the
i
-th real embedding of
K
, define

C(\gamma)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=C(\CH((\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}))),

where
C
is the indicator function defined in Definition
3.30
.

Lemma 10.1
.

The following equality holds

\lim_{z\to 0}\frac{L_{\infty}(z,\chi_{\gamma})}{L_{\infty}(1-z,\chi_{\gamma})}\frac{\zeta_{K}(2z)}{\zeta_{K}(z+1)}=-C(\gamma)\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}2^{n-1}

where
L_{\infty}(z,\chi_{\gamma})=\prod_{\nu}L_{\nu}(z,\chi_{\gamma})
and
\nu
varies over all real places.

Notation
.

In this proof we write
\chi
in place of
\chi_{\gamma}
for ease of notation.

Proof.

Recall that for a totally real field
K
, the completed Dedekind zeta function is defined as

\xi_{K}(z)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{z/2}L_{{\mathbb{R}}}(z)^{n}\zeta_{K}(z).

Then

\frac{\xi_{K}(2z)}{\xi_{K}(z+1)}=\frac{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{z}L_{\mathbb{R}}(2z)^{n}\zeta_{K}(2z)}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{(z+1)/2}L_{\mathbb{R}}(z+1)^{n}{\zeta_{K}(z+1)}}=\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{(z-1)/2}\frac{L_{\mathbb{R}}(2z)^{n}\zeta_{K}(2z)}{L_{\mathbb{R}}(z+1)^{n}\zeta_{K}(z+1)}

whose limit at
z=0
is equal to
-1/2
.
Rearranging terms we write

\frac{\zeta_{K}(2z)}{\zeta_{K}(z+1)}=\frac{\xi_{K}(2z)}{\xi_{K}(z+1)}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{(1-z)/2}\frac{L_{\mathbb{R}}(z+1)^{n}}{L_{\mathbb{R}}(2z)^{n}}.

We can therefore obtain

\frac{L_{\infty}(z,\chi)}{L_{\infty}(1-z,\chi)}\frac{\zeta_{K}(2z)}{\zeta_{K}(z+1)}=\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{(1-z)/2}\prod_{\nu}\frac{L_{\mathbb{R}}(z+\delta_{\nu})L_{\mathbb{R}}(z+1)}{L_{\mathbb{R}}(1-z+\delta_{\nu})L_{\mathbb{R}}(2z)}\frac{\xi_{K}(2z)}{\xi_{K}(z+1)},

where as before,
\delta_{\nu}=\delta_{\nu,\gamma}=1
if
\chi
is ramified at
\nu
and 0 otherwise.
As
z\to 0
, we have

\lim_{z\to 0}\frac{L_{\infty}(z,\chi)}{L_{\infty}(1-z,\chi)}\frac{\zeta_{K}(2z)}{\zeta_{K}(z+1)}=-\frac{1}{2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}\lim_{z\to 0}\left(\prod_{\nu}\frac{L_{\mathbb{R}}(z+\delta_{\nu})L_{\mathbb{R}}(z)}{L_{\mathbb{R}}(1-z+\delta_{\nu})L_{\mathbb{R}}(2z)}\right),

and it remains to compute the limit explicitly.
We have

\displaystyle\lim_{z\to 0}\frac{L_{\mathbb{R}}(z+\delta_{\nu})L_{\mathbb{R}}(1+z)}{L_{\mathbb{R}}(2z)L_{\mathbb{R}}(1-z+\delta_{\nu})}

\displaystyle=\lim_{z\to 0}\pi^{z}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{z+\delta_{\nu}}{2})\Gamma(\frac{z+1}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{1-z+\delta_{\nu}}{2})\Gamma(z)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{z\to 0}2^{1-z}\pi^{z+1/2}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{z+\delta_{\nu}}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{1-z+\delta_{\nu}}{2})\Gamma(\frac{z}{2})}=\begin{cases}2,&\textrm{ if }\delta=0\\
0,&\textrm{ if }\delta=1\end{cases}

Altogether then

\lim_{z\to 0}\frac{L_{\infty}(z,\chi)}{L_{\infty}(1-z,\chi)}\frac{\zeta_{K}(2z)}{\zeta_{K}(z+1)}=-\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}2^{n-1}

when
\chi
is unramified
at every real place
and
0
otherwise.
By the Theorem
5.11
, this dichotomy is equivalent to
C(\gamma)=1
or
0
, respectively.
∎

From the above and proposition (
5.12
) we get immediately

Corollary 10.2
.

The following limit holds for any
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n-1}

\lim_{z\to 0}\frac{L_{\infty}(z,\chi)}{L_{\infty}(1-z,\chi)}\frac{\zeta_{K}(2z)}{\zeta_{K}(z+1)}=-C(x,y)\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}2^{n-1}.

Remark 10.3
.

Recall that the
L
-factors at infinity only depend on the regions, i.e. the behaviour at infinity, and thus we can disregard the dependence on any particular
\gamma
.

10.2.
Rewriting the dominant term

We do this calculation in two steps.
First, we rewrite the
\Phi^{1}_{\pm}(0,0)
-part:

(16)

\begin{split}&\dfrac{h_{K}R_{K}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{4^{n}}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{Y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\pm}^{1}(0,0)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{3}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{2}}\right)K_{{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}}(\pm u(Y))\\
&=\dfrac{h_{K}R_{K}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{4^{n}}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{Y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{3}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\\
&\qquad\qquad\int\int\theta^{\pm}(x,y)F\left(\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}}){\mathsf{p}}^{-k\alpha}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{\alpha}}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)^{2\alpha}\right)\left(\frac{K_{{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}}(\pm u(Y))}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{2}}\right)dydx\\
&=\dfrac{h_{K}R_{K}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{4^{n}}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{Y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{3}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{K_{{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}}(\pm u(Y))}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{2}}\\
&\qquad\qquad\times\left(\int\int\frac{\theta^{\pm}(x,y)}{2\pi i}\int_{\Re{(z)}=1}\widetilde{F}(z)\left(\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}}){\mathsf{p}}^{-k\alpha}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{\alpha}}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)^{2\alpha}\right)^{-z}dzdydx\right)\\
&=\dfrac{h_{K}R_{K}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{4^{n}}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{Y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}}\left(\int\int\frac{\theta^{\pm}(x,y)}{2\pi i}\int_{\Re{(z)}=1}\widetilde{F}(z)\left(\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-k\alpha}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{\alpha}}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)^{2\alpha}\right)^{-z}\right.\\
&\qquad\qquad\left.\times\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2(z+1)+1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{K_{{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}}(\pm u(Y))}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{(z+1)+1}}dzdydx\right)\\
&=\dfrac{h_{K}R_{K}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{4^{n}}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{Y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}}\left(\int\int\frac{\theta^{\pm}(x,y)}{2\pi i}\int_{\Re{(z)}=1}\widetilde{F}(z)\left(\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-k\alpha}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{\alpha}}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)^{2\alpha}\right)^{-z}{\mathbb{D}}(z+1,u)dzdydx\right)\\
&=\dfrac{h_{K}R_{K}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{2^{n+1}}\sum_{\pm}\left(\int\int\frac{\theta^{\pm}(x,y)}{2\pi i}\int_{\Re{(z)}=1}\widetilde{F}(z)\left(\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-k\alpha}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{\alpha}}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)^{2\alpha}\right)^{-z}{\mathbb{D}}(z+1,u)dzdydx\right).\end{split}

The last line is obtained by by evaluating the sum over
Y\in\mathbb{F}^{n-1}_{2}
.
Note that after evaluating the Kloosterman-type sums, nothing in the integrals depends on
Y
.
Similarly for the second part of the sum, we have

\begin{split}&\dfrac{h_{K}R_{K}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{4^{n}}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{Y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\left(\frac{\Psi_{\pm}^{1}(0,0)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})}\right)K_{{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}}(\pm u(Y))\\
&=\dfrac{h_{K}R_{K}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{4^{n}}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{Y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{3}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\left(\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})K_{{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}}(\pm u(Y))}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{2}}\Psi_{\pm}^{1}(0,0)\right)\\
&=\dfrac{h_{K}R_{K}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{4^{n}}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{Y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{3}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\left(\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})K_{{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}}(\pm u(Y))}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{2}}\right)\\
&\quad\times\int\int\theta^{\pm}(x,y)\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{-1/2}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}H_{\gamma}\left(1,\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}}){\mathsf{p}}^{-k(1-\alpha)}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1-\alpha}}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)^{2(1-\alpha)}\right)dydx\\
&=\dfrac{h_{K}R_{K}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{4^{n}}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{Y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{3}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\left(\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})K_{{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}}(\pm u(Y))}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{2}}\right)\\
&\times\int\int\frac{\theta^{\pm}(x,y)}{2\pi i}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{-1/2}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\int_{\Re(z)=1}\widetilde{F}(z)\left(\frac{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}}){\mathsf{p}}^{-k(1-\alpha)}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1-\alpha}}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)^{2(1-\alpha)}\right)^{-z}\\
&\hskip 307.14749pt\frac{L_{\infty}(z,\chi)}{L_{\infty}(1-z,\chi)}dzdydx\\
&=\dfrac{h_{K}R_{K}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{4^{n}}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{Y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}}\int\int\frac{\theta^{\pm}(x,y)}{2\pi i}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{-1/2}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\\
&\hskip 72.26999pt\int_{\Re(z)=1}\widetilde{F}(z)\left(\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-k(1-\alpha)}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1-\alpha}}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)^{2(1-\alpha)}\right)^{-z}{}\frac{L_{\infty}(z,\chi)}{L_{\infty}(1-z,\chi)}{\mathbb{D}}(z)dzdydx.\end{split}

Once again, we apply Theorem
9.3
to the above expression and evaluating the sum over
Y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-1}
.
This cancels the
2^{3n-1}
in the denominator and the above expression simplifies to

(17)

\dfrac{h_{K}R_{K}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{-1/2}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k}}{2^{n+1}}\sum_{\pm}\int\int\frac{\theta^{\pm}(x,y)}{2\pi i}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)\\
\times\int_{\Re(z)=1}\widetilde{F}(z)\left(\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-k(1-\alpha)}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1-\alpha}}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)^{2(1-\alpha)}\right)^{-z}\frac{L_{\infty}(z,\chi)}{L_{\infty}(1-z,\chi)}\mathbb{D}(z,u)dzdydx.

Note that (
15
) is the sum of (
16
) and (
17
).
However, we write the summands separately as we work with each piece one-at-a-time.
The trivial representation will come from (
16
), whereas the special representation will come from (
17
) after contour shifting.
In both cases, they will arise from the residue at
z=0
.

10.3.
Calculating the residue

While our main interest is the residue at
z=0
, we shall follow
[
4
]
to shift past the line
\Re(z)=-1/2
.
More precisely, we shift the first contour to the line
\mathcal{C}_{1}
defined by
\Re(z)=\sigma
for
-1<\sigma<-1/2
, and the second to the line
\mathcal{C}_{2}
given by the line
\Re(z)=\sigma
for
-1/2<\sigma<0
.

Lemma 10.4
.

The expression (
16
) is equal to the sum of

(18)

\displaystyle 2^{n-1}h_{K}R_{K}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\frac{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-(k+1)}}{1-{{\mathsf{p}}^{-1}}}\sum_{\pm}\int\int\theta^{\pm}(x,y)dydx,

(19)

\displaystyle 2^{n-1}h_{K}R_{K}\dfrac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\kappa}{\zeta_{K}(3/2)}\frac{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-(k+1)/2}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-1/2}}\widetilde{F}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\right)\sum_{\pm}\left(\int\int{\theta^{\pm}(x,y)}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-k\alpha}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{\alpha}}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)^{2\alpha}\right)^{1/2}dydx\right),\textrm{ and }

(20)

\displaystyle\frac{h_{K}R_{K}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{2^{n+1}}\sum_{\pm}\left(\int\int\frac{\theta^{\pm}(x,y)}{2\pi i}\int_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}\widetilde{F}(z)\left(\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-k\alpha}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{\alpha}}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)^{2\alpha}\right)^{-z}{\mathbb{D}}(z+1)dzdydx\right).

Proof.

The constant in front of (
16
) is
\dfrac{h_{K}R_{K}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}}{2^{n+1}}
.

To shift the contour of integration to the line
\mathcal{C}_{1}
, we describe the poles in the region that is traversed.
The integral over
(x,y)
can be taken over the regular set, so that
\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)
is finite and non-zero.
The Mellin transform
\widetilde{F}(z)
has a simple pole at
z=0
with residue equal to
1
.
So, the total residue at
z=0
is equal to (
18
).

The auxiliary Dirichlet series
{\mathbb{D}}(z)
has a simple pole at
z=1/2
with residue equal to

(21)

\lim_{z\rightarrow 1/2}(z-1/2){\mathbb{D}}(z)=\lim_{z\rightarrow 1/2}(z-1/2)4^{n}\frac{\zeta_{K}(2z)}{\zeta_{K}(z+1)}\cdot\frac{1-1/{\mathsf{p}}^{z(k+1)}}{1-1/{\mathsf{p}}^{z}}=\frac{4^{n}\kappa}{\zeta_{K}(3/2)}\frac{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-(k+1)/2}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-1/2}}.

By a change of variables, we have the same for
{\mathbb{D}}(z+1)
at
z=-1/2
.
Thus the total residue there is equal to (
19
).
Notice that the residue at
z=-1/2
of the quotient of zeta functions is

\displaystyle\lim_{z\longrightarrow-1/2}(z+1/2)\dfrac{\zeta_{K}(2z+2)}{\zeta_{K}(z+2)}=\dfrac{\kappa}{\zeta_{K}(3/2)},

which explains the constant in front of equation (
19
).
The two residues together yield the main term; the remainder (
20
) is described by the integral over
\mathcal{C}_{1}
.
∎

Lemma 10.5
.

The expression (
17
) is equal to the sum of the following three expressions

(22)

\displaystyle-4^{n-1}h_{K}R_{K}(k+1){\mathsf{p}}^{-k}\sum_{\pm}\int\int\theta^{\pm}(x,y)\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)C(x,y)dxdy,

(23)

\displaystyle 2^{n-1}h_{K}R_{K}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\frac{\kappa}{\zeta_{K}(3/2)}\frac{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-(k+1)/2}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-1/2}}\widetilde{F}\left(\dfrac{1}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 108.405pt\times\sum_{\pm}\int\int\theta^{\pm}(x,y)\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)\left(\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-k\alpha}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{\alpha}}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)^{2\alpha}\right)^{1/2}dydx,\textrm{
and }

(24)

\displaystyle\dfrac{h_{K}R_{K}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{-1/2}}{2^{n+1}}\sum_{\pm}\int\int\frac{\theta^{\pm}(x,y)}{2\pi i}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 93.95122pt\times\int_{\mathcal{C}_{2}}\widetilde{F}(z)\left(\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-k(1-\alpha)}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1-\alpha}}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)^{2(1-\alpha)}\right)^{-z}\frac{L_{\infty}(z,\chi)}{L_{\infty}(1-z,\chi)}{\mathbb{D}}(z)dzdydx.

Proof.

Shifting the
dz
contour of integration to the left, we see that at
z=1/2
the Dirichlet series
{\mathbb{D}}(z)
has a pole with the same residue (
21
), so the contribution there is equal to

(25)

\dfrac{4^{n}h_{K}R_{K}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{-1/2}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k}}{2^{n+1}}\times\frac{\kappa}{\zeta_{K}(3/2)}\times\frac{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-(k+1)/2}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-1/2}}\widetilde{F}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\\
\times\sum_{\pm}\int\int\theta^{\pm}(x,y)\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)\left(\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-k(1-\alpha)}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1-\alpha}}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)^{2(1-\alpha)}\right)^{-1/2}dydx.

Notice that

{\mathsf{p}}^{-k}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)\left(\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-k(1-\alpha)}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1-\alpha}}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)^{2(1-\alpha)}\right)^{-1/2}={\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}\dfrac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-k\alpha/2}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)^{\alpha}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{\alpha/2}}.

Thus the residue (
25
) can be rewritten as

2^{n-1}h_{K}R_{K}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\frac{\kappa}{\zeta_{K}(3/2)}\frac{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-(k+1)/2}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-1/2}}\widetilde{F}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\sum_{\pm}\int\int\theta^{\pm}(x,y)\left(\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-k\alpha}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{\alpha}}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)^{2\alpha}\right)^{1/2}dydx.

This is (
23
) above.
Then shifting the contour past to
z=0
, we note that

\frac{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-z(k+1)}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-z}}=\sum_{n=0}^{k}{\mathsf{p}}^{-nz},

which equals
k+1
at
z=0
.
Recall from Lemma
10.1
that

\lim_{z\to 0}\frac{L_{\infty}(z,\chi)}{L_{\infty}(1-z,\chi)}\frac{\zeta_{K}(2z)}{\zeta_{K}(z+1)}=-C(x,y)\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}2^{n-1}

and that the residue at
z=0
of
\widetilde{F}(z)
is
1
.
Thus the residue at
z=0
is

\dfrac{h_{K}R_{K}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{-1/2}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k}}{2^{n+1}}\sum_{\pm}\int\int\theta^{\pm}(x,y)\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)\cdot(-C(x,y)\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D_{K}\right\rvert}^{1/2}2^{n-1})\cdot(k+1)dydx,

which becomes

-\dfrac{4^{n}h_{K}R_{K}(k+1){\mathsf{p}}^{-k}}{4}\sum_{\pm}\int\int\theta^{\pm}(x,y)\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)\cdot C(x,y)dydx,

Thus the total residue is given by (
22
) as desired.
The two residues together yield the main term, while the remainder (
24
) is described by the integral over
\mathcal{C}_{2}
.
Once more, the
4^{n}
appears precisely because we have put
{\mathbb{D}}(z)
again.
∎

Recall that
\widetilde{F}
is an odd function which allows us to conclude that (
19
) + (
23
) = 0.
We obtain the following result.

Proposition 10.6
.

The dual sum (
15
) is equal to
\eqref{eq:triv res new}+\eqref{eq:spec residue2new}+\eqref{eq:C(1)new}+\eqref{C2new}
.

11.
Computation of the trace of the trivial and the special representations

In this section, we compute the traces of the representations we desire to cancel; namely the trivial representation and the special representations.
The core of the argument lies the computing the trace at the archimedean places.

11.1.
The main statement

We begin with the following

Definition 11.1
.

The special representation
\xi
is the induced representation from the Borel subgroup
B({\mathbb{A}})
of the trivial representation.

Remark 11.2
.

In
[
31
,
29
]
the special representation is reached at in a different way.
The difference stems from how the Jacquet–Langlands trace formula is constructed in
[
31
]
.
For our purposes, it suffices to know that the special representation enters the Jacquet–Langlands trace formula in the so-called term (vi) in
[
24
, §16]
, where it is verified to be a constant factor of the trace of the above defined
\xi
.

Our main result of this section states the trace of the trivial and the special representations.
We have

Theorem 11.3
.

Let
f
be the fixed test function.
Then

(i)

The trace of the trivial representation is

\Tr({\bf 1}(f))={{\mathsf{p}}}^{-k/2}R_{K}\frac{1-{{\mathsf{p}}}^{-(k+1)}}{1-{{\mathsf{p}}}^{-1}}\sum_{\pm}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2n-1}}\theta^{\pm}(x,y)dxdy,

which equals (
18
)

(ii)

The trace of the special representation is

\Tr(\xi(f))=(k+1){\mathsf{p}}^{-k}2^{n-2}R_{K}\sum_{\pm}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2n-1}}C(x,y)\theta^{\pm}(x,y)\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)dxdy,

which is
-1/2
times (
22
).

In these equations,
\theta^{\pm}
is the interpolation function defined in §
4.2
and
C(x,y)
is the indicator function defined in Definition
3.30
.

As we will see, the main computational task is the calculation of the archimedean part of the traces.
Consequently, we begin by proving the general statements involved.

11.2.
Integral and measure calculations

11.2.1.
Change of variables in the regions

Recall that we have defined the following maps in Section
3.7

\displaystyle\CH\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\ch\otimes\cdots\otimes\ch\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}G_{\infty}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times\cdots\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}

\displaystyle\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n}

\displaystyle\longmapsto(r_{1},N_{1},\dots,r_{n},N_{n}).

Restricted to the tori representatives, we have
\CH(T_{J})=S_{J}
and it is a
2\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}1
covering map.
Each tori carries a product measure induced by the measures in
T_{\el}
and
T_{\spl}
.
Thus to understand the change of measures under this map it is enough to do it for
\GL(2,{\mathbb{R}})
.

Proposition 11.4
.

Let
T
be one of
T_{\el}
or
T_{\spl}
, and
S
be the corresponding region.
Then

\int_{T}h(\ch(t))dt=2\displaystyle\int_{S}h(r,N)\dfrac{drdN}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N\right\rvert}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert r^{2}-N\right\rvert}^{1/2}},

where
h
is any integrable function on
S
.

Proof.

The
2
that appears in front of the integral is because the map
\ch
is a
2\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}1
covering map.
The rest is a calculation with differential forms.

For the split torus we have that

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=\alpha+\beta,

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle=4\alpha\beta.

Taking derivatives we obtain

\displaystyle dr

\displaystyle=d\alpha+d\beta,

\displaystyle dN

\displaystyle=4\left(\beta d\alpha+\alpha d\beta\right).

Thus,

dr\wedge dN=4(\alpha-\beta)d\alpha\wedge d\beta.

Using that
4\alpha\beta=N
and that
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\alpha-\beta\right\rvert}^{2}=\mathinner{\!\left\lvert r^{2}-N\right\rvert}
, we get

\dfrac{d\alpha\wedge d\beta}{\alpha\beta}=\dfrac{dr\wedge dN}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N\right\rvert}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert r^{2}-N\right\rvert}^{1/2}},

as desired.

For the elliptic torus, we have

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=2a

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle=4(a^{2}+b^{2}).

Taking derivatives we get

\displaystyle dr

\displaystyle=2da

\displaystyle dN

\displaystyle=8(ada+bdb).

This implies

dr\wedge dN=16da\wedge(ada+bdb)=16bda\wedge db.

Notice that

b^{2}=\dfrac{N}{4}-a^{2}=\dfrac{N-4a^{2}}{4}=\dfrac{N-r^{2}}{4}.

We conclude

b=\dfrac{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert r^{2}-N\right\rvert}^{1/2}}{2}.

Substituting this we obtain

\dfrac{2da\wedge db}{a^{2}+b^{2}}=\dfrac{drdN}{N\mathinner{\!\left\lvert r^{2}-N\right\rvert}^{1/2}}

Taking absolute values to recover the measure associated to the differential form finishes the proof.
∎

The following corollary is an immediate consequence of the above result.

Corollary 11.5
.

Let
j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\in\{{\spl},{\el}\}
and
J=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{n})
.
Then

\int_{T_{J}}h(\CH(t))dt=2^{n}\displaystyle\int_{S_{J}}h(r_{1},N_{1},\ldots,r_{n},N_{n})\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{dr_{i}dN_{i}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{i}\right\rvert}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert r_{i}^{2}-N_{i}\right\rvert}^{1/2}},

where
h
is any integrable function on
S_{J}
.

11.2.2.
Fundamental domain of the action of
Z_{+}
and quotient integration

Recall that
Z_{+}
acts on
G_{\infty}
by matrix multiplication.
Each torus is invariant under this action.
As we have seen, there is a corresponding action in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}
given by

z\cdot(r_{1},N_{1},\ldots,r_{n},N_{n})=(zr_{1},z^{2}N_{1},\ldots,zr_{n},z^{2}N_{n}).

Each region
S_{J}
is invariant under this action.

Definition 11.6
.

Let
j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\in\{{\spl},{\el}\}
and
J=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{n})
.
Define

{\mathcal{F}}_{J}^{\pm}=S_{J}\cap\{N_{1}=\pm 1\}.

These sets will allow us to do the integration under the action of
Z_{+}
.
The reason is the following result.

Proposition 11.7
.

With notation as above,
{\mathcal{F}}_{J}^{\pm}
is a fundamental domain of the action of
Z_{+}
on
S_{J}
.

Proof.

Let
(r_{1},N_{1},\ldots,r_{n},N_{n})\in S_{J}
and define
z=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{1}\right\rvert}}}
.
Then, under the action of
Z_{+}
, we have

z\cdot(r_{1},N_{1},\ldots,r_{n},N_{n})=(zr_{1},z^{2}N_{1},\ldots,zr_{n},z^{2}N_{n})=\left(\dfrac{r_{1}}{\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{1}\right\rvert}}},\dfrac{N_{1}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{1}\right\rvert}},\ldots,\dfrac{r_{n}}{\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{1}\right\rvert}}},\dfrac{N_{n}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{1}\right\rvert}}\right).

The latter point is in
{\mathcal{F}}_{J}^{\pm}
.
It is clear no two such points are equivalent under
Z_{+}
.
∎

Integrating in a quotient space is the same as integrating on one of its fundamental domains.
We shall use in the regions
S_{J}
the measures obtained in Corollary
11.5
, We need to understand integration of functions in this fundamental domain.

Lemma 11.8
.

Let
j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\in\{{\spl},{\el}\}
and
J=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{n})
.
Let
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}R_{J}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
be an integrable function invariant under
Z_{+}
.
Then

\int_{Z_{+}\backslash R_{J}}\phi(\dot{r})\dfrac{dr}{dz}=int_{{\mathcal{F}}_{J}^{\pm}}\phi\left(r_{1},\pm 1,r_{2},N_{2},\ldots,r_{n},N_{n}\right)\dfrac{dr_{1}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert r_{1}^{2}\pm 1\right\rvert}^{1/2}}\prod_{i=2}^{n}\dfrac{dr_{i}dN_{i}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{i}\right\rvert}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert r_{i}^{2}-N_{i}\right\rvert}^{1/2}}.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.26
,
\left(r_{1},\pm 1,r_{2},N_{2},\ldots,r_{n},N_{n}\right)\in{\mathcal{F}}_{J}
are coordinates for the manifold
Z_{+}\backslash S_{J}
.
Thus, the current lemma states how to integrate in coordinates.

We have to integrate over a product domain (of integration) which in the direction of the variable
N_{1}
integrates to
1
.
This product domain is

(1,e)\times{\mathcal{F}}_{J}^{\pm}.

As we will see, integrating over this domain allows us to uncouple the variable
N_{1}
appropriately.
Indeed, we have

(26)

\int_{1}^{e}\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{F}}_{J}^{\pm}}\phi\left(\dfrac{r_{1}}{\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{1}\right\rvert}}},\dfrac{N_{1}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{1}\right\rvert}},\ldots,\dfrac{r_{n}}{\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{1}\right\rvert}}},\dfrac{N_{n}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{1}\right\rvert}}\right)\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{dr_{i}dN_{i}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{i}\right\rvert}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert r_{i}^{2}-N_{i}\right\rvert}^{1/2}},

where the outer integral is over
N_{1}
.
Consider the change of variables, in the inner integral, given by

\displaystyle r_{j}

\displaystyle=\tilde{r}_{j}\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{1}\right\rvert}},j=1,\ldots,n

\displaystyle N_{j}

\displaystyle=\tilde{N}_{j}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{1}\right\rvert},j=2,\ldots,n.

This depends on the fixed value of
N_{1}
, which is the outer integral variable.
Taking derivatives we get

\displaystyle dr_{j}

\displaystyle=d\tilde{r}_{j}\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{1}\right\rvert}},j=1,\ldots,n

\displaystyle dN_{j}

\displaystyle=d\tilde{N}_{j}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{1}\right\rvert},j=2,\ldots,n.

By direct calculation, we get that for
j=2,\ldots,n

\displaystyle dr_{j}dN_{j}

\displaystyle=d\tilde{r}_{j}d\tilde{N}_{j}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{1}\right\rvert}^{3/2},

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{j}\right\rvert}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert r_{j}^{2}-N_{j}\right\rvert}^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\tilde{N}_{j}\right\rvert}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\tilde{r}_{j}^{2}-\tilde{N}_{j}\right\rvert}^{1/2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{1}\right\rvert}^{3/2}.

Consequently, the factor
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{1}\right\rvert}^{3/2}
cancels and the quotient

\dfrac{dr_{j}dN_{j}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{j}\right\rvert}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert r_{j}^{2}-N_{j}\right\rvert}^{1/2}},

is invariant for
j=2,\ldots,n
.
For
j=1
we have

\displaystyle\dfrac{dr_{1}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert r_{1}^{2}-N_{1}\right\rvert}^{1/2}}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{d\tilde{r}_{1}\sqrt{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{1}\right\rvert}}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{1}\right\rvert}^{1/2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert(r_{1}/\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{1}\right\rvert}^{1/2})^{2}-N_{1}/\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{1}\right\rvert}\right\rvert}^{1/2}}=\dfrac{d\tilde{r}_{1}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\tilde{r}_{1}^{2}\pm 1\right\rvert}^{1/2}}.

The
\pm
depends on which part of the fundamental domain
{\mathcal{F}}_{J}^{\pm}
we are integrating over.
As a consequence, if we relabel the variables again without tildes, we have that integral (
26
) equals

\int_{{\mathcal{F}}_{J}^{\pm}}\phi\left(r_{1},\pm 1,r_{2},N_{2},\ldots,r_{n},N_{n}\right)\dfrac{dr_{1}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert r_{1}^{2}\pm 1\right\rvert}^{1/2}}\displaystyle\prod_{i=2}^{n}\dfrac{dr_{i}dN_{i}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{i}\right\rvert}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert r_{i}^{2}-N_{i}\right\rvert}^{1/2}}\cdot\displaystyle\int_{1}^{e}\dfrac{dN_{1}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{1}\right\rvert}}.

The latter integral over
N_{1}
equals
1
.
Thus integral (
26
) equals

\int_{{\mathcal{F}}_{J}^{\pm}}\phi\left(r_{1},\pm 1,r_{2},N_{2},\ldots,r_{n},N_{n}\right)\dfrac{dr_{1}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert r_{1}^{2}\pm 1\right\rvert}^{1/2}}\displaystyle\prod_{i=2}^{n}\dfrac{dr_{i}dN_{i}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{i}\right\rvert}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert r_{i}^{2}-N_{i}\right\rvert}^{1/2}}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

11.3.
The Weyl integration formula in coordinates

We now establish the Weyl integration formula in coordinates, which is the technical tool to prove our main result.
We shall first carry out all the technical manipulations and then we shall discuss our motivations for the whole process.
We defer the motivation because we would need to introduce the notation anyway in order to discuss it.

11.3.1.
The integral over the quotient tori

We begin by understanding the Weyl discriminant as it appears in the Weyl integration formula.
The factor that appears in the Weyl integration formula is

\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D(t)\right\rvert}=\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\right\rvert}^{1/2}=\dfrac{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}\right\rvert}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\right\rvert}^{1/2}},

where
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
are the corresponding eigenvalues of the torus element
t
.
Changing to the
(r,N)
coordinates this becomes

2\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\dfrac{r^{2}}{N}-1\right\rvert}^{1/2}.

Under the action of the center
Z_{+}
this is invariant.
Indeed,

2\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\dfrac{(zr)^{2}}{z^{2}N}-1\right\rvert}^{1/2}=2\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\dfrac{r^{2}}{N}-1\right\rvert}^{1/2}.

This observation implies that if, for some index
J
, the function
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}S_{J}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
is invariant under the action of
Z_{+}
then so is

2\phi(r,N)\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\dfrac{r^{2}}{N}-1\right\rvert}^{1/2}.

The same discussion applies for the tori of
G_{\infty}
because their Weyl discriminant is the product of those of the component tori.
Consequently, Lemma
11.8
can be used to compute its integral on the quotient space.

Proposition 11.9
.

Let
j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\in\{{\spl},{\el}\}
and
J=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{n})
.
Let
T_{J}
be the corresponding maximal tori of
G_{\infty}
.
Let
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}S_{J}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
