be an integrable function invariant under
Z_{+}
.
Then

\int_{Z_{+}\setminus T_{J}}\phi(\CH(t))\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D(t)\right\rvert}dt=2^{n}\int_{{\mathcal{F}}_{J}^{\pm}}\phi\left(r_{1},\pm 1,r_{2},N_{2},\ldots,r_{n},N_{n}\right)\dfrac{dr_{1}dr_{2}\cdots dr_{n}dN_{2}\cdots dN_{n}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{2}\cdots N_{n}\right\rvert}^{3/2}}.

Proof.

Applying Lemma
11.8

to

2^{n}\phi(r_{1},N_{1},\ldots,r_{n},N_{n})\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\dfrac{r_{j}^{2}}{N_{j}}-1\right\rvert}^{1/2},

as we explained above, we get that the quotient integral is

2^{n}\int_{{\mathcal{F}}_{J}^{\pm}}\phi\left(r_{1},\pm 1,r_{2},N_{2},\ldots,r_{n},N_{n}\right)\mathinner{\!\left\lvert r_{1}^{2}\pm 1\right\rvert}^{1/2}\prod_{j=2}^{n}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\dfrac{r_{j}^{2}}{N_{j}}-1\right\rvert}^{1/2}\dfrac{dr_{1}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert r_{1}^{2}\pm 1\right\rvert}^{1/2}}\prod_{i=2}^{n}\dfrac{dr_{i}dN_{i}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{i}\right\rvert}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert r_{i}^{2}-N_{i}\right\rvert}^{1/2}}.

Upon simplification this becomes

2^{n}\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{F}}_{J}^{\pm}}\phi\left(r_{1},\pm 1,r_{2},N_{2},\ldots,r_{n},N_{n}\right)\dfrac{dr_{1}dr_{2}\cdots dr_{n}dN_{2}\cdots dN_{n}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{2}\cdots N_{n}\right\rvert}^{3/2}}.\qed

We are still one step away from our desired formula.
In our previous computations, we have not used
N_{2},\ldots,N_{n}
as our variables.
Rather, we used the totally positive fundamental units to get the lattice that corresponds to the units.
Recall that
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n-1}
is our system of totally positive fundamental units and that

\beta_{i,j}=\sigma_{j}(\beta_{i}),

for
i=1,\ldots,n-1
and
j=1,\ldots,n
.

Proposition 11.10
.

Define the map
B\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\{\pm 1\}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}\longrightarrow\{\pm 1\}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}
given by

B(\pm 1,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1})=\left(\pm 1,\pm\dfrac{\beta_{1,2}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,2}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{2}}{\beta_{1,1}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,1}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{1}},\cdots,\pm\dfrac{\beta_{1,n}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,n}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{n}}{\beta_{1,1}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,1}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{1}}\right).

This map is a diffeomorphism onto its image, which consists of the vectors whose entries are nonzero.

Proof.

Consider a fix point
P=(s,N_{2},\ldots,N_{n})
where
s\in\{1,-1\}
.
The product

sN_{2}\cdots N_{n}

has a fixed sign, which we denote by
t\in\{1,-1\}
.
With this convention, there exists a unique real number
z>0
such that

z^{2n}\cdot(sN_{2}\cdots N_{n})=t{\mathsf{p}}^{k}.

Redistributing the above product, and taking absolute values, we see that

|(z^{2}s)(z^{2}N_{2})\cdots(z^{2}N_{n})|={\mathsf{p}}^{k}.

Recall that
\varepsilon
is the generator of the ideal
{\mathfrak{p}}^{k}
with embeddings (into the different real places) denoted by
\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}
.
By the product formula

\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\varepsilon_{1}\right\rvert}\cdots\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\varepsilon_{n}\right\rvert}={\mathsf{p}}^{k}.

Comparing the above equalities, we deduce that

\mathinner{\!\left\lvert z^{2}s\varepsilon_{1}^{-1}\right\rvert}\cdots\mathinner{\!\left\lvert z^{2}N_{n}\varepsilon_{n}^{-1}\right\rvert}=1.

Taking logarithms we get

\log\left(\mathinner{\!\left\lvert z^{2}s\varepsilon_{1}^{-1}\right\rvert}\right)+\cdots+\log\left(\mathinner{\!\left\lvert z^{2}N_{n}\varepsilon_{n}^{-1}\right\rvert}\right)=0.

By definition, we can find real numbers
y_{1},\dots,y_{n-1}
and a sign
\pm
such that

(27)

z^{2}s=\pm\beta_{1,1}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,1}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{1}

and for
j=2,\dots,n
we have

z^{2}N_{j}=\pm\beta_{1,j}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,j}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{j}.

From equation (
27
) we deduce that the uniform sign is
s
, which explains the first entry our map
B
.
Solving for
z^{2}
we obtain, for
j=2,\ldots,n
that

N_{j}=s\dfrac{\beta_{1,j}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,j}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{j}}{\beta_{1,1}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,1}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{1}}.

This proves that the map is surjective.

We must now verify it is injective.
To this end, for
j=2,\ldots,n
we express everything in terms of the exponential function.
We get

N_{j}=\exp(y_{1}\log(\beta_{1,j}/\beta_{1,1})+\cdots+y_{n-1}\log(\beta_{n-1,j}/\beta_{n-1,1}))\frac{s\varepsilon_{j}}{\varepsilon_{1}}.

This is the same as

\dfrac{\varepsilon_{1}N_{j}}{s\varepsilon_{j}}=\exp(y_{1}\log(\beta_{1,j}/\beta_{1,1})+\cdots+y_{n-1}\log(\beta_{n-1,j}/\beta_{n-1,1})).

Notice that, by construction, the left hand side is positive.
In particular, we are justified in taking logarithms and we get

y_{1}\log(\beta_{1,j}/\beta_{1,1})+\cdots+y_{n-1}\log(\beta_{n-1,j}/\beta_{n-1,1})=\log\left(\dfrac{\varepsilon_{1}N_{j}}{s\varepsilon_{j}}\right).

This is a square system of equations of dimension
n-1
.
The matrix associated to this system is

\begin{pmatrix}\log\left(\beta_{1,2}/\beta_{1,1}\right)&\cdots&\log\left(\beta_{1,n}/\beta_{1,1}\right)\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\log\left(\beta_{n-1,2}/\beta_{n-1,1}\right)&\cdots&\log\left(\beta_{n-1,n}/\beta_{n-1,1}\right)\\
\end{pmatrix}

Because

\log\left(\beta_{i,j}/\beta_{i,1}\right)=\log\left(\beta_{i,j}\right)-\log\left(\beta_{i,1}\right),

we see that

\left(\log\left(\beta_{i,j}/\beta_{i,1}\right)\right)_{i,j}=\left(\log\left(\beta_{i,j})-\log(\beta_{i,1}\right)\right)_{i,j}.

The standard properties of determinants then imply that

\det\left(\log\left(\beta_{i,j}/\beta_{i,1}\right)\right)=\det\left(\log\left(\beta_{i,j}\right)\right)=R_{K},

where
R_{K}
is the regulator of
K
.
So, this system always has a unique solution
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}
.

In the process of the previous analysis we have seen that
B
has an inverse, obtained by solving the system of equation.
This proves that both
B
and its inverse, by inspection, are smooth.
This concludes the proof.
∎

In Proposition
11.9
we have reduced our calculations over a quotient to the integral in coordinates

\int_{{\mathcal{F}}_{J}^{\pm}}\phi\left(r_{1},\pm 1,r_{2},N_{2},\ldots,r_{n},N_{n}\right)\dfrac{dr_{1}dr_{2}\cdots dr_{n}dN_{2}\cdots dN_{n}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{2}\cdots N_{n}\right\rvert}^{3/2}}.

Our next goal is to change of variables.
We begin with some preparations.
Upon rearranging the coordinates,

(r_{1},\pm,r_{2},N_{2},\ldots,r_{n},N_{n})

as

(\pm 1,r_{1},\ldots,r_{n},N_{2},\ldots,N_{n}).

The previous proposition allowed us to obtain the exponents that correspond to the determinant variables.
We now extend
B
to a map that also modifies appropriately the trace variables.

Definition 11.11
.

Define the map
N\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\{\pm 1\}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2n-1}\longrightarrow\{\pm 1\}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2n-1}
by sending

(\pm 1,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1})

to

\left(\pm 1,\dfrac{x_{1}}{\beta_{1,1}^{y_{1}/2}\cdots\beta_{n-1,1}^{y_{n-1}/2}\varepsilon_{1}^{1/2}},\ldots,\dfrac{x_{n}}{\beta_{1,1}^{y_{1}/2}\cdots\beta_{n-1,1}^{y_{n-1}/2}\varepsilon_{1}^{1/2}},\pm\dfrac{\beta_{1,2}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,2}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{2}}{\beta_{1,1}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,1}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{1}},\cdots,\pm\dfrac{\beta_{1,n}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,n}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{n}}{\beta_{1,1}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,1}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{1}}\right).

Proposition 11.12
.

The map
N
is a diffeomorphism onto its image, which consists of the vectors whose last
n-1
entries are nonzero.

Proof.

Consider a point
(\pm 1,r_{1},\ldots,r_{n},N_{2},\ldots,N_{n})
.
The last
n-1
entries of the map are, by definition,
B
.
This implies there exists unique
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1}
with

B(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1})=(\pm 1,N_{2},\ldots,N_{n}).

Once these values are known, one can solve for
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
uniquely (as they are just scaled by a vector depending on
\pm 1,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1}
.

That it is a diffeomorphism onto its image follows from the fact that
B
is and that the trace variables are scaled by an invertible factor.
∎

Notation
.

Let
N
be the map defined in Proposition
11.12
.
Let
j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\in\{{\spl},{\el}\}
and
J=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{n})
.
Define

{\mathcal{D}}^{\pm}_{J}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=N^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}^{\pm}_{J}).

Remark 11.13
.

Notice that
{\mathcal{D}}^{\pm}_{J}
is an open subset of
\{\pm 1\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n-1}
and that

\{{\mathcal{D}}^{\pm}_{J}\}_{J},

is an open partition of
\{\pm 1\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n-1}
up to a set of measure zero.
This set of measure zero corresponds to the singular set (i.e. the parabolas in the different
(r,N)
coordinates).

Proposition 11.14
.

Let
j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\in\{{\spl},{\el}\}
and
J=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{n})
.
Let
T_{J}
be the corresponding maximal tori of
G_{\infty}
.
Let
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}S_{J}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
be an integrable function invariant under
Z_{+}
.
Then the integral

\int_{{\mathcal{F}}_{J}^{\pm}}\phi\left(\pm 1,r_{1},\ldots,r_{n},N_{2},\ldots,N_{n}\right)\dfrac{dr_{1}dr_{2}\cdots dr_{n}dN_{2}\cdots dN_{n}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{2}\cdots N_{n}\right\rvert}^{3/2}},

equals

{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}R_{K}\int_{{\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\pm}}\phi\left(N(\pm,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1})\right)dx_{1}dx_{2}\cdots dx_{n}dy_{1}\cdots dy_{n-1}.

Proof.

This follows from the change of variable formula applied to the map
B
.
We will understand the determinant variables first.

We saw within the proof of Proposition
11.10
that

N_{j}=\exp(y_{1}\log(\beta_{1,j}/\beta_{1,1})+\cdots+y_{n-1}\log(\beta_{n-1,j}/\beta_{n-1,1}))\frac{s\varepsilon_{j}}{\varepsilon_{1}}.

Using the chain rule we get

dN_{j}=N_{j}\left(\log(\beta_{1,j}/\beta_{1,1})dy_{1}+\cdots+\log(\beta_{n-1,j}/\beta_{n-1,1})dy_{n-1}\right).

Taking the wedge product (and recalling that we are considering only the associated measure) we get

dN_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge dN_{n}=\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{2}\right\rvert}\cdots\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{n}\right\rvert}R_{K}dy_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dy_{n-1}.

Here, we have used the fact that the wedge product associated to a change of variable brings out the determinant of the associated derivative linear map.
We have calculated this derivative in the proof of Proposition
11.10
and realized it is the regulator.

We now study the trace variables.
From the definition of the map
N
we have

r_{j}=\dfrac{x_{j}}{\beta_{1,1}^{y_{1}/2}\cdots\beta_{n-1,1}^{y_{n-1}/2}\varepsilon_{1}^{1/2}}.

To simplify the computations, define

w(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\dfrac{1}{\beta_{1,1}^{y_{1}/2}\cdots\beta_{n-1,1}^{y_{n-1}/2}\varepsilon_{1}^{1/2}}.

Then

r_{j}=x_{j}w(y_{1},\ldots y_{n}).

Taking derivatives we find

dr_{j}=w(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\cdot dx_{j}+x_{j}dw.

The final change of variables, as it respects the involved differential form, is

dr_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dr_{n}\wedge dN_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge dN_{n}=\\
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{2}\right\rvert}\cdots\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{n}\right\rvert}R_{K}\cdot(wdx_{1}+x_{1}dw)\wedge\cdots\wedge(wdx_{n}+x_{n}dw)\wedge dy_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dy_{n-1}.

Notice that
dw
is a linear combination of
dy_{1},\ldots,dy_{n}
(with coefficients the partial derivatives with respect to
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1}
).
Thus,

dw\wedge dy_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dy_{n-1}=0.

We conclude the change of variable formula is

dr_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dr_{n}\wedge dN_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge dN_{n}=\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{2}\right\rvert}\cdots\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{n}\right\rvert}R_{K}w^{n}dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{n}\wedge dy_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dy_{n-1}.

Which is to say, in measures we have

dr_{1}\ldots dr_{n}dN_{2}\ldots dN_{n}=\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{2}\right\rvert}\cdots\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{n}\right\rvert}R_{K}w^{n}dx_{1}\ldots dx_{n}dy_{1}\cdots dy_{n-1}.

We conclude that the integrand

\phi\left(\pm 1,r_{1},\ldots,r_{2},N_{2},\ldots,N_{n}\right)\dfrac{dr_{1}dr_{2}\cdots dr_{n}dN_{2}\cdots dN_{n}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{2}\cdots N_{n}\right\rvert}^{3/2}}

transformed as

\phi\left(N(\pm,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1})\right)\cdot\dfrac{R_{K}w^{n}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{2}\cdots N_{n}\right\rvert}^{1/2}}\cdot dx_{1}dx_{2}\cdots dx_{n}dy_{1}\cdots dy_{n-1}.

Recall from the proof of Proposition
11.10
that

\displaystyle z^{2n}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{2}\ldots N_{n}\right\rvert}

\displaystyle={\mathsf{p}}^{k}

\displaystyle z^{2}

\displaystyle=\beta_{1,1}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,1}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{1}.

Thus
z=w^{-1}
.
It follows that

\dfrac{w^{n}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{2}\cdots N_{n}\right\rvert}^{1/2}}=\dfrac{1}{z^{n}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{2}\cdots N_{n}\right\rvert}^{1/2}}=\dfrac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}^{k/2}}.

We thus conclude that the integrand transformed as

dr_{1}\ldots dr_{n}dN_{2}\ldots dN_{n}={\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}R_{K}\phi\left(N(\pm,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1})\right)dx_{1}\ldots dx_{n}dy_{1}\cdots dy_{n-1}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

11.3.2.
The interpolation function and
{\mathcal{P}}^{\pm}

We now connect all our computations with the formulas we have studied in the previous sections.
We begin by recalling that the interpolation function is

\theta^{\pm}(r_{1},\dots,r_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n-1})=\\
{\mathsf{p}}^{\frac{k}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(2\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{r_{i}^{2}}{\pm 4\beta_{1,i}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,i}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{i}}-1\right\rvert}^{1/2}g_{\nu_{i},1}(r_{i},\pm 4\beta_{1,i}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,i}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{i})+g_{\nu_{i},2}(r_{i},\pm 4\beta_{1,i}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,i}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{i})\right),

As discussed in Section
4.2
, this function is the product of the orbital integral and the Weyl discriminant as it comes from the volume term.
In particular, it is a function invariant under the action of
Z_{+}
.

Recall the element

w=\dfrac{1}{\beta_{1,1}^{y_{1}/2}\cdots\beta_{n-1,1}^{y_{n-1}/2}\varepsilon_{1}^{1/2}}>0,

from the proof of Proposition
11.12
.
By the invariance just mentioned, we have

\displaystyle\theta^{\pm}(r_{1},\dots,r_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n-1})

\displaystyle={\mathsf{p}}^{\frac{k}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(2\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{r_{i}^{2}}{\pm 4\beta_{1,i}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,i}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{i}}-1\right\rvert}^{1/2}g_{\nu_{i},1}(r_{i},\pm 4\beta_{1,i}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,i}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{i})+g_{\nu_{i},2}(r_{i},\pm 4\beta_{1,i}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,i}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{i})\right)

\displaystyle={\mathsf{p}}^{\frac{k}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(2\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{(r_{i}w)^{2}}{\pm 4w^{2}\beta_{1,i}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,i}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{i}}-1\right\rvert}^{1/2}g_{\nu_{i},1}(wx_{i},\pm 4w^{2}\beta_{1,i}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,i}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{i})+g_{\nu_{i},2}(x_{i}w,\pm 4w^{2}\beta_{1,i}^{y_{1}}\cdots\beta_{n-1,i}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{i})\right).

By inspecting the last line of the previous chain of equations and using the definition, we see

(28)

\theta^{\pm}(r_{1},\dots,r_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n-1})=\theta^{\pm}(N(\pm 1,r_{1},\ldots,r_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1})).

We see that our function is precisely created to capture the appropriate change of variable in the Weyl integration formula.
We now proceed to do the analogous process for
{\mathcal{P}}^{\pm}(x,y)
.
Recall that this function is

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(r,y)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert r_{i}^{2}\pm 4\beta_{1,i}^{y_{1}}\dots\beta_{n-1,i}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{i}\right\rvert}^{-1/2}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert 4\beta_{1,i}^{y_{1}}\dots\beta_{n-1,i}^{y_{n-1}}\varepsilon_{i}\right\rvert}^{-1/2}}\right).

By inspection we see this function is invariant under the action of
Z_{+}
(which we know must be the case, because this a multiple of the Weyl discriminant in coordinates).
Consequently, using the same
w
as above and repeating the same process, we deduce

\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(r,y)=\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(N(\pm 1,r,y)).

11.3.3.
The Weyl Integration Formula

We are now ready to write the Weyl integration formula in our coordinates.

Theorem 11.15
.

Let
h\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}G_{\infty}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
be a function in
C_{c}(Z_{+}\backslash G_{\infty})
, then

\int_{Z_{+}\backslash G_{\infty}}h(\dot{g})\dfrac{dg}{dz}={\mathsf{p}}^{-k}R_{K}\displaystyle\sum_{\pm}\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2n-1}}\theta^{\pm}\left(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1}\right)dx_{1}\cdots dx_{n}dy_{1}\cdots dy_{n-1}.

where
\dot{g}
denotes the variable of integration in the quotient
Z_{+}\backslash G_{\infty}
.

Proof.

The Weyl integration formula is

\int_{Z_{+}\backslash G_{\infty}}f(g)dg=\dfrac{1}{2^{n}}\displaystyle\sum_{T}\int_{Z_{+}\backslash T}\int_{T\setminus G_{\infty}}f(g^{-1}tg)dg\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D(t)\right\rvert}^{2}dt,

A set of representatives of maximal tori of
G_{\infty}
is
T_{J}
where
J=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{n})
and
j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\in\{{\spl},{\el}\}
.
Let
J
be a fixed subindex.
We can define

\phi_{J}(t)=\int_{T\setminus G_{\infty}}f(g^{-1}tg)dg.

This is the orbital integral over
T_{J}
, which is invariant under the action of the center by the choice of our function
f
.
Then Proposition
11.9
implies

\int_{Z_{+}\backslash T}\phi_{J}(t)\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D(t)\right\rvert}^{2}dt=2^{n}\int_{{\mathcal{F}}_{J}^{\pm}}\phi_{J}\left(\pm 1,r_{1},r_{2},N_{2},\ldots,r_{n},N_{n}\right)\dfrac{dr_{1}dr_{2}\cdots dr_{n}dN_{2}\cdots dN_{n}}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert N_{2}\cdots N_{n}\right\rvert}^{3/2}}.

Then, Proposition
11.14
applied to the latter integral implies

2^{n}{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}R_{K}\int_{{\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\pm}}\phi_{J}\left(N(\pm,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1})\right)dx_{1}dx_{2}\cdots dx_{n}dy_{1}\cdots dy_{n-1}.

Substituting in the Weyl integration formula gives

\int_{Z_{+}\backslash G_{\infty}}f(\dot{g})\dfrac{dg}{dz}=\\
{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}R_{K}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{J}\int_{{\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\pm}}\phi_{J}\left(N(\pm,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1})\right)dx_{1}dx_{2}\cdots dx_{n}dy_{1}\cdots dy_{n-1}.

Notice that the factor
2^{n}
cancels.
By definition, if
(\pm 1,r_{1},\ldots,r_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1})
corresponds to a point in
S_{J}
, and consequently to some element
t(\pm 1,r_{1},\ldots,r_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1})
in the tori
T_{J}
, then

\theta^{\pm}(r_{1},\ldots,r_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1})=\\
{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\phi_{J}(t(\pm 1,r_{1},\ldots,r_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1}))\mathinner{\!\left\lvert D(t(\pm 1,r_{1},\ldots,r_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1}))\right\rvert}.

In particular,

\sum_{\pm}\displaystyle\sum_{J}\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\pm}}\phi_{J}\left(N(\pm,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1})\right)dx_{1}dx_{2}\cdots dx_{n}dy_{1}\cdots dy_{n-1},

equals

{\mathsf{p}}^{-k/2}\displaystyle\sum_{\pm}\sum_{J}\int_{{\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\pm}}\theta^{\pm}\left(N(\pm,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1})\right)dx_{1}dx_{2}\cdots dx_{n}dy_{1}\cdots dy_{n-1}

Using the invariance (
28
) and that
{\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\pm}
are a partition of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2n-1}
up to a set of measure zero, gives the desired result.
∎

11.4.
The trace of the Trivial Representation

We now compute the trace of the trivial representation.
Following Langlands’ calculation
[
31
, p. 644]
, the trace of the
trivial representation
at the non-archimedean place
{\mathfrak{p}}
is

\int_{G(K_{\mathfrak{p}})}f(g)dg={\mathsf{p}}^{k/2}\frac{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-(k+1)}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-1}}.

By our choice of the test function, the contribution at all other non-archimedean places is 1.
So we have

(29)

\Tr({\bf 1}(f))={\mathsf{p}}^{k/2}\frac{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-(k+1)}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-1}}\Tr({\bf 1}(f_{\infty})),

where
\bf 1
is the trivial representation and
f
is the test function.
We can break the integral as a product of local integrals because the function factorizes and the adèlic measure we have picked is the restricted product measure.

The above formula explains the need to study the archimedean trace of the trivial representation.
Theorem
11.15
implies

\Tr({\bf 1}(f_{\infty}))={\mathsf{p}}^{-k}R_{K}\displaystyle\sum_{\pm}\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2n-1}}\theta^{\pm}\left(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1}\right)dx_{1}dx_{2}\cdot dx_{n}dy_{1}\cdots dy_{n-1}.

Substituting this into (
29
) proves Theorem
11.3
(i)
.

11.5.
The trace of the special representation at the archimedean places

11.5.1.
The case of
\mathbb{R}

Langlands computed the character
\theta_{\pm,\pm}
of the representation
\pi_{\pm,\pm}
in
[
31
, pp. 630–631]
.
The values of the character of the representation are given by the regions in which the parabola and the axis divide the plane (notice that this makes sense, because characters only depend on conjugacy class, so they are genuine functions of the variables
(r,N)
).

Lemma 11.16
.

The character
\theta_{+,+}
satisfy the following properties

(i)

The character is equal to
0
in region
S_{\el}
(that is, in the elliptic torus).

(ii)

In
S_{\spl}
, the character is
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{r^{2}}{N}-1\right\rvert}^{-1/2}
.
More precisely, for
\pi_{+,+}
the constant
C_{+,+}
equals
1
in regions
S_{\spl}
.

Proof.

See the discussion in
[
31
, pp. 630–631]
.
∎

Langlands and Altuğ perform change of variables related to quotienting by
Z_{+}
, and reinterpret the above result in terms of the trace
r
.
We avoid doing this, as the action of
Z_{+}
enters the picture slightly differently for us.

11.5.2.
Totally Real Fields

Starting with the representation
\chi_{+,+}\otimes\cdots\otimes\chi_{+,+}
of
T_{\spl}\times\cdots\times T_{\spl}
we can obtain the induced representation
\pi_{+,+}\otimes\cdots\otimes\pi_{+,+}
.
We now prove the analogue of Lemma
11.16
in our setting.

Proposition 11.17
.

Let
\CH\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\ch\otimes\cdots\otimes\ch\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}G_{\infty}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times\cdots\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be given by

\CH(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n})=(r_{1},N_{1},\dots,r_{n},N_{n}).

Let
\theta_{+,+,\dots,+,+}
be the character of
\pi_{+,+}\otimes\cdots\otimes\pi_{+,+}
and
C
be the indicator function defined in Definition
3.30
.
Then

\CH(\theta_{+,+,\dots,+,+}(r_{1},N_{1},\dots,r_{n},N_{n}))=C(r_{1},N_{1},\dots,r_{n},N_{n})\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{r_{i}^{2}}{N_{i}}-1\right\rvert}^{-1/2}.

Proof.

We begin with the observation that the trace of a tensor product is the product of the traces.
Therefore, the character splits into the characters of each individual archimedean place.

Lemma
11.16
asserts that at each place the character is a multiple of
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{r_{i}^{2}}{N_{i}}-1\right\rvert}^{-1/2}
.
This constant multiple depends on the region and takes a value in
\{0,1\}
.
The result follows by multiplying over all real places.
∎

We now complete the proof of Theorem
11.3
(ii)
.
Our objective is to find the value of
\Tr(\pi_{+,+}\otimes\cdots\otimes\pi_{+,+}(f))
.
The steps are the same as in §
11.4
, via Theorem
11.15
, except that we substitute

h(r_{1},\ldots,r_{n},N_{2},\ldots,N_{n})=\theta_{+,+,\ldots,+,+}(r_{1},\ldots,r_{n},N_{2},\ldots,N_{n})\theta^{\pm}(r_{1},\ldots,r_{n},N_{2},\ldots,N_{n}).

From this we get

\int_{Z_{+}\backslash G_{\infty}}f(g)\theta_{+,+,\dots,+,+}(g)dg={\mathsf{p}}^{-k}2^{n}R_{K}\sum_{\pm}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2n-1}}C(x,y)\theta^{\pm}(x,y)\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)dxdy.

where
C(x,y)
is the indicator function given by
C
in the variables
x,y
.

To explain the appearance of
\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)
and the factors of
2
, recall that Proposition
11.17
introduces

\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{r_{i}^{2}}{N_{i}}-1\right\rvert}^{-1/2}.

Using Definition
6.1
, this product in the variables
(x,y)
is precisely
2^{n}\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)
.

11.6.
The computation of the one dimensional traces

We now put everything together.
In Theorem
3.11
we proved that if
(Hyp-div)
is not satisfied, then the regular elliptic part of the trace formula for the test function
f
is zero.
We now prove that on the spectral side a similar implication holds.
More precisely, we prove the following result.

Theorem 11.18
.

The following dichotomy holds for the test function
f

(i)

if
(Hyp-div)
holds then

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\chi}\Tr((\boldsymbol{1}\otimes\chi)(f))

\displaystyle=2^{n-1}h_{K}\Tr(f),

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\chi}\Tr((\xi_{0}\otimes\chi)(f))

\displaystyle=2^{n-1}h_{K}\Tr(\xi_{0}(f)).

(ii)

if
(Hyp-div)
does not hold, then

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\chi}\Tr((\boldsymbol{1}\otimes\chi)(f))

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\chi}\Tr((\xi_{0}\otimes\chi)(f))

\displaystyle=0.

In each case, the sum is over characters
\chi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\mathbb{A}}_{K}\rightarrow{\mathbb{Q}}
which are trivial on
K^{*}\displaystyle\prod_{v}{\mathbb{R}}^{*}
.

The result follows from several observations.
We remind the reader that as a character on
\GL(2,{\mathbb{A}}_{K})
, it is possible to identify
\chi
with
\chi\circ\det
.
We begin with a local lemma.

Lemma 11.19
.

Let
{\mathfrak{q}}
be any finite prime of
K
and
\chi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}G(K_{{\mathfrak{q}}})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a character.
If
\chi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
is not spherical then

\int_{G(K_{{\mathfrak{q}}})}f_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(x)\chi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(x)dx=0.

Proof.

For notational simplicity, we write
\chi=\chi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
in this proof.
Recall that a character
\chi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}K_{\mathfrak{q}}^{*}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
is called spherical if its restriction to the ring of units
{\mathcal{O}}_{K_{\mathfrak{q}}}^{*}
is the identity.
Therefore, if
\chi
is not spherical then there exists
a\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K_{\mathfrak{q}}}^{*}
such that
\chi(a)\neq 1
.
Using this element, define the matrix

A\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&1\\
\end{pmatrix}\in\GL(2,K_{\mathfrak{q}}).

We have
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\det(A)\right\rvert}_{\mathfrak{q}}=\mathinner{\!\left\lvert a\right\rvert}_{\mathfrak{q}}=1
.
Recall the definition of
f_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
from Definition
3.4

f_{\mathfrak{q}}=\begin{cases}f_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{(0)}=\mathbbm{1}\left(X\in\Mat({\mathcal{O}}_{K_{{\mathfrak{q}}}})\mid\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\det(X)\right\rvert}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}=1\right)&\text{ if }{\mathfrak{q}}\neq{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{p}})^{-k/2}f_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{(k)}=\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{p}})^{-k/2}\mathbbm{1}\left(X\in\Mat({\mathcal{O}}_{K_{{\mathfrak{p}}}})\mid\;\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\det(X)\right\rvert}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}=\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{p}})^{-k}\right)&\text{ if }{\mathfrak{q}}={\mathfrak{p}};\end{cases}

we can thus conclude
f_{\mathfrak{q}}(xA)=f_{\mathfrak{q}}(x)
.
Using this we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{G(K_{{\mathfrak{q}}})}f_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(x)\chi(x)dx

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{G(K_{{\mathfrak{q}}})}f_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(xA)\chi(xA)dx

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{G(K_{{\mathfrak{q}}})}f_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(x)\chi(x)\chi(a)dx

\displaystyle=\chi(a)\displaystyle\int_{G(K_{{\mathfrak{q}}})}f_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(x)\chi(x)dx.

Notice we have used the invariance of the Haar measure.
In particular,

(1-\chi(a))\displaystyle\int_{G(K_{{\mathfrak{q}}})}f_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(x)\chi(x)dx=0.

The result follows.
∎

The sum of the traces of the representations constituting the residual spectrum is

\sum_{\chi}\Tr((\boldsymbol{1}\otimes\chi)(f)).

We see that for each individual term we can apply Lemma
11.19
.
Concretely, we have

\Tr((\boldsymbol{1}\otimes\chi)(f))=\prod_{\mathfrak{q}}\int_{G(K_{{\mathfrak{q}}})}f_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(x_{\mathfrak{q}})\chi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(x_{\mathfrak{q}})dx_{\mathfrak{q}}\times\int_{Z_{+}\backslash G_{\infty}}f_{\infty}(x_{\infty})\chi_{\infty}(x_{\infty})dx_{\infty}.

Lemma
11.19
asserts that only those characters that are spherical at every finite place may have non-zero contribution to the sum.
In other words, the sum really runs over the characters of

K^{*}\displaystyle\prod_{v}{\mathbb{R}}^{*}_{>0}\displaystyle\prod_{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathcal{O}}_{K_{\mathfrak{q}}}^{*}\backslash{\mathbb{A}}_{K}.

We know this quotient is finite and equals the narrow class group.
In particular, the sum really runs over the characters of the narrow class group and hence it is finite.

The finiteness of this sum allows us to exchange the sum and the integral, that is,

\sum_{\chi}\Tr((\boldsymbol{1}\otimes\chi)(f))=\sum_{\chi}\int_{Z_{+}\backslash G({\mathbb{A}}_{K})}f(g)\chi(g)dg=\int_{Z_{+}\backslash G({\mathbb{A}}_{K})}f(g)\sum_{\chi}\chi(\det(g))dg.

Analyzing the character sum carefully leads to the proof of Theorem
11.18
.

Proof of Theorem
11.18
.

We carry out a detailed argument for the trivial representation.
The argument for the special representation is the same.

Part (b):

Define the set

U\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\prod_{{\mathfrak{q}}\neq{\mathfrak{p}}}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{*}\times\pi_{\mathfrak{p}}^{k}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{*}\times\displaystyle\prod_{v}{\mathbb{R}}^{*},

where
\pi_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a uniformizer at
{\mathfrak{p}}
.

By definition of
f
, if for some
g\in\GL(2,{\mathbb{A}}_{K})
we have
\det(g)\notin U
then
f(g)=0
.
Indeed, if
g=(g_{\mathfrak{p}})
then

\det(g)=(\det(g_{{\mathfrak{p}}}))_{{\mathfrak{p}}},

and thus for some of its finite components the condition defining the indicator is not satisfied.
In this case,

\sum\chi(\det(g))=\sum\chi({\mathfrak{p}}^{k})=0,

On the other hand, every idèle of
U
corresponds to the ideal
{\mathfrak{p}}^{k}
.
Hence, for
g
in the support of
f
, we have

\sum\chi(\det(g))=\sum\chi({\mathfrak{p}}^{k})=0,

because
{\mathfrak{p}}^{k}\neq 1
in the class group (i.e. not principal) since
(Hyp-div)
does not hold.
Thus, for all
g
, the product

f(g)\displaystyle\sum_{\chi}\chi(\det(g))=0.

This proves (b).
We emphasize that we have used that the sum runs over characters that come from the narrow class groups and thus their value at an idèle depends only on their finite part.

Part (a):
The same analysis we did above proves that, restricted to
U
, we have

f(g)\displaystyle\sum_{\chi}\chi(\det(g))=h_{K}^{+}f(g),

because this time
{\mathfrak{p}}^{k}=1
in the narrow class group.
In here,
h_{K}^{+}
is the narrow class number.
Upon integrating, we get

\sum_{\chi}\Tr((\boldsymbol{1}\otimes\chi)(f))=\int_{Z_{+}\backslash G({\mathbb{A}}_{K})}f(g)\sum_{\chi}\chi(\det(g))dg=h_{K}^{+}\int_{Z_{+}\backslash U}f(g)dg=h_{K}^{+}\Tr((\boldsymbol{1}\otimes\chi)(f)).

Finally, we recall that the narrow class number satisfies (see
[
14
, Proposition 2.1]
)

h_{K}^{+}=\dfrac{h_{K}2^{r_{1}}}{[U_{K}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}U_{K}^{+}]},

where
r_{1}
is the number of real embeddings and
[U_{K}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}U_{K}^{+}]
refers to the index of the totally positive units inside the units.
Under the conditions we have put to our number field, we have
[U_{K}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}U_{K}^{+}]=2
.
This completes the proof of (a).
∎

Finally we conclude with the main result.

Theorem 11.20
.

The following equality holds

\overline{\Sigma_{0}}(f)=\displaystyle\sum_{\chi}\Tr((\boldsymbol{1}\otimes\chi)(f))-2\displaystyle\sum_{\chi}\Tr((\xi_{0}\otimes\chi)(f))+\eqref{eq:C(1)new}+\eqref{C2new}.

Proof.

By Theorem
11.18
we have

\sum_{\chi}\Tr((\boldsymbol{1}\otimes\chi)(f))=2^{n-1}h_{K}\Tr(f),

and by Theorem
11.3
,

\Tr({\bf 1}(f))={{\mathsf{p}}}^{-k/2}R_{K}\frac{1-{{\mathsf{p}}}^{-(k+1)}}{1-{{\mathsf{p}}}^{-1}}\sum_{\pm}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2n-1}}\theta^{\pm}(x,y)dxdy.

Hence, by substituting, we get

\sum_{\chi}\Tr((\boldsymbol{1}\otimes\chi)(f))=2^{n-1}h_{K}R_{K}{{\mathsf{p}}}^{-k/2}\frac{1-{{\mathsf{p}}}^{-(k+1)}}{1-{{\mathsf{p}}}^{-1}}\sum_{\pm}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2n-1}}\theta^{\pm}(x,y)dxdy,

which we recognize as (
18
).
In exactly the same way, Theorem
11.18
implies

\displaystyle\sum_{\chi}\Tr((\xi_{0}\otimes\chi)(f))=2^{n-1}h_{K}\Tr(\xi_{0}(f))

and by Theorem
11.3
,

\Tr(\xi_{0}(f))=(k+1){\mathsf{p}}^{-k}2^{n-2}R_{K}\sum_{\pm}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2n-1}}C(x,y)\theta^{\pm}(x,y)\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)dxdy

Consequently,

\sum_{\chi}\Tr((\xi_{0}\otimes\chi)(f))=\dfrac{1}{2}(k+1){\mathsf{p}}^{-k}4^{n-1}h_{K}R_{K}\sum_{\pm}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2n-1}}C(x,y)\theta^{\pm}(x,y)\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(x,y)dxdy,

which we recognize as
-\dfrac{1}{2}
of (
22
).

Finally, by Proposition
10.6
we have

\overline{\Sigma_{0}}(f)=\eqref{eq:triv res new}+\eqref{eq:spec residue2new}+\eqref{eq:C(1)new}+\eqref{C2new}.

Using Theorem
11.3
above, we get the result.
∎

Appendix A
Kloosterman Sum Calculations

In this appendix, we compute the Kloosterman sums and provide a proof of Theorem
9.3
.
For the calculations in this section, we assume that
K
is a number field satisfying
(Hyp-div)
and
(Hyp-split)
.
For the convenience of the reader, we repeat the statement below.

Theorem
.

Let
K
be a number field satisfying
(Hyp-split)
and let
({\mathfrak{p}},k)
be the pair of prime ideal and corresponding positive integers satisfying
(Hyp-div)
fixed in §
3.1
.
Write
\mathsf{p}=\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{p}})
.
Let
u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{*}
be a unit.
For a complex parameter
z
with
\Re(z)>1
,

{\mathbb{D}}(z,u)=4^{n}\frac{\zeta_{K}(2z)}{\zeta_{K}(z+1)}\cdot\frac{1-1/{\mathsf{p}}^{z(k+1)}}{1-1/{\mathsf{p}}^{z}}.

Hence,
{\mathbb{D}}(z,u)
admits an analytic continuation to a meromorphic function in the whole complex plane with a pole at
z=\frac{1}{2}
.

Proof.

The assertion follows from Lemma
A.7
and Propositions
A.10
,
A.13
,
A.18
,
A.21
, and
A.22
whose proofs will occupy the remainder of this section.
∎

The remainder of this section is dedicated to proving the aforementioned propositions.

A.1.
Factorization into local factors.

At each prime ideal
{\mathfrak{q}}
(which may or may not equal
{\mathfrak{p}}
), we fix a uniformizer
\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
.
When
{\mathfrak{q}}\mid 2
, we assume that
\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}=2
.

Proposition A.1
.

Let
({\mathfrak{p}},k)
be the pair of prime ideal and corresponding positive integer satisfying
(Hyp-div)
fixed in §
3.1
.
Let
{\mathfrak{q}}
be any prime ideal of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
(possibly equal to
{\mathfrak{p}}
).
Fix a generator
\rho
of
{\mathfrak{p}}^{h_{\mathfrak{p}}}
and fix
k^{\prime}=k/h_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
.
Let
{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}
be ideals of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
and let
u
be a unit.
An integer class
x
modulo
4{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}
satisfies the congruence conditions for
K_{{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}}(u)
if and only if for
each
prime
{\mathfrak{q}}
its restriction
x_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
modulo
{\mathfrak{q}}^{\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(4{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{2})}
satisfies the following conditions:

•

If
{\mathfrak{q}}\nmid 2
, then

x_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\pmod{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathfrak{d}}^{2})}},

•

If
{\mathfrak{q}}\mid 2
, then

x_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\pmod{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathfrak{d}}^{2})}},

and

\frac{x_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}\equiv 0,1\pmod{4}.

Proof.

This is the Chinese Remainder Theorem.
∎

Based on the above proposition we introduce the following local Kloosterman sum.

Definition A.2
.

With notation as in Proposition
A.1
,
For
\rho
a generator of
{\mathfrak{p}}^{h_{\mathfrak{p}}}
and non-negative integers
\upsilon,r
define

\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon+2r}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}\end{subarray}}\binom{\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon}}\quad\text{ if }{\mathfrak{q}}\nmid 2\\
\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{2+\upsilon+2r}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}\\
\frac{\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{2r}}\equiv 0,1\pmod{4}\end{subarray}}\binom{\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon}}\quad\text{ if }{\mathfrak{q}}\mid 2.\end{cases}

Remark A.3
.

The definitions coincide at the primes above 2 with
K_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)
.
However, for the primes not above
2
, the definition is slightly different because the modulus in
K_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)
is still
4{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon+2r}
, which cannot be recovered as a local object because of the factor
4
.
More precisely, when
{\mathfrak{q}}\nmid 2
the corresponding local object arise without the factor of
4
because it is a unit.

Proposition A.4
.

With notation as above, suppose that the ideals
{\mathfrak{a}}
and
{\mathfrak{d}}
have the following factorization

{\mathfrak{a}}=\prod{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon_{{\mathfrak{q}}}},\quad{\mathfrak{d}}=\prod{\mathfrak{q}}^{r_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}.

Then

K_{{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}}(u)=\prod_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon_{{\mathfrak{q}}}},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}}(u).

Proof.

This is a restatement of Proposition
A.1
.
∎

With this at hand, we make the following definition.

Definition A.5
.

Let
{\mathfrak{q}}
be a prime ideal of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
and let
z
be a complex parameter with
\Re(z)>1
.
Define

{\mathbb{D}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(z,u)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{q}})^{r(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=0}^{\infty}\frac{\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{q}})^{\upsilon(z+1)}}.

We now record some useful properties of
{\mathbb{D}}(z,u)
and
{\mathbb{D}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(z,u)
which we will be used without further mention.

Proposition A.6
.

Let
{\mathfrak{q}}
be any prime ideal of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
.
The series defining
{\mathbb{D}}(z,u)
and
{\mathbb{D}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(z,u)
converge absolutely and uniformly in compact subsets of the right half plane
\Re(z)>1
.

Proof.

For ideals
{\mathfrak{a}}
and
{\mathfrak{d}}
we have the bound

\mathinner{\!\left\lvert K_{{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}}(u)\right\rvert}\leq 4^{n}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2},

where
n=[K\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\mathbb{Q}}]
.
Writing
\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{q}})={\mathsf{q}}
, we can analogously show that

\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)\right\rvert}\leq\begin{cases}{\mathsf{q}}^{\upsilon+2r}&\text{ if }{\mathfrak{q}}\nmid 2\\
{\mathsf{q}}^{2+\upsilon+2r}&\text{ if }{\mathfrak{q}}\mid 2.\end{cases}

Writing
z=\sigma+it
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2z+1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert K_{{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{d}}}(u)\right\rvert}}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{z+1}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2\sigma+1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{4^{n}\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{\sigma+1}}

\displaystyle\leq 4^{n}\sum_{{\mathfrak{d}}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{d}})^{2\sigma-1}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{1}{\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{a}})^{\sigma}}

\displaystyle=4^{n}\zeta_{K}(2\sigma-1)\zeta_{K}(\sigma).

Notice that for
\sigma>1
we have
2\sigma-1>1
, so the bound implies the absolute convergence and uniformity in compact sets.
The result for
{\mathbb{D}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(z,u)
is completely analogous.
∎

Lemma A.7
.

The following local Euler decomposition holds for a complex parameter
z
with
\Re(z)>1
, i.e.,

{\mathbb{D}}(z,u)=\prod_{{\mathfrak{q}}}{\mathbb{D}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(z,u).

Further, the convergence is absolute and uniform in compact sets.

Proof.

This follows from uniqueness of factorization into prime ideals of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
and Proposition
A.4
.
By Lemma
A.6
we have the convergence of the product absolutely and uniformly in compact sets.
∎

A.2.
The primes not above
2

Using the definition of the modified Hilbert symbol we have

\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon+2r}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}\end{subarray}}\binom{\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon+2r}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}\end{subarray}}\binom{(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{rH}^{\upsilon}.

We quote the following facts, which will be used repeatedly in all the subsequent computations.

(
Fact
1)

When
\upsilon=0
, this is a sum of 1’s, so the value is the number of terms in the sum.

(
Fact
2)

When
\upsilon\neq 0
, the only thing that matters from
\upsilon
is its parity.

The following lemma is proven over
{\mathbb{Q}}
in
[
31
]
.
We provide a proof in our case.

Lemma A.8
.

Let
{\mathfrak{q}}\nmid 2
and let
m
be any rational integer relatively prime to
{\mathfrak{q}}
.
Then

\sum_{x\pmod{{\mathfrak{q}}}}\binom{x^{2}-m}{{\mathfrak{q}}}=-1.

Proof.

The number of
y
such that
x^{2}-m=y^{2}
is given by
\binom{x^{2}-m}{{\mathfrak{q}}}+1
.
Hence, the number of solutions modulo
{\mathfrak{q}}
to the curve
x^{2}-m\equiv y^{2}\pmod{{\mathfrak{q}}}
is

\sum_{x\pmod{{\mathfrak{q}}}}\left(\binom{x^{2}-m}{{\mathfrak{q}}}+1\right).

On the other hand, factoring the curve as

(x-y)(x+y)=m,

we can parameterize all solutions as
x=\frac{a+a^{-1}m}{2}
and
y=\frac{a-a^{-1}m}{2}
for some
a
which is invertible modulo
{\mathfrak{q}}
.
Hence, there are
\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{q}})-1
solutions.
The result follows.
∎

A.2.1.
Case
{\mathfrak{q}}\nmid 2\rho

We begin with the computations of
\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)
.

Lemma A.9
.

Let
({\mathfrak{p}},k)
be the pair of prime ideal and corresponding positive integer satisfying
(Hyp-div)
fixed in §
3.1
.
Set
{k^{\prime}}=k/h_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
and denote by
\rho
the generator of
{\mathfrak{p}}^{h_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
.
Let
{\mathfrak{q}}
be a prime ideal of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
such that
{\mathsf{q}}=\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{q}})
and
{\mathfrak{q}}\nmid 2\rho
, i.e.
{\mathfrak{q}}
is coprime to
2
and
{\mathfrak{p}}
.
Then the values of
\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)
are given as follows:

(i)

Case
\upsilon=0,r=0
:

\widetilde{K}_{1,1}(u)=1

(ii)

Case
\upsilon=0,r>0
:

\widetilde{K}_{1,{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)=1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}

(iii)

Case
\upsilon>0
even ,
r=0
:
\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},1}(u)={\mathsf{q}}^{\upsilon}-{\mathsf{q}}^{\upsilon-1}\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)

(iv)

Case
\upsilon>0
odd ,
r=0
:
\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},1}(u)=-{\mathsf{q}}^{\upsilon-1}

(v)

Case
\upsilon>0
even ,
r>0
:
\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)={\mathsf{q}}^{\upsilon-1}({\mathsf{q}}-1)\left(1+\binom{\mu\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)

(vi)

Case
\upsilon>0
odd ,
r>0
:
\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)=0

Proof.

We divide into the cases as in the statement.

Case
\upsilon=r=0
.

Then,

(30)

\begin{split}\widetilde{K}_{(1),(1)}(u)&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{0}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{0}\end{subarray}}\binom{(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{0}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{0}=1\end{split}

because the sum goes over the representatives of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}/(1)=1
.
This settles
(i)
.

Case
\upsilon=0
,
r>0
.

Then,

(31)

\begin{split}\widetilde{K}_{(1),({\mathfrak{q}}^{r})}(u)&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}\end{subarray}}\binom{(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{0}\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}\end{subarray}}1\\
&=1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}.\end{split}

We used
(Fact 1)
above because
\upsilon=0
.
Notice for the third equality we used the representatives run over
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}/{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}
and that the number of solutions to the congruence

\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}

is given by
1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}
since
{\mathfrak{q}}\nmid 2
and by the use of Hensel’s Lemma.
This settles
(ii)
.

Case
\upsilon>0
,
r=0
:

Then,

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}_{({\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon}),(1)}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{0}\end{subarray}}\binom{\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{\upsilon}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon}}\binom{\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{\upsilon}

\displaystyle=q^{\upsilon-1}\sum_{\mu^{\prime}\mod{{\mathfrak{q}}}}\binom{{\mu^{\prime}}^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{\upsilon}\textrm{ by periodicity of the modified Hilbert symbol modulo }{\mathfrak{q}}.

When
\upsilon
is even, each term contributes
1
except for the solutions of
{\mu^{\prime}}^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\pmod{{\mathfrak{q}}}
, which are
1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}
in quantity.
Hence, for even
\upsilon
we have

\sum_{\mu^{\prime}\mod{{\mathfrak{q}}}}\binom{{\mu^{\prime}}^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{\upsilon}={\mathsf{q}}-\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right).

We use Lemma
A.8
for the case when
\upsilon
is odd to get the value of the sum.
Therefore,

(32)

K_{({\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon}),(1)}(u)=\begin{cases}{\mathsf{q}}^{\upsilon}-{\mathsf{q}}^{\upsilon-1}\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)&\text{ if }\upsilon\text{ is even }\\
-{\mathsf{q}}^{\upsilon-1}&\text{ if }\upsilon\text{ is odd }.\end{cases}

This settles
(iii)
and
(iv)
.

Case
\upsilon>0
,
r>0
:

Then,

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon+2r}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}\end{subarray}}\binom{(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{\upsilon}

\displaystyle=q^{\upsilon-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{1+2r}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}\end{subarray}}\binom{(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{\upsilon}\textrm{ by periodicity of modified Hilbert symbol mod }{\mathfrak{q}}.

If
\upsilon
is even we have to count the number of solutions to the congruence

\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}

modulo
{\mathfrak{q}}^{1+2r}
because each one contributes a
1
to the sum.
As we have said before, the number of solutions is
1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}
.
Each such solution has
{\mathsf{q}}
lifts modulo
{\mathfrak{q}}
, so there are

{\mathsf{q}}\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)

solutions to this congruence we seek.
However, we must subtract from our count those for which

\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r+1},

because for those
\binom{(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}
is 0, since
(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r}=0
is a multiple of
\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
.
We conclude, for even
\upsilon
,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{1+2r}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}\end{subarray}}\binom{(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{\upsilon}={\mathsf{q}}\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)-\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)=({\mathsf{q}}-1)\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right).

On the other hand, for odd
\upsilon
, we must evaluate the sum

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{1+2r}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}\end{subarray}}\binom{(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}.

The terms that contribute to the sum are again those congruences modulo
{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r+1}
such that

\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}

but not

\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r+1}.

If
4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}
is a quadratic residue modulo
{\mathfrak{q}}
, it follows from Hensel’s lemma that we can find a solution to
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}
with any prescribed congruence modulo
{\mathfrak{q}}
of
(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r}
.
Since the number of quadratic residues and non-residues is the same modulo
{\mathfrak{q}}
, we conclude half the terms contribute
1
and the other half contribute
-1
, i.e.

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{1+2r}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}\end{subarray}}\binom{(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}.

In conclusion,

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}{\mathsf{q}}^{\upsilon-1}({\mathsf{q}}-1)\left(1+\binom{\mu\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)&\text{ if }\upsilon\text{ is even}\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

This settles the remaining cases
(v)
and
(vi)
.
∎

With this at hand, the following result is a (tedious) algebraic computation.

Proposition A.10
.

With the same notation introduced in Lemma
A.9
,

{\mathbb{D}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(z,u)=\frac{1-1/{\mathsf{q}}^{z+1}}{1-1/{\mathsf{q}}^{2z}}.

Proof.

We divide the sum according to the six cases of Proposition
A.9
.
Note that the term corresponding to
(vi)
vanishes.
For the cases where
\upsilon
is assumed to be even or odd we will write
2\upsilon
or
2\upsilon+1
, respectively.

We have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{D}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(z,u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{r(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)}{{\mathsf{q}}^{\upsilon(z+1)}}

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{\widetilde{K}_{(1),{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)}{{\mathsf{q}}^{r(2z+1)}}+\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},(1)}(u)}{{\mathsf{q}}^{\upsilon(2z+1)}}+\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{r(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{K_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)}{{\mathsf{q}}^{\upsilon(z+1)}}

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{\widetilde{K}_{(1),{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)}{{\mathsf{q}}^{r(2z+1)}}+\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{2\upsilon},(1)}(u)}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon(2z+1)}}+\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{2\upsilon+1},(1)}(u)}{{\mathsf{q}}^{(2\upsilon+1)(2z+1)}}+\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{{\mathsf{q}}}^{r(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{\widetilde{K}_{{{\mathsf{q}}}^{2\upsilon},{{\mathsf{q}}}^{r}}(u)}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon(z+1)}}.

We simplify each of the terms appearing in the final expression of
{\mathbb{D}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(z,u)
We proceed in order of appearance.

The first one, corresponding to
\upsilon=0,r>0
, is

\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{\widetilde{K}_{(1),{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)}{{\mathsf{q}}^{r(2z+1)}}

\displaystyle=\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{r(2z+1)}.}

Next we have the ones corresponding to
\upsilon>0
and
r=0
.
When
\upsilon
is odd we have

\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{2\upsilon+1},(1)}(u)}{{\mathsf{q}}^{(2\upsilon+1)(2z+1)}}=-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}}\sum_{\upsilon=0}^{\infty}\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon+1}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{(2\upsilon+1)(z+1)}}=-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}}\sum_{\upsilon=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{z(2\upsilon+1)}}.

On the other hand, when
\upsilon
is even we get

\displaystyle\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{2\upsilon},(1)}(u)}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon(2z+1)}}

\displaystyle=\left({\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon}-{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon-1}\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\right)\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon(z+1)}}}

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}}\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{p}}}\right)\right)\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon(z+1)}}

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}}\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\right)\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon z}}.

Finally, when both
\upsilon,r
are positive:

\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{r(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{2\upsilon},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon(z+1)}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{r(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon}-{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon-1}\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon(z+1)}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{r(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon}(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}})\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon(z+1)}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{r(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}})\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon z}}

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}}\right)\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{r(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon z}}.

So we consider the following sum

\displaystyle 1+\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}}\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon z}}-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}}\sum_{\upsilon=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{(2\upsilon+1)z}}+\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{zr(2z+1)}}

\displaystyle\ +\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}}\right)\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{r(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon z}}

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon z}}-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}}\sum_{\upsilon=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{(2\upsilon+1)z}}-\left[\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}}\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon z}}\right.

\displaystyle\ \left.+\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{r(2z+1)}}+\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}}\right)\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{r(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon z}}\right]

\displaystyle=1+\frac{1/{\mathsf{q}}^{2z}}{1-1/{\mathsf{q}}^{2z}}-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}}\cdot\frac{1/{\mathsf{q}}^{z}}{1-1/{\mathsf{q}}^{2z}}

\displaystyle=1+\frac{1/{\mathsf{q}}^{2z}}{1-1/{\mathsf{q}}^{2z}}-\frac{1/{\mathsf{q}}^{(z+1)}}{1-1/{\mathsf{q}}^{2z}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-1/{\mathsf{q}}^{z+1}}{1-1/{\mathsf{q}}^{2z}}.

To justify the second equality we show that the terms in the square brackets sum to zero.
Indeed,

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}}\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)

\displaystyle\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon z}}+\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{r(2z+1)}}+\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}}\right)\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{r(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon z}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}}\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon z}}+\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{r(2z+1)}}

\displaystyle+\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{r(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon z}}-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}}\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{r(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon z}}

\displaystyle=\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{r(2z+1)}}\left(1+\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon z}}\right)-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}}\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\left(1+\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{r(2z+1)}}\right)\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon z}}

\displaystyle=\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{r(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon z}}-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}}\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{r(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2\upsilon z}}

\displaystyle=\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\left(\frac{1/{\mathsf{q}}^{(2z+1)}}{1-1/{\mathsf{q}}^{(2z+1)}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{1-1/{\mathsf{q}}^{2z}}\right)-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}}\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{1-1/{\mathsf{q}}^{(2z+1)}}\right)\left(\frac{1/{\mathsf{q}}^{2z}}{1-1/{\mathsf{q}}^{2z}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{(1-1/{\mathsf{q}}^{(2z+1)})}\frac{1}{(1-1/{\mathsf{q}}^{2z})}\right)\left(\frac{\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)}{{\mathsf{q}}^{(2z+1)}}-\frac{\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2z+1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{(1-1/{\mathsf{q}}^{(2z+1)})}\frac{1}{(1-1/{\mathsf{q}}^{2z})}\right)\left(\frac{\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2z+1}}-\frac{\left(1+\binom{u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2z+1}}\right)

\displaystyle=0.\qed

A.2.2.
Case
{\mathfrak{q}}\nmid 2
and
{\mathfrak{q}}\mid\rho

Here, we have to further consider whether
k
is even or odd.
Recording the following lemma will be useful in proving the next case of Kloostermn type sums (and we can avoid repeating certain arguments).

Lemma A.11
.

Let
{\mathfrak{q}}\nmid 2
and fix an
odd
positive integer
k
.
Let
r>k
be any integer.
Then for any
a\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
with
\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(a)=k
, there are no solutions to the congruence

x^{2}\equiv a\pmod{{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}.

Proof.

Suppose
x
is a potential solution to

x^{2}\equiv a\pmod{{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}.

Since
r>k
we have
x^{2}\equiv 0\pmod{{\mathfrak{q}}^{k}}
.
This implies

2\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(x)\geq k.

Since
k
is odd this inequality is actually strict.
That is,

\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(x^{2})\geq k+1.

Since
r\geq k+1
, the congruence
x^{2}\equiv a\pmod{{\mathfrak{q}}^{k+1}}
holds.
Hence we conclude

a\equiv 0\pmod{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{k+1},

which contradicts
\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(a)=k
.
Thus no solution can exist.
∎

Lemma A.12
.

Let
{\mathfrak{q}}\nmid 2
and suppose that
{\mathfrak{q}}^{k}\|\rho^{k^{\prime}}
.
In other words,
{\mathfrak{q}}={\mathfrak{p}}
and
\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{q}})=\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{p}})={\mathsf{p}}
.
If
k=2k_{0}+1
is odd, then the values of
\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)
are as below:

(O-i)

Case
\upsilon=0,r=0
:

\widetilde{K}_{1,1}(u)=1
,

(O-ii)

Case
\upsilon>0
,
r=0
:
\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},1}(u)={\mathsf{p}}^{\upsilon}-{\mathsf{p}}^{\upsilon-1}
,

(O-iii)

Case
\upsilon=0,1\leq r\leq k_{0}
:

\widetilde{K}_{1,{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)={\mathsf{p}}^{r}
,

(O-iv)

Case
\upsilon=0,r\geq k_{0}+1
:

\widetilde{K}_{1,{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)=0
,

(O-v)

Case
\upsilon>0
,
1\leq r\leq k_{0}
:
\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)={\mathsf{p}}^{r+\upsilon}-{\mathsf{p}}^{r+\upsilon-1}
,

(O-vi)

Case
\upsilon>0
,
r\geq k_{0}+1
:
\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)=0
.

On the other hand, if
k=2k_{0}
is even and
\rho^{k^{\prime}}=\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{k}w
for some unit
w
, then

(E-i)

Case
\upsilon=0,r=0
:

\widetilde{K}_{1,1}(u)=1

(E-ii)

Case
\upsilon=0,1\leq r\leq k_{0}
:

\widetilde{K}_{1,{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)={\mathsf{p}}^{r}

(E-iii)

Case
\upsilon=0,r\geq k_{0}+1
:

\widetilde{K}_{1,{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)={\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}}\left(1+\binom{4uw}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)

(E-iv)

Case
\upsilon>0
,
0\leq r\leq k_{0}-1
:
\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},1}(u)={\mathsf{p}}^{r+\upsilon}-{\mathsf{p}}^{r+\upsilon-1}

(E-v)

Case
\upsilon>0
even,
r=k_{0}
:
\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},1}(u)={\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}+\upsilon}-{\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}+\upsilon-1}\left(1+\binom{4uw}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)

(E-vi)

Case
\upsilon>0
odd,
r=k_{0}
:
\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},1}(u)=-{\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}+\upsilon-1}

(E-vii)

Case
\upsilon>0
even ,
r\geq k_{0}+1
:
\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)=\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}}\right){\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}+\upsilon}\left(1+\binom{4uw}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)

(E-viii)

Case
\upsilon>0
odd,
r\geq k_{0}+1
:
\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)=0

Proof.

The proof is very similar to the one of Lemma
A.9
above.
We deal with each case separately.

Case
k
is odd:

Case
\upsilon=r=0
:
Just as before,

\widetilde{K}_{(1),(1)}(u)=1.

This settles
(O-i)
.

Case
\upsilon>0,r=0
:
The sum becomes

(33)

\begin{split}\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},(1)}(u)&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{0}\end{subarray}}\binom{\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{\upsilon}\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{0}\end{subarray}}\binom{\mu^{2}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{\upsilon}\\
&={\mathsf{p}}^{\upsilon}-{\mathsf{p}}^{\upsilon-1},\end{split}

where we used that
{\mathfrak{q}}\mid\rho
to deduce

\binom{\mu_{i}^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}=\binom{\mu_{i}^{2}}{{\mathfrak{q}}},

and the involved symbols are
1
only when
{\mathfrak{q}}\nmid\mu_{i}
, which does not occur exactly
{\mathsf{p}}^{\upsilon-1}
times, and otherwise are
0
.

Case
\upsilon=0,r>0
:
The sum becomes

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}_{(1),{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}\end{subarray}}\binom{(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\pmod{{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\pmod{{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}}\end{subarray}}1,

where we have used
(Fact 1)
above since
\upsilon=0
.

Since
k
is odd, the last computation breaks down into the cases
r\leq k_{0}
and
r>k_{0}
.
The first case reduces to counting modulo
{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}
the solutions to
\mu^{2}\equiv 0\pmod{{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}}
, which has
{\mathsf{p}}^{r}
solutions.
The latter case by Proposition
A.11
has no solutions.
Concretely, we get

\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},(1)}(u)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\text{if }r>k_{0},\\
{\mathsf{p}}^{r}&\text{if }r\leq k_{0}.\end{array}\right.

This settles
(O-iii)
and
(O-iv)
.

Case
\upsilon,r>0
:
The sum is

\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon+2r}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}\end{subarray}}\binom{(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{\upsilon}.

The is non-zero only if
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\pmod{{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}}
but
\mu^{2}\not\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\pmod{{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r+1}}
.
By Proposition
A.11
this congruence has no solution if
2r>k
, because
k
is odd.
In the other case we can split

(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r}=(\mu\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-r})^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r},

and each term is an integer, with the second one still multiple of
\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
(because
k
is odd!).
Hence, using the periodicity modulo
{\mathfrak{q}}
of the symbol,

\binom{(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}=\binom{(\mu\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-r})^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}=\binom{(\mu\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-r})^{2}}{{\mathfrak{q}}},

which is
1
or
0
according to whether
\val_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\mu)=r
or bigger.
That is, this sum counts the integers mod
{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r+\upsilon}
that are in
{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}
but not in
{\mathfrak{q}}^{r+1}
.
This has cardinality

{\mathsf{p}}^{r+\upsilon}-{\mathsf{p}}^{r+\upsilon-1}.

This concludes
(O-v)
and
(O-vi)
.

Case
k
is even:

Case
\upsilon=r=0
:
Exactly as before.

Case
\upsilon=0
,
r>0
:

The sum becomes

\widetilde{K}_{(1),{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}\end{subarray}}\binom{(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-2r}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{0}.

Using
(Fact 1)
and because
\upsilon=0
, the value it has is the number of solutions modulo
{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}
of the congruence
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\pmod{{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}}
.
When
2r\leq k
, this reduces to
\mu^{2}\equiv 0\pmod{{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}}
, which has
q^{r}
solutions.
On the other hand, if
2r>k
then our sought congruence implies
\mu^{2}\equiv 0\pmod{{\mathfrak{q}}^{2k_{0}}}
.
Hence
\mu=\pi^{k_{0}}\alpha
for some
\alpha
.
So the congruence
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\pmod{{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r}}
becomes

\alpha^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-k}\equiv 4uw\pmod{\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{2r-k}}.

This has
1+\binom{4uw}{{\mathfrak{q}}}
solutions modulo
{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r-k}
.
This corresponds to
{\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}}\left(1+\binom{4uw}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)
lifts modulo
{\mathfrak{q}}^{2r-k_{0}}
, any of which can be the value of
\alpha
.
We conclude

\widetilde{K}_{(1),{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathsf{p}}^{r}&\text{if }r\leq k_{0},\\
{\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}}\left(1+\binom{4uw}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)&\text{if }r\geq k_{0}+1.\end{array}\right.

This settles cases
(E-ii)
and
(E-iii)
.

Case
\upsilon\geq 0
,
0\leq r\leq k_{0}-1
:

This is solved exactly as case
(O-v)
.

Case
\upsilon>0
,
r=k_{0}
:
The sum becomes

\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},{\mathfrak{q}}^{k_{0}}}(u)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon+k}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{k}\end{subarray}}\binom{(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-k}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{\upsilon}=q^{\upsilon}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{k}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 0\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{k}\end{subarray}}\binom{(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-k}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{\upsilon},

where we have used the periodicity of the modified Hilbert symbol.

When
\upsilon
is even, we are counting the solutions to
\mu^{2}\equiv 0\pmod{{\mathfrak{q}}^{k}}
such that
\mu^{2}\not\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\pmod{{\mathfrak{q}}^{k+1}}
.
The first congruence has
{\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}}
solutions.
On the other hand, counting similarly to the case
(E-iii)
, the second congruence has

{\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}-1}\left(1+\binom{4uw}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right),

By subtracting, we deduce

\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},{\mathfrak{q}}^{k_{0}}}={\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}+\upsilon}-{\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}+\upsilon-1}\left(1+\binom{u}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right).

This settles case
(E-v)
.

When
\upsilon
is odd, we get

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{k}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 0\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{k}\end{subarray}}\binom{(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-k}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{\upsilon}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{k}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 0\mod{\mathfrak{q}}^{k}\end{subarray}}\binom{(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-k}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}=q^{k_{0}-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\mod{\mathfrak{q}}\end{subarray}}\binom{\alpha^{2}-4uw}{{\mathfrak{q}}},

where we have used that
\mu^{2}\equiv 0\pmod{k}
is equivalent to
\mu=\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{k_{0}}\alpha
where alpha can be any class modulo
{\mathfrak{q}}^{k_{0}}
.
These classes form groups of size
{\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}-1}
as lifts of the classes modulo
{\mathfrak{q}}
, which explains the factor
{\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}-1}
that comes out from the periodicity of the symbol.
This last sum is
-1
by Lemma
A.8
.
We conclude

\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},{\mathfrak{q}}^{k_{0}}}=-{\mathsf{p}}^{\upsilon+k_{0}-1},

This settles case
(E-vi)
.

Case
\upsilon>0
,
r\geq k_{0}+1
:
This is solved in exactly the same way as cases
(v)
and
(vi)
of Lemma
A.9
.
∎

Proposition A.13
.

Keep the notation introduced in Lemma
A.12
.
Then

{\mathbb{D}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(z,u)={\mathbb{D}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(z,u)=\frac{(1-{\mathsf{p}}^{z(k+1)})(1-1/{\mathsf{p}}^{z+1})}{(1-1/{\mathsf{p}}^{2z})(1-1/{\mathsf{p}}^{z})}

Proof.

We do each case separately by separating the series into the cases given by Proposition
A.12
.

Case
k
is odd
:

We write
k=2k_{0}+1
.
Then

\displaystyle{\mathbb{D}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(z,u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}^{r(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=0}^{\infty}\frac{\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)}{{\mathsf{p}}^{\upsilon(z+1)}}

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{\upsilon}-{\mathsf{p}}^{\upsilon-1}}{{\mathsf{p}}^{z(\upsilon+1)}}+\sum_{r=1}^{k_{0}}\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{r}}{{\mathsf{p}}^{r(2z+1)}}+\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\sum_{r=1}^{k_{0}}\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{r+\upsilon}-{\mathsf{p}}^{r+\upsilon-1}}{{\mathsf{p}}^{z(\upsilon+1)}{\mathsf{p}}^{r(2z+1)}}

\displaystyle=1+\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}^{z\upsilon}}+\sum_{r=1}^{k_{0}}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}^{2rz}}+\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}^{z\upsilon}}\sum_{r=1}^{k_{0}}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}^{2rz}}

\displaystyle=\left(\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}^{z\upsilon}}+1\right)\sum_{r=0}^{k_{0}}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}^{2rz}}

\displaystyle=\left(\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-z}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-z}}+1\right)\frac{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{2z(k_{0}+1)}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-(z+1)}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}\frac{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-z(k+1)}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-z}}.

Case
k
is even
:
Writing
k=2k_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{D}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(z,u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}^{r(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=0}^{\infty}\frac{\widetilde{K}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\upsilon},{\mathfrak{q}}^{r}}(u)}{{\mathsf{p}}^{\upsilon(z+1)}}

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{\upsilon}-{\mathsf{p}}^{\upsilon-1}}{{\mathsf{p}}^{\upsilon(z+1)}}+\sum_{r=1}^{k_{0}}\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{r}}{{\mathsf{p}}^{r(2z+1)}}+\sum_{r=k_{0}+1}^{\infty}\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}}\left(1+\binom{4uw}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)}{{\mathsf{p}}^{r(2z+1)}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}^{\upsilon(z+1)}}\sum_{r=1}^{k_{0}-1}\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{r+\upsilon}-{\mathsf{p}}^{r+\upsilon-1}}{{\mathsf{p}}^{r(2z+1)}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}+2\upsilon}-{\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}+2\upsilon-1}\left(1+\binom{4uw}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)}{{\mathsf{p}}^{(2\upsilon)(z+1)}}+\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=0}^{\infty}\frac{-{\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}+(2\upsilon+1)-1}}{{\mathsf{p}}^{(2\upsilon+1)(z+1)}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\sum_{r=k_{0}+1}^{\infty}\frac{\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}}\right){\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}+2\upsilon}\left(1+\binom{4uw}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)}{{\mathsf{p}}^{2\upsilon(z+1)}}.

We evaluate separately and then we substitute back into the previous sums.
The second term is

\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{\upsilon}-{\mathsf{p}}^{\upsilon-1}}{{\mathsf{p}}^{\upsilon(z+1)}}=\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-z}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-z}}.

The third and the fourth term together contribute

\sum_{r=1}^{k_{0}}\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{r}}{{\mathsf{p}}^{r(2z+1)}}+\sum_{r=k_{0}+1}^{\infty}\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}}\left(1+\binom{4uw}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)}{{\mathsf{p}}^{r(2z+1)}}={\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}\frac{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2zk_{0}}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}+{\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}}\left(1+\binom{4uw}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-(2z+1)(k_{0}+1)}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-(2z+1)}}.

The next term in the sum becomes

\displaystyle\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}^{\upsilon(z+1)}}\sum_{r=1}^{k_{0}-1}\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{r+\upsilon}-{\mathsf{p}}^{r+\upsilon-1}}{{\mathsf{p}}^{r(2z+1)}}

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\sum_{r=1}^{k_{0}-1}\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{\upsilon+r}}{{\mathsf{p}}^{\upsilon(z+1)}{{\mathsf{p}}^{r(2z+1)}}}

\displaystyle={\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-z}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-z}}\frac{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z(k_{0}-1)}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}},

since the
r
and
\upsilon
sums are independent.
The sixth term in the sum is

\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}+2\upsilon}-{\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}+2\upsilon-1}\left(1+\binom{4uw}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)}{{\mathsf{p}}^{(2\upsilon)(z+1)}}={\mathsf{p}}^{-2k_{0}z}\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}-\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}-1}}{{\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}(2z+1)}}\left(1+\binom{4uw}{{\mathfrak{p}}}\right)\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}},

while the seventh is

\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=0}^{\infty}\frac{-{\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}+(2\upsilon+1)-1}}{{\mathsf{p}}^{(2\upsilon+1)(z+1)}}=-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2k_{0}z-1}\sum_{\upsilon=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}^{(2\upsilon+1)z}}=-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2k_{0}z}\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-(z+1)}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}.

Finally the last term is

\displaystyle\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\sum_{r=k_{0}+1}^{\infty}\frac{\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}}\right){\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}+2\upsilon}\left(1+\binom{4uw}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)}{{\mathsf{p}}^{2\upsilon(z+1)}{\mathsf{p}}^{r(2z+1)}}

\displaystyle={\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}}\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\left(1+\binom{4uw}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\sum_{r=k_{0}+1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}^{2\upsilon z}{\mathsf{p}}^{r(2z+1)}}

\displaystyle={\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}}\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\left(1+\binom{4uw}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-(k_{0}+1)(2z+1)}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-(2z+1)}}.

By collecting the terms that share the factor
\left(1+\binom{4uw}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)
we obtain from them

\displaystyle\;{\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}}\left(1+\binom{4uw}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\left(\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-(2z+1)(k_{0}+1)}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-(2z+1)}}-\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-(2z+1)(k_{0}+1)}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}+\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-(k_{0}+1)(2z+1)}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-(2z+1)}}\right)

\displaystyle={\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}}\left(1+\binom{4uw}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\left({\mathsf{p}}^{-(2z+1)(k_{0}+1)}\left(\frac{1}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-(2z+1)}}-\frac{1}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}\right)+\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-(k_{0}+1)(2z+1)}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-(2z+1)}}\right)

\displaystyle={\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}}\left(1+\binom{4uw}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\left({\mathsf{p}}^{-(2z+1)(k_{0}+1)}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-(2z+1)}-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}{(1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z})(1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-(2z+1)})}\right)+\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-(k_{0}+1)(2z+1)}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-(2z+1)}}\right)

\displaystyle={\mathsf{p}}^{k_{0}}\left(1+\binom{4uw}{{\mathfrak{q}}}\right)\left(-\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-(k_{0}+1)(2z+1)}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-(2z+1)}}+\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-(k_{0}+1)(2z+1)}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-(2z+1)}}\right)

\displaystyle=0.

Plugging the terms in order the terms that remain are

\displaystyle{\mathbb{D}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(z,u)

\displaystyle=1+\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-z}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-z}}+{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}\frac{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2zk_{0}}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}

\displaystyle+{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-z}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-z}}\frac{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z(k_{0}-1)}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}

\displaystyle+{\mathsf{p}}^{-2k_{0}z}\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2k_{0}z}\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-(z+1)}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}.

Notice that

\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-z}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-z}}+{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-z}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-z}}\frac{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z(k_{0}-1)}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}=\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-z}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-z}}\frac{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2k_{0}z}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}},

and so we get finally

\displaystyle{\mathbb{D}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(z,u)

\displaystyle=1+{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}\frac{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2zk_{0}}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}+\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-z}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-z}}\frac{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2k_{0}z}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}+{\mathsf{p}}^{-2k_{0}z}\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2k_{0}z}\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-(z+1)}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2k_{0}z-(z+1)}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}+\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\frac{{\mathsf{p}}^{-z}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-z}}\frac{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2k_{0}z}}{1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z})(1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-z})}\left((1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2k_{0}z-(z+1)})(1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-z})+\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathsf{p}}}\right){\mathsf{p}}^{-z}(1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2k_{0}z})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-(z+1)})(1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-z(2k_{0}+1)})}{(1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z})(1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-z})}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-(z+1)})(1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-z(k+1)})}{(1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-2z})(1-{\mathsf{p}}^{-z})}.

∎

A.3.
The primes above
2

Assumption
(Hyp-split)
that
2
is totally split in
K
means that the computations are the same ones as the ones for
{\mathbb{Z}}
with the Kronecker Symbol.
Unfortunately, these computations were neither written in
[
4
]
nor in
[
3
]
.
We include the computations for the sake of completeness.
For ease of notation, we write
2
instead of
{\mathfrak{q}}
and
\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
.

In this case, the Kloosterman-type sums look like

\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},2^{r}}(u)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod 2^{2+\upsilon+2r}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod 2^{2r}\\
\frac{\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{2^{2r}}\equiv 0,1\pmod{4}\end{subarray}}\binom{\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}},2^{r}}{2^{\upsilon}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod 2^{2+\upsilon+2r}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod 2^{2r}\\
\frac{\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{2^{2r}}\equiv 0,1\pmod{4}\end{subarray}}\binom{(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})2^{-2r}}{2}_{K}^{\upsilon}.

Notation
.
