For the remainder of this section we drop the ‘K’ from the notation of the symbol as we always work with the Kronecker symbol and there is no chance of confusion.

The steps to follow are similar in essence to what we did before.
The main differences are the following

(a)

the congruence conditions.

(b)

the Kronecker Symbol is not the quadratic character.

These complicate the computations significantly.

Remark A.14
.

Set
c\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}/8{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
to denote the reduction map modulo
8
.
By Hensel’s Lemma we know that for a unit
w
the quantity
c(w)=1
if and only if
w
is a square in
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
.
The main complication with the following computations is that the Kronecker Symbol does not detect this: it assigns
1
also to the class
7
modulo
8
, even though
7
is not a square in
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
.

A.3.1.
Case
2\nmid\rho

We begin with a series of lemmas that will make arguments clearer later.
The purpose of the following lemma is to understand when do have solutions to the system of congruence conditions

(34)

\begin{split}\mu^{2}&\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\pmod{4^{r}},\\
\frac{\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{2^{2r}}&\equiv 0,1\pmod{4}.\end{split}

Lemma A.15
.

Let
({\mathfrak{p}},k)
be the tuple fixed in §
3.1
such that the prime ideal and corresponding positive integer satisfy
(Hyp-div)
.
Set
{k^{\prime}}=k/h_{\mathfrak{p}}
and denote by

the generator of
{\mathfrak{p}}^{h_{\mathfrak{p}}}
.
Suppose that
2\nmid\rho
(equivalently
{\mathfrak{p}}\neq 2
).
Then

When

, the solutions to the system of congruence conditions (
34
) can be read off from the table below
7
7

7

We explain how to read the first line of the table.
Note that
\mu^{2}\equiv 4\equiv 0\pmod{4}
has a solution precisely when
\mu\equiv 0,2\pmod{4}
.
Suppose that
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\equiv 1\pmod{8}
.
If
\mu\equiv 2\pmod{4}
then
\frac{\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{4}\equiv 0\pmod{4}
; whereas
\mu\equiv 0\pmod{4}
gives
\frac{\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{4}\equiv-1\pmod{4}
.
:

\frac{\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{4}\pmod{4}

and

the solutions to the system (

) can be read off from the table:

If
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\equiv 3,7\pmod{8}
and

, then there are no solutions modulo

to the system (

).

(iv)

If
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\equiv 3,5,7\pmod{8}

, then there are no solutions modulo

to the system (

).

Proof.

(i)

is straightforward counting, so we omit the details.

(ii)

is also straightforward counting.
We mention that we have to consider both lifts, since each case of
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})
produces potential solutions representatives modulo
16
which have to be checked.

(iii)

when
r\geq 2
, the existence of a solution to the system (either of
(iii)
or
(iv)
) implies that
\mu=2\beta
for some
\beta\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
.
Hence, canceling a
4
we obtain that

\beta^{2}\equiv u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\pmod{4^{r-1}}.

If
r\geq 2
, then
u\rho^{k^{\prime}}
is an odd square modulo
4
, i.e.
1
.
Therefore, modulo
8
it must be
1
or
5
.

(iv)

if
r\geq 3
, then we can conclude

\beta^{2}\equiv u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\pmod{8}.

which implies
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=1
.

∎

Lemma
A.15
shows that when
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\not\equiv 1\pmod{8}
, the Kloosterman-type sum will have most of its term equal to
0
because the congruence conditions are mostly not satisfied.
For
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\equiv 1\pmod{8}
the situation is different.

Lemma A.16
.

Keep the notation introduced in Lemma
A.15
.
Let
r\geq 3
and
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\equiv 1\pmod{8}
with
u\rho^{k^{\prime}}=\beta^{2}
for some
\beta\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{*}
.
Then the solutions to

(35)

\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\pmod{4^{r}},

are of the form
2\beta+4\delta
where
\delta
is a lift of
0
or
-\beta
modulo
4^{r-1}
.
Of these, only
four
satisfy the other congruence

(36)

\frac{\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{4^{r}}\equiv 0,1\pmod{4}.

Among these solutions,
two
give
0
modulo
4
while the other
two
give
1
modulo
4
.

Proof.

Notice that
2\beta
satisfies the congruence system.
Another potential solution is
2\beta+\gamma
.
The first congruence (
35
) can be rewritten as

\gamma(4\beta+\gamma)\equiv 0\pmod{4^{r}}.

This implies that
2\mid\gamma
; say
\gamma=2\alpha
.
Then, the above congruence becomes

\alpha(2\beta+\alpha)\equiv 0\pmod{4^{r-1}}.

This implies that
2
divides
\alpha
(we are using that
r-1\geq 1)
; say
\alpha=2\delta
.
Then

\delta(\beta+\delta)\equiv 0\pmod{4^{r-2}}.

Since
\beta
is relatively prime to
2
,

\delta\equiv 0,-\beta\pmod{4^{r-2}}.

These classes together produce
eight
lifts modulo
4^{r-1}
.
Since
\gamma=4\delta
is a class modulo
4^{r}
, these are all the possibilities.

We now check when the second congruence condition (
36
) is satisfied.
The lifts modulo
4^{r-1}
of
0\pmod{4^{r-2}}
are
0,4^{r-2},2\cdot 4^{r-2},3\cdot 4^{r-2}
.
Hence

\frac{(2\beta+k4^{r-1})^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{4^{r}}=\frac{k\beta 4^{r}+k^{2}4^{2r-2}}{4^{r}}=k\beta+k^{2}4^{r-2}\equiv k\beta\pmod{4}.

Hence only when
k=0
or
k=\beta^{-1}\pmod{4}
we get
0
or
1
.
Similarly, the lifts of
-\beta\pmod{4^{r-2}}
are
-\beta+k4^{r-2}
with
k=0,1,2,3
.
The congruence condition is

\frac{(2\beta-4\beta+k4^{r-1})^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{4^{r}}=\frac{(-2\beta+k4^{r-1})^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{4^{r}}=-\beta k+k^{2}4^{r-2}\equiv-\beta k\pmod{4}.

Once more, the ones that satisfy it are when
k=0
or when
k=-\beta^{-1}
.
Notice that for the choices
k=0
, we get the congruence
0
and there are
two
of them.
This concludes the proof.
∎

With all the previous results at hand, we now proceed to the computations of
\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},2^{r}}(u)
.

Lemma A.17
.

Keep the notation introduced in Lemma
A.15
.
Let
\upsilon,r
be any non-negative integers.
Then the values of
\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},2^{r}}
are given as follows:

(i)

Case
\upsilon=0,r=0
:

\widetilde{K}_{1,1}(u)=4

(ii)

Case
\upsilon=0,r=1
:

\widetilde{K}_{1,2}(u)=4

(iii)

Case
\upsilon=0,r=2
:

\widetilde{K}_{1,4}(u)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}8&\mbox{if }c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=1,5\\
0&\mbox{if }c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=3,7\end{array}\right.

(iv)

Case
\upsilon=0,r\geq 3
:

\widetilde{K}_{1,2^{r}}(u)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}16&\mbox{if }c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=1\\
0&\mbox{if }c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=3,5,7\end{array}\right.

(v)

Case
\upsilon>0
even ,
r=0
:
\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},1}(u)=2^{\upsilon+1}

(vi)

Case
\upsilon>0
odd ,
r=0
:
\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},1}(u)=-2^{\upsilon+1}

(vii)

Case
\upsilon>0
even ,
r=1
:
\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},2}(u)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\mbox{if }c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=1,5\\
2^{\upsilon+2}&\mbox{if }c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=3,7\end{array}\right.

(viii)

Case
\upsilon>0
even ,
r=2
:
\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},4}(u)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}2^{\upsilon+3}&\mbox{if }c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=5\\
0&\mbox{if }c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=1,3,7\end{array}\right.

(ix)

Case
\upsilon>0
even ,
r\geq 3
:
\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},2^{r}}(u)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}2^{\upsilon+3}&\mbox{if }c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=1\\
0&\mbox{if }c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=3,5,7\end{array}\right.

(x)

Case
\upsilon>0
odd ,
r=1
:
\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},2}(u)=0

(xi)

Case
\upsilon>0
odd ,
r=2
:
\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},4}(u)=0

(xii)

Case
\upsilon>0
odd ,
r\geq 3
:
\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},2^{r}}(u)=0

Proof.

First, we explain the counting strategy.
Lemmas
A.15
and
A.16
give the number of solutions to the system (
34
) for the different values of
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})
.
On the other hand, the Kloosterman-type sums we are evaluating consider the solutions to this system modulo
2^{2+\upsilon+2r}
.
The system itself remains invariant, but the value of the symbol might not (since it can exchange
1
and
5
).

Note that if
x
is a solution to the system modulo
4^{r}
, then
x+4^{r}
is another a lift modulo
4^{r+1}
.
We get

\frac{(x+4^{r})^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{4^{r}}=\frac{x^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{4^{r}}+2x+4^{r}.

Recall that all solutions to the system are even.
Hence, this equation implies the following: if the quotient is
0
, then its lifts are also
0
modulo
4
(since all lifts are obtained by adding
4^{r}
consecutively).
Hence, they can only count when
\upsilon=0
as otherwise the symbol has value
0
.

If
\frac{x^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{4^{r}}+2x+4^{r}\equiv 1\pmod{4}
, the term
2x+4^{r}
can swap between the two lifts
1,5\pmod{8}
.
Using Lemma
A.15
, we see that (for quotient having value
1
) when
r=1
,
2x
is a multiple of
4
and so the change is
x
to
x+4
, so a swap of sign happens in the symbol.
Similarly, for
r\geq 2
, the change is controlled by
2x
and we see this is an odd multiple of
4
every time.
Once more, a swap of sign happens in the symbol.

The conclusion is: when
\upsilon\geq 2
is
even
, all lifts of the solutions with quotient
1
contribute a
1
(to the counting) and it is enough to count the number of lifts.
On the other hand, if
\upsilon\geq 1
is
odd
then all lifts cancel in pairs, except for
r=0
.
In this final case, the division is vacuous.
This proves
(x)
,
(xi)
and
(xii)
having value
0
.

We do each case separately.

Case
\upsilon=r=0
:

In this case the congruence conditions are vacuous.
We just had a term, contributing
1
to the sum, per class
\mu\pmod{4}
.
Hence
\widetilde{K}_{1,1}(u)=4
.
This settles
(i)
.

Case
\upsilon=0,r=1
:
In this case

\widetilde{K}_{(1),2}(u)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod 2^{4}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod 2^{2}\\
\frac{\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{2^{2}}\equiv 0,1\pmod{4}\end{subarray}}\binom{(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})2^{-2}}{2}^{0}=4\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod 2^{2}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod 2^{2}\\
\frac{\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{2^{2}}\equiv 0,1\pmod{4}\end{subarray}}\binom{(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})2^{-2}}{2}^{0},

where we have used the periodicity of the symbol for the last line.
Since
\upsilon=0
we have that we have one term per solution to the congruence solutions.
The table in Proposition
A.15
(i)
shows there is in each case a single solution.
This settles (2).

Case
\upsilon=0,r=2
:

Since
\upsilon=0
, we must count the corresponding number of solutions of the given system.
By Proposition
A.15
(ii)
and
(iii)
, the result follows.

Case
\upsilon=0,r\geq 3
:
We count one more time the number of solutions modulo
4^{r}
of the system

\displaystyle\mu^{2}

\displaystyle\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\pmod{4^{r}},

\displaystyle\frac{\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{4^{r}}

\displaystyle\equiv 0,1\pmod{4}.

By Proposition
A.15
we get that for
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\neq 1
there are no solutions.
On the other hand, if
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=1
then there are
4
solutions.
The results follows.

Case
\upsilon>0,r=0
:
In this case

\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},(1)}(u)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod 2^{2+v}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod 2^{0}\\
\frac{\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{2^{0}}\equiv 0,1\pmod{4}\end{subarray}}\binom{(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})2^{0}}{2}^{\upsilon}=2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod 2^{v+1}\end{subarray}}\binom{\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{2}^{\upsilon}.

Notice we have used in the last line that the congruences conditions are vacuous, since all squares are
0,1
modulo
4
.
The expression

\binom{(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})2^{0}}{2}

is periodic modulo
4
, and modulo
2^{\upsilon+1}
there are
2^{\upsilon-1}
lifts of each class modulo
4
.
Hence

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},(1)}(u)

\displaystyle=2^{\upsilon}\left(\binom{0^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{2}^{\upsilon}+\binom{1^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{2}^{\upsilon}+\binom{2^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{2}^{\upsilon}+\binom{3^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{2}^{\upsilon}\right)

\displaystyle=2^{\upsilon}\left(\binom{-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{2}^{\upsilon}+\binom{1-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{2}^{\upsilon}+\binom{4-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{2}^{\upsilon}+\binom{1-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{2}^{\upsilon}\right)

\displaystyle=2^{\upsilon}\left(\binom{1-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{2}^{\upsilon}+\binom{1-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{2}^{\upsilon}\right),

\displaystyle=2^{v+1}\binom{1-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{2}^{\upsilon},

where we have used in the last equality that the symbol is
0
because
-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}
and
4-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}
are multiples of
2
.
If
\upsilon
is even, the symbol equals
1
and we obtain statement
(v)
.

On the other hand, if
\upsilon
is odd then

\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},(1)}(u)=2^{v+1}\binom{1-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{2}.

Notice that
4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\equiv 4\pmod{8}
, since
u\rho^{k^{\prime}}
is not a multiple of
2
.
Hence,

\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},(1)}(u)=2^{v+1}\binom{1-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{2}=2^{\upsilon+1}\binom{-3}{2}=-2^{\upsilon+1},

which is statement
(vi)
.

Case
\upsilon>0
even ,
r=1
:
In this case

\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},2}(u)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod 2^{2+\upsilon+2}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod 2^{2}\\
\frac{\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{2^{2}}\equiv 0,1\pmod{4}\end{subarray}}\binom{(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})2^{-2}}{2}^{\upsilon}=4\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod 2^{\upsilon+2}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}\mod 2^{2}\\
\frac{\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{2^{2}}\equiv 0,1\pmod{4}\end{subarray}}\binom{(\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}})2^{-2}}{2}^{\upsilon}.

This time, since
\upsilon
is even and positive, we only get a contribution of
1
from the solutions with

\frac{\mu^{2}-4u\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{4}\equiv 1\pmod{4}

We obtain the result using the table of Proposition
A.15
(i)
.

Case
\upsilon>0
even ,
r=2
:
Result follows from Proposition
A.15
(ii)
and
(iii)
.

Case
\upsilon>0
even ,
r\geq 3
:
When
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})\neq 1
, the sum has no terms by Proposition
A.15
(iv)
.
If
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=1
, then Proposition
A.16
proves there are
two
solutions.
This settles
(ix)
.

Case
\upsilon>0
odd ,
r=1
:

We obtain the result using the tables in Propositions
A.15
and
A.16
.
In each case, we see that there are as many terms whose quotients are
1
modulo
8
as cases where it it
5
modulo
8
.
Hence, their contribution cancels.

Case
\upsilon>0
odd ,
r\geq 2
:
Proposition
A.15
implies there are no solutions and the claim follows
∎

Proposition A.18
.

We keep the notation introduced in Lemma
A.15
.
Then

{\mathbb{D}}_{2}(z,u)=4\frac{1-1/2^{z+1}}{1-1/2^{2z}}

Proof.

We separate into cases according to the value of
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})
.
Notice that the following sum is common to all the cases (corresponding to
(i)
,
(ii)
,
(v)
, and
(vi)
in Lemma
A.17
):

(37)

4+\frac{4}{2^{2z+1}}+\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{2^{2\upsilon+1}}{2^{2\upsilon(z+1)}}-\sum_{\upsilon=0}^{\infty}\frac{2^{2\upsilon+2}}{2^{(2\upsilon+1)(z+1)}}=4+\frac{4}{2^{2z+1}}+\frac{2^{-2z+1}-2^{-z+1}}{1-2^{-2z}}=\frac{4-2^{-4z+1}-2^{-z+1}}{1-2^{-2z}}.

We begin with
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=5
and then we compare all of them to this one.

Case
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=5
:
We have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{D}}_{2}(z,U)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{r(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=0}^{\infty}\frac{\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},2^{r}}(u)}{2^{v(z+1)}}

\displaystyle=4+\frac{4}{2^{2z+1}}+\frac{8}{2^{2(2z+1)}}+\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{2^{2\upsilon+1}}{2^{2\upsilon(z+1)}}-\sum_{\upsilon=0}^{\infty}\frac{2^{2\upsilon+2}}{2^{(2\upsilon+1)(z+1)}}+\frac{8}{2^{2(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{2^{2\upsilon}}{2^{2\upsilon(z+1)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4-2^{-4z+1}-2^{-z+1}}{1-2^{-2z}}+\frac{8}{2^{2(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=0}^{\infty}\frac{2^{2\upsilon}}{2^{2\upsilon(z+1)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4-2^{-4z+1}-2^{-z+1}}{1-2^{-2z}}+\frac{8}{2^{2(2z+1)}}\frac{1}{1-2^{-2z}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4-2^{-z+1}}{1-2^{-2z}}

\displaystyle=4\cdot\frac{1-2^{-z-1}}{1-2^{-2z}}

Case
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=1
:

Notice that
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=1
and
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=5
share almost all terms except for
(iv)
and
(ix)
, which appear when
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=1
but not when
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=5
.
Similarly,
(viii)
appears for
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=5
but not for
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=1
.

Since we already proved that the case
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=5
works, we only need to prove that the terms
(iv)
and
(ix)
sum to the same as
(viii)
.
We have

\displaystyle\sum_{r\geq 3}\frac{16}{2^{r(2z+1)}}+\sum_{r\geq 3}\frac{1}{2^{r(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{2^{2\upsilon+3}}{2^{2\upsilon(z+1)}}

\displaystyle=16\cdot\frac{2^{-6z-3}}{1-2^{-2z-1}}+8\cdot\frac{2^{-6z-3}}{1-2^{-2z-1}}\cdot\frac{2^{-2z}}{1-2^{-2z}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{-6z+1}}{1-2^{-2z-1}}+\frac{2^{-6z}}{1-2^{-2z-1}}\cdot\frac{2^{-2z}}{1-2^{-2z}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{-6z+1}(1-2^{-2z})+2^{-8z}}{(1-2^{-2z-1})(1-2^{-2z})}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{-6z+1}-2^{-8z+1}+2^{-8z}}{(1-2^{-2z-1})(1-2^{-2z})}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{-6z+1}-2^{-8z}}{(1-2^{-2z-1})(1-2^{-2z})}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{-6z+1}}{1-2^{-2z}}.

On the other hand, sum
(v)
is

\frac{1}{2^{2(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{2^{2\upsilon+3}}{2^{2\upsilon(z+1)}}=\frac{8}{2^{2(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{2\upsilon z}}=\frac{8}{2^{2(2z+1)}}\frac{2^{-2z}}{1-2^{-2z}}=\frac{2^{-6z+1}}{1-2^{-2z}}.

This proves that the assertion also holds for the case
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=1
.

Case
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=3,7
:

Notice that all the values of the Kloosterman sums coincide for
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=3,7
.
In either case, the sum has one additional term compared to the expression (
37
), namely

\frac{1}{2^{2z+1}}\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{2^{2\upsilon+2}}{2^{2\upsilon(z+1)}}=\frac{8}{2^{2(2z+1)}}+\frac{8}{2^{2(2z+1)}}\sum_{\upsilon=1}^{\infty}\frac{2^{2\upsilon}}{2^{2\upsilon(z+1)}}.

The result follows from the case
c(u\rho^{k^{\prime}})=5
.
∎

A.3.2.
Case
2\mid\rho
and
k
odd.

In order to complete this case we need some lemmas.
As before, we write
k=2k_{0}+1
.

Lemma A.19
.

For
r>k_{0}+1
,
\mu
an integer, and
u
a unit, the quantity
\frac{x^{2}-2^{2+k}u}{2^{2r}}
is never an integer.
Equivalently,

2^{2r}\nmid x^{2}-2^{2+k}u.

Proof.

By assumption, we have that
2r>2+2k_{0}
.
It is clear that
2r>2+2k_{0}+1=2+k
.
In other words,
2r\geq 3+k
.
We have that
2^{2+k}\mid 2^{2r}
or in fact,
2^{3+k}\mid 2^{2r}
.

If we are to assume that
2^{2r}\mid\mu^{2}-2^{2+k}u
, then the above observation forces that

2^{2+k}\mid 2^{2r}\mid\mu^{2}-2^{2+k}u.

Hence,
2^{2+k}\mid\mu^{2}
.
But, comparing powers (and parity]), we actually have that

2^{3+k}\mid x^{2}.

This, together with the above divisibility, implies

2^{3+k}\mid 2^{2+k}.

which is impossible.
This contradicts our assumption.
∎

We now have the values of the Kloosterman-type sums:

Lemma A.20
.

Let
{\mathfrak{q}}=2
and suppose that
2^{k}\|\rho^{k^{\prime}}
.
In other words,
{\mathfrak{q}}={\mathfrak{p}}=2
and in our previous notation
\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{q}})=\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{p}})=2
.
If
k=2k_{0}+1
is odd, then the values of
\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},2^{r}}(u)
are as given below:

(O-i)

Case
\upsilon=0,r=0
:

\widetilde{K}_{1,1}(u)=4

(O-ii)

Case
\upsilon>0,r=0
:

\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},1}(u)=2^{v+2}-2^{v+1}

(O-iii)

Case
\upsilon=0
,
r\leq k_{0}
:
\widetilde{K}_{1,2^{r}}(u)=2^{2+r}

(O-iv)

Case
\upsilon=0
,
r=1+k_{0}
:
\widetilde{K}_{1,2^{r}}(u)=0

(O-v)

Case
\upsilon=0
,
r>1+k_{0}
:
\widetilde{K}_{1,2^{r}}(u)=0

(O-vi)

Case
\upsilon>0
even ,
r\leq k_{0}
:
\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},2^{r}}(u)=2^{v-1}(2^{r+3}-2^{r+2})

(O-vii)

Case
\upsilon>0
even ,
r=k_{0}+1
:
\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},2^{r}}(u)=0

(O-viii)

Case
\upsilon>0
even ,
r>k_{0}+1
:
\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},2^{r}}(u)=0

(O-ix)

Case
\upsilon>0
odd ,
r\leq k_{0}
:
\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},2^{r}}(u)=2^{\upsilon+r+1}

(O-x)

Case
\upsilon>0
odd ,
r=k_{0}+1
:
\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},2^{r}}(u)=0

(O-xi)

Case
\upsilon>0
odd ,
r>k_{0}+1
:
\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},2^{r}}(u)=0

Proof.

We do each case separately.

Case
\upsilon=r=0
:

The calculations are the same as Lemma
A.17
(i)
.

Case
\upsilon>0,r=0
:
The congruence condition is vacuously satisfied in this case.
By the periodicity property of the symbol at the even prime (modulo
4
), we have

\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},1}=\sum_{\mu\mod{2^{\upsilon+2}}}\binom{\mu^{2}-2^{2+k}u}{2}^{v}=2^{\upsilon}\sum_{\mu\mod{4}}\binom{\mu^{2}-2^{2+k}u}{2}^{v}

Notice that for
k\geq 1
we always have

\mu^{2}-2^{2+k}u\equiv\mu^{2}\pmod{8}.

Hence,

\sum_{\mu\mod{4}}\binom{\mu^{2}-2^{2+k}u}{2}^{v}=\sum_{\mu\mod{4}}\binom{\mu^{2}}{2}^{v}=\sum_{\mu\mod{4}}\binom{\mu^{2}}{2}.

The final equality follows from the fact that the symbol only take the value
0
or
1
on squares.
To evaluate the Kloosterman sum, we only sum over the odd values of
\mu
(which contribute a 1 to the sum).
Since there are
2^{\upsilon+1}
-many even terms, we get that

\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},1}=2^{\upsilon+2}-2^{\upsilon+1}.

Case
\upsilon=0
,
r\leq k_{0}
:

The definition of the Kloosterman-type sum becomes

\widetilde{K}_{1,2^{r}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod{2^{2r+2}}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 2^{2+k}\pmod{2^{2r}}\\
(\mu^{2}-2^{2+k}u)2^{-2r}\equiv 0,1\mod{4}\end{subarray}}\binom{(\mu^{2}-2^{2+k}u)2^{-2r}}{2}^{0}.

More precisely, a term contributes a
1
to the sum if and only if it is a class
\mu\pmod{2^{2r+2}}
such that

\mu^{2}\equiv 2^{2+k}u\pmod{2^{2r}},

and that satisfies the congruence condition.
Otherwise it contributes a
0
.

Since
r\leq k_{0}
, we have
2r\leq 2k_{0}<2+k
, so that this last congruence is actually

\mu^{2}\equiv 2^{2+k}u\equiv 0\pmod{2^{2r}}.

Equivalently,
\mu\equiv 0\pmod{2^{r}}
.
There are
2^{r}
solutions to this congruence modulo
2^{2r}
, and each of them lifts to
four
solutions modulo
2^{2+2r}
.

Suppose that
\mu=2^{r}y
is such a solution.
Then

\frac{\mu^{2}-2^{2+k}u}{2^{2r}}=\frac{y^{2}\cdot 2^{2r}-2^{2+k}u}{2^{2r}}=y^{2}-2^{2+k-2r}u\equiv y^{2}\pmod{4}.

Where, we have used the fact that

2+k-2r=2+1+2(k_{0}-r)>2.

The congruence condition is therefore satisfied and we conclude

\widetilde{K}_{1,2^{r}}=2^{2+r}.

Case
\upsilon=0
,
r=1+k_{0}
:

In this situation

\widetilde{K}_{1,2^{1+k_{0}}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod{2^{2r+2}}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 2^{2+k}u\pmod{2^{2r}}\\
(\mu^{2}-2^{2+k}u)2^{-2r}\equiv 0,1\mod{4}\end{subarray}}\binom{(\mu^{2}-2^{2+k}u)2^{-2r}}{2}^{0}.

Here, we will show that the congruence conditions are never satisfied simultaneously.
Note that if we require
2^{2r}\mid\mu^{2}-2^{2+k}u
then
\mu=2^{r}x
, for some
x
.
Upon simplification and using the fact that
r=k_{0}+1
,

\frac{\mu^{2}-2^{2+k}u}{2^{2r}}=x^{2}-2u.

Observe that
u\equiv\pm 1\pmod{4}
and that

x^{2}\pm 2\not\equiv 0,1\pmod{4}.

This calculation concludes this case.

Case
\upsilon=0
,
r>1+k_{0}
:

Lemma
A.19
asserts that the congruence conditions can never be satisfied.
This is an empty sum and the Kloosterman sum is 0.

Case
\upsilon>0
even ,
r\leq k_{0}
:

Using the periodicity of the symbol, we get that

\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},2^{r}}=2^{\upsilon-1}\sum_{\mu\mod{2^{2r+3}}}\binom{(\mu^{2}-2^{2+k}u){2^{-2r}}}{2}^{v}.

The assumption on
r
guarantees that

2r<2r+1\leq 2k_{0}+1=k<2+k.

Therefore, the terms that contribute to the Kloosterman-type sum are those
\mu\pmod{2^{2r+3}}
such that

•

\mu^{2}\equiv 2^{2+k}u\equiv 0\pmod{2^{2r}}
,

•

\mu^{2}\not\equiv 2^{2+k}u\pmod{2^{2r+1}}
(equivalently,
\mu^{2}\not\equiv 0\pmod{2^{2r+1}}
)

•

they satisfy the congruence condition modulo
4
.

Now observe that modulo
2^{2r+1}
, there are
2^{r+1}
solutions to the first congruence; of those,
2^{r}
satisfy the second one.

Write
\mu=2^{r}y
for some solution
\mu
that satisfies the first two the congruence conditions.
Then

\frac{\mu^{2}-2^{2+k}u}{2^{2r}}=\frac{y^{2}\cdot 2^{2r}-2^{2+k}u}{2^{2r}}=y^{2}-2^{2+k-2r}u\equiv y^{2}\pmod{4},

where we have used the fact that

2+k-2r=3+2(k_{0}-r)>2.

The calculations show that the third congruence condition is always satisfied and

\widetilde{K}_{2^{\upsilon},2^{r}}=2^{\upsilon-1}(2^{r+3}-2^{r+2}).

Case
\upsilon>0
even ,
r=k_{0}+1
:

As in the case
(O-iv)
, the congruence conditions are not satisfied simultaneously and the sum is 0.

Case
\upsilon>0
even ,
r>k_{0}+1
:

We can once again use Lemma
A.19
to show that the sum is 0.

Case
\upsilon>0
odd ,
r\leq k_{0}
:

As in
(O-vi)
above, the condition
r\leq k_{0}
implies that
2r<2+k
.
The quotient
\frac{\mu^{2}-2^{2+k}u}{2^{2r}}
is an integer if and only if
2^{r}\mid\mu
.
Writing
\mu=2^{r}y
for some
y
and using the periodicity of the symbol, we can replace the sum over the
\mu
to that over the
y
.
We get

\displaystyle K_{2^{\upsilon},2^{r}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\mod{2^{\upsilon+2r+2}}\\
\mu^{2}\equiv 2^{2+k}u\pmod{2^{2r}}\\
(\mu^{2}-2^{2+k}u)2^{-2r}\equiv 0,1\mod{4}\end{subarray}}\binom{(\mu^{2}-2^{2+k}u)2^{-2r}}{2}^{v}.

\displaystyle=2^{v}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\mod{2^{r+2}}\\
y^{2}-2^{2+k-2r}u\equiv 0,1\mod{4}\end{subarray}}\binom{(2^{2r}y^{2}-2^{2+k}u)2^{-2r}}{2}.

The congruence condition

y^{2}-2^{2+k-2r}u\equiv 0,1\pmod{4}

holds because
2+k-2r\geq 2
.
Actually, more is true; because
r\leq k_{0}
, we have

2+k-2r=3+2(k_{0}-r)\geq 3.

Invoking the periodicity modulo
8
of the symbol and the fact that the congruences condition hold, the sum becomes:

\displaystyle K_{2^{\upsilon},2^{r}}

\displaystyle=2^{\upsilon+r}\sum_{y\mod{4}}\binom{y^{2}-2^{2+k-2r}u}{2}

\displaystyle=2^{\upsilon+r}\sum_{y\mod{4}}\binom{y^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle=2^{\upsilon+r+1}\quad\text{ since contribution comes from $y=\pm 1$ and the sum equals $2$}.

Case
\upsilon>0
odd ,
r=k_{0}+1
:
As in cases
(O-iv)
and
(O-vii)
above, the sum is 0.

Case
\upsilon>0
odd ,
r>k_{0}+1
:
For this case, we once again use Lemma
A.19
to show that the sum is 0.
∎

Proposition A.21
.

With notation introduced in Lemma
A.20
,

{\mathbb{D}}_{2}(z,u)=4\frac{(1-2^{z(k+1)})(1-1/2^{z+1})}{(1-1/2^{2z})(1-1/2^{z})}

Proof.

The sum
{\mathbb{D}}_{2}(z,U)
splits into several parts according to the values of
r
and
\upsilon
given by Lemma
A.20
.
Cases
(O-iv)
,
(O-v)
,
(O-vii)
,
(O-viii)
,
(O-x)
, and
(O-xi)
do not contribute to the sum.
On the other hand,

•

(O-i)
contributes
4
,

•

(O-ii)
contributes
\frac{2^{-z+1}}{1-2^{-z}}
,

•

(O-iii)
contributes
4\cdot\frac{2^{-2z}(1-2^{-2k_{0}z})}{1-2^{-2z}}
,

•

(O-vi)
contributes
2\cdot\frac{2^{-2z}(1-2^{-2k_{0}z})}{1-2^{-2z}}\cdot\frac{2^{-2z}}{1-2^{-2z}}
,

•

(O-ix)
contributes
2\cdot\frac{2^{-2z}(1-2^{-2k_{0}z})}{1-2^{-2z}}\cdot\frac{2^{-z}}{1-2^{-2z}}
.

The sum of all these terms gives

4\frac{(1-1/2^{z(k+1)})(1-1/2^{z+1})}{(1-1/2^{2z})(1-1/2^{z})}.

We leave the details to the reader.
∎

A.3.3.
Case
2\mid\rho
and
k
even.

In this section, we only record the main result.

Proposition A.22
.

Let
{\mathfrak{q}}=2
and suppose that
2^{k}\|\rho^{k^{\prime}}
with
k
even; i.e.
{\mathfrak{q}}={\mathfrak{p}}=2
and
\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{q}})=\Norm_{K}({\mathfrak{p}})=2
.
Then

{\mathbb{D}}_{2}(z,u)=4^{n}\frac{(1-2^{z(k+1)})(1-1/2^{z+1})}{(1-1/2^{2z})(1-1/2^{z})}.

Proof.

This can be proven in exactly the same way as before.
This proof is left as an exercise for the reader.
∎
